The scaling limit of random walk and the intrinsic metric on planar critical percolation Thanks: Department of Pure Mathematics and Mathematical Statistics, University of Cambridge Thanks: Faculty of Mathematics, University of Vienna
Irina Đanković, Maarten Markering, Jason Miller, and Yizheng Yuan

Abstract
. We consider critical site percolation ( p = p c = 1 / 2 p=p_{c}=1/2 ) on the triangular lattice 𝐓 \mathbf{T} in two dimensions. We show that the simple random walk on the clusters of open vertices converges in the scaling limit to a continuous diffusion which lives in the gasket of a conformal loop ensemble with parameter κ = 6 \kappa=6 ( CLE 6 \CLE_{6} ), the so-called CLE 6 \CLE_{6} Brownian motion . We also show that the intrinsic (i.e., chemical distance) metric converges in the scaling limit to the geodesic CLE 6 \CLE_{6} metric . As a consequence, we deduce the existence of the chemical distance exponent, the resistance exponent, and the spectral dimension of the critical percolation clusters. Moreover, we show that the exponents satisfy the Einstein relations.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Overview

The main focus of this paper is the site percolation model on the triangular lattice
\mathbf{T}
in two dimensions. Recall that it is defined by fixing a parameter
p\in[0,1]
and then declaring each vertex to be either open or closed independently with probability
p
. Given a percolation configuration, a
cluster
of open (resp. closed) vertices is a connected component in the subgraph of
\mathbf{T}
where two vertices are connected by an edge if they are adjacent in
\mathbf{T}
and they are both open (resp. closed). Recall that the
critical probability

p_{c}
is given by
\inf\{p\in[0,1]:\theta(p)>0\}
, where
\theta(p)=\mathbf{P}[0\longleftrightarrow\infty]
and the notation
0\longleftrightarrow\infty
means that the cluster of open vertices containing the origin is infinite, and for
\mathbf{T}
we have that
p_{c}=1/2
. In the case that
p>p_{c}
, there is almost surely a unique infinite cluster and it has been shown that its behavior is strongly Euclidean at large scales in several different senses while for
p=p_{c}
the large scale geometry is highly non-trivial and fractal. In particular, it was shown by Smirnov
[
79
]
and Camia–Newman
[
26
]
that the cluster interfaces converge in the scaling limit to the conformal loop ensemble with
\kappa=6
(
\CLE_{6}
)
[
26
,
76
]
, the loop variant of Schramm’s
\SLE_{6}
curves
[
74
]
.

In this paper, we will be concerned with establishing scaling limit results for:

(i)

the
simple random walk
and

(ii)

the
intrinsic
(i.e., chemical distance) metric

on the clusters of open sites for critical percolation on
\mathbf{T}
.

In the case of (
i
), we mean that we consider a cluster of open vertices and then study the random walk on that cluster which moves from a site to each of its neighbors at rate
1
(so the associated discrete time random walk moves from a site to each of its neighbors with equal probability). The study of this model was popularized by de Gennes in 1976
[
33
]
in which he referred to it as the “
Ant in the Labyrinth
”, the ant corresponding to the random walker and the labyrinth corresponding to the random environment described by the percolation configuration that the random walker lives in. As we will describe just below, this has been the inspiration for a large number of subsequent works. Our contribution will be to show that the simple random walk on large clusters converges in the scaling limit to the
\CLE_{6}
Brownian motion
, the canonical diffusion on a
\CLE_{6}
constructed in
[
4
,
66
]
. In order to prove this convergence result, we will use the framework of resistance metrics developed by Kigami
[
52
,
53
]
as well as the work of Croydon
[
31
]
which reduces the problem of process convergence to the problem of resistance metric convergence.

In the case of (
ii
), we will consider a cluster of open vertices and then study the graph metric where there is a unit length edge connecting any pair of adjacent open sites. We will show that the graph metric converges in the scaling limit to the
geodesic
\CLE_{6}
metric
, the canonical conformally covariant geodesic metric that one can associate with a
\CLE_{6}
constructed in
[
4
,
67
]
.

The CLE
6
in a simply connected domain
D\subsetneq\mathbf{C}
is a random countable collection of loops contained in
\overline{D}
, introduced in
[
26
,
76
]
. The CLE
6
loops intersect themselves and can intersect each other and the domain boundary, but they do not cross upon intersecting. Each of the loop segments looks locally like an SLE
6
curve
[
74
]
. The CLE
6
loops describe the scaling limit of the collection of cluster boundaries in critical percolation. The intersection points of the loops correspond to the so-called
4
-arm points
or
pivotal points
in the percolation. We note that in this paper we will consider the
nested CLE
6
in which each loop contains a further collection of loops on its inside. The
non-nested CLE
6
, in contrast, consists only of the outermost loops. The nested CLE
6
is obtained from the non-nested CLE
6
by repeatedly adding conditionally independent CLE
6
’s in each of the connected components inside the loops.

The
CLE
6
gaskets
describe the scaling limit of the clusters themselves. The gasket of a non-nested CLE
6
is the set of points that are connected to
\partial D
by a path not crossing any of the loops (i.e.,the set of points that do not lie inside the loops). In a nested CLE
6
, we can define for each loop
\gamma
the gasket inside
\gamma
as the set of points on or inside
\gamma
that are connected to
\gamma
by a path not crossing any of the loops inside
\gamma
(i.e., the gasket of the non-nested CLE
6
inside
\gamma
). The CLE
6
gaskets are fractal sets and their Hausdorff dimension (with respect to the Euclidean metric) is shown in
[
72
,
65
]
to be
91/48
.

Note that since the CLE
6
loops are self-intersecting, there will be distinct points on the percolation clusters (the points on the opposite arms of a pivotal) that converge to the same point on the Euclidean plane in the scaling limit. These points need to be distinguished in the CLE
6
metric space. Therefore we will view the CLE
6
gasket as an abstract metric space with an embedding in the plane where each point in the plane has one or two preimages in the gasket. See Section
1.3
below.

The main goal of this paper is to show the convergence of the simple random walk and the intrinsic metric, respectively, to the
\CLE_{6}
Brownian motion and the geodesic
\CLE_{6}
metric on all macroscopic clusters.

1.2.
Related work

Both the simple random walk and the intrinsic metric on percolation clusters have been the subject of a substantial amount of prior work which we now review. In each case, one can think of putting the existing results into one of three categories: supercritical percolation, critical percolation in two dimensions, and critical percolation in high dimensions. We will also mention results for related models.

1.2.1.
Ant in the labyrinth

We direct the reader to
[
57
]
for an overview of random walks on critical percolation and related models. Early investigations of the random walk on percolation include experiments by Last and Thouless
[
59
]
, who studied the conductivity of a sheet of graphite with randomly placed holes in it, and Brandt
[
24
]
, who numerically studied the diffusion of gases in glasses using random walk on percolation. Kesten was the first to rigorously study random walk on percolation. In 1986, he introduced the
infinite incipient cluster
(IIC) in two dimensions
[
51
]
. This is the random configuration which arises by starting off with critical percolation, conditioning on the event that the origin is connected to the ball of radius
n
by an open path, and then taking a limit as
n\to\infty
. In the same year, he showed that simple random walk on the IIC is subdiffusive
[
50
]
, meaning that there exists
\varepsilon>0
so that it takes at least
n^{2+\varepsilon}
steps for it to reach Euclidean distance
n
. This is much longer than the
n^{2}
steps it takes for random walk on the entire lattice. The reason for this phenomenon is that one can think of a large critical percolation cluster as consisting of a long backbone together with many smaller clusters hanging off it. Since the number of vertices in the backbone is negligible compared to the size of the entire cluster, the walk spends most of its time exploring the dead end clusters hanging off the backbone. Thus, the study of random walk on percolation and effective resistance can tell us a great deal about the structure of critical clusters. We refer to
[
69
]
for more background on the percolation backbone and its relation to the random walk. Recently, Ganguly and Lee
[
38
]
improved Kesten’s subdiffusivity result by showing that it holds with the intrinsic metric in place of the Euclidean metric.

A number of recent works have been aimed at the scaling limit of random walk on high-dimensional critical percolation clusters. For example, Ben Arous, Cabezas and Fribergh
[
17
]
obtained the scaling limit of random walk on the range of branching random walk in high dimensions and recently in the case of critical lattice trees
[
18
]
. These are both simpler models which are conjectured to be in the same universality class as random walk on high-dimensional critical percolation clusters.

The
Alexander-Orbach conjecture
states that the
spectral dimension
of the IIC for critical percolation is
4/3
. Recall that the spectral dimension of an infinite connected graph
G
is defined as

(1.1)

d_{s}=d_{s}(G)=-2\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{p_{2n}(x,x)}{\log n},

where
p_{n}
is the
n
-step transition kernel of simple random walk on
G
. When
d_{s}=4/3
, we have that
p_{2n}(x,x)\sim n^{-2/3+o(1)}
as
n\to\infty
. This conjecture was confirmed for critical percolation in high dimensions by Kozma and Nachmias
[
54
]
. It is expected to be false in dimensions
d<6
, however, numerical simulations suggest that it is remarkably close to being true
[
19
]
. In particular, the numerical estimate for planar critical percolation is roughly
1.318\pm 0.001
, which is not far from the conjectured value.

In the case of supercritical percolation, the clusters converge to the Euclidean space, and the annealed invariance principle towards an ordinary Brownian motion was proved in
[
34
]
. This was extended to a quenched invariance principle by Sidoravicius and Sznitman
[
77
]
for
d\geq 4
and independently by Berger and Biskup
[
20
]
and Mathieu and Piatnitski
[
63
]
for
d\geq 2
. Gaussian heat kernel estimates in this setting were proved by Barlow
[
15
]
. In the intervening years, Grimmett, Kesten, and Zhang
[
44
]
showed that random walk on supercritical percolation is recurrent in dimension
2
and transient in dimensions
d\geq 3
.

Another line of research from the last
40
years has focused on constructing random processes on deterministic fractals, with the aim of developing a better understanding of random walk on low-dimensional critical percolation clusters. Some of the early investigations were by physicists in the 1980s, see
[
2
,
41
]
. In 1987, the mathematicians Kusuoka
[
58
]
and Goldstein
[
42
]
constructed the scaling limit of the random walk on the
Sierpinski gasket
. Barlow and Perkins
[
13
]
called this process
Brownian motion on the Sierpinski gasket
and further studied its properties. This spurred a great deal of activity by e.g., Lindstrom
[
62
]
, Kigami
[
52
]
, and Barlow and Bass
[
13
]
, studying random walks on other deterministic fractals. We refer to Barlow’s St. Flour lecture notes from 1995
[
14
]
for an overview of the work on random walk on (deterministic) fractals.

Kigami developed a useful framework for constructing and studying stochastic processes on low-dimensional fractals, which connects these processes to resistance forms and resistance metrics. See
[
52
,
53
]
for an overview. It is precisely this framework that we rely on to obtain the scaling limit of random walk on critical percolation. In particular, we use a result by Croydon
[
31
]
to transfer the scaling limit of the resistance metric to that of the random walk.

1.2.2.
Intrinsic metric

The study of intrinsic (e.g., chemical) distances and scaling limits of large percolation clusters has a rich history. In the case of supercritical percolation, increasingly strong results proved by Gärtner and Molchanov, Antal and Pisztora, and Garet and Marchand
[
37
,
5
,
40
]
showed that the intrinsic metric behaves like the Euclidean metric in all dimensions. This implies that the scaling limit of a large supercritical percolation cluster is simply Euclidean space. These works complement the scaling limit results for random walk towards Brownian motion in this setting mentioned above
[
34
,
77
,
20
,
63
]
. For high-dimensional (
d>11
for nearest-neighbor and
d>6
for spread-out) critical percolation, it was shown using lace expansion by Hara and Slade
[
45
]
that the intrinsic distance between two points at Euclidean distance
n
is of order
n^{2}
, and the limiting distribution of the rescaled distance between two points was obtained recently in
[
27
]
. However, existence of the large cluster scaling limit is still an open problem. Blanc, Renaudie, Broutin and Nachmias
[
23
]
established the scaling limit of percolation on the hypercube. See also recent work by Chatterjee, Chinmay, Hanson and Sosoe
[
28
]
on the IIC in high dimensions. In low dimensions, the chemical distance is not well-understood, even by physicists. For planar critical percolation, it is conjectured (e.g., in Schramm’s 2006 ICM contribution
[
75
, Problem 3.3]
) that the intrinsic distance between two points at Euclidean distance
n
should also be of order
n^{\beta}
for some exponent
\beta
. In 1999, Aizenman and Burchard
[
1
]
showed that
\beta>1
. About twenty years later, Damron, Hanson and Sosoe
[
32
]
obtained an upper bound of
\beta<4/3
. Various simulations are consistent with
\beta\approx 1.131

[
43
,
46
,
84
]
. Further numerical work by Pose, Schrenk, Araujo and Hermann
[
71
]
compared the geodesics to
\SLE_{\kappa}
curves with
\kappa\approx 1.04
.

1.2.3.
Other models

Barlow, Croydon and Kumagai
[
12
]
obtained a (subsequential) scaling limit of random walk on the planar uniform spanning tree. In high dimensions, the analogous result was proved by Archer, Nachmias and Shalev
[
8
]
. On a tree, the resistance metric is the same as the geodesic metric, which makes it more straightforward to obtain the scaling limit of the random walk. See
[
9
,
10
,
6
,
7
,
11
,
31
,
55
]
for some examples of random walk scaling limits on other tree-like critical models.

A related model is
long-range
percolation, where every pair of sites has a positive probability of being connected, with the connection probability behaving like a power law in the distance between vertices. Berger–Tokushige and Crawford–Sly
[
21
,
29
]
established the scaling limit of random walk on long-range supercritical percolation in all dimensions. It was very recently shown by Ding, Fang and Huang
[
36
]
that the geodesic metric on critical long-range percolation has a scaling limit, improving on a subsequential convergence result by Bäumler
[
25
]
. We refer to the discussion in
[
36
]
for more background on long-range percolation and the intrinsic distance.

1.3.
Main results

We now give the statement of our main results. In order to be concrete, we take
\mathbf{T}
to be the triangular lattice centered at
0
which contains the horizontal axis and for each
n\in\mathbf{N}
we take
\mathbf{T}_{n}=\tfrac{1}{n}\mathbf{T}
as mentioned above. Throughout the paper, we will work in the
triangular coordinate system
where the vertical axis is the line intersecting the horizontal axis at
0
at an angle of
\frac{\pi}{3}
. In particular, the point with triangular coordinates
(1,1)
denotes the Euclidean point
(3/2,\sqrt{3}/2)
, and the notation
[0,1]^{2}
refers to the parallelogram enclosed by the triangular vertices
(0,0),(1,0),(0,1),(1,1)
, which correspond to the Euclidean vertices
(0,0),(1,0),(1/2,\sqrt{3}/2),(3/2,\sqrt{3}/2)
. Define
\Lambda_{n}
to be the
n\times n
subgraph of
\mathbf{T}_{n}
restricted to vertices in
[0,1)^{2}
. By a slight abuse of notation, we also write
\Lambda_{n}
to refer to the vertex set of
\Lambda_{n}
and, as is standard in the percolation literature, we refer to the vertices of
\Lambda_{n}
as
sites
.

Let
\Omega_{n}:=\{0,1\}^{\Lambda_{n}}
be the set of site percolation configurations on
\Lambda_{n}
with
\omega(x)=0
(resp.
\omega(x)=1
) denoting the site
x\in\Lambda_{n}
being closed (resp. open). Throughout, we consider
critical percolation
so that each site is open independently with probability
1/2
. We say two open sites
x,y\in\Lambda_{n}
are
connected
if there exists a nearest-neighbor path in
\Lambda_{n}
from
x
to
y
consisting only of open sites. This induces an equivalence relation on the set of open sites. The equivalence classes are called
open clusters
;
closed clusters
are defined analogously.

Let
\mathcal{X}^{(1)}_{n},\mathcal{X}^{(2)}_{n},\ldots
be the (open and closed) clusters of
\Lambda_{n}
induced by
\omega\in\Omega_{n}
, ordered descending in Euclidean diameter and breaking ties arbitrarily. We define the following metrics on
\Lambda_{n}
. For
x,y\in\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n}
, let
P_{n}(x,y)
be the set of paths in
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n}
between
x
and
y
. Let
\diam(\gamma)
be the Euclidean diameter of a path
\gamma\in P_{n}(x,y)
. Define the
path metric

d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}
as

(1.2)

d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}(x,y)=\inf_{\gamma\in P_{n}(x,y)}\diam(\gamma).

We use the path metric as an auxiliary reference metric to distinguish points on the clusters whose Euclidean distance converges to zero but are separated by a macroscopic closed path.

We recall the definition of the effective resistance metric of a graph
G=(V,E)
which is assumed to be simple, finite, and connected. For each
A,B\subset V

define the
effective resistance
between
A
and
B
to be

D^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{G}(A,B)=\bigl(\min\{\mathcal{E}(f,f)\mid f\colon V\to\mathbf{R},\,f|_{A}\equiv 1,\,f|_{B}\equiv 0\}\bigr)^{-1}

where

\mathcal{E}(f,f)=\sum_{\{u,v\}\in E}(f(u)-f(v))^{2}.

For
x,y\in V
, we write
D_{G}^{{\mathrm{res}}}(x,y):=D_{G}^{{\mathrm{res}}}(\{x\},\{y\})
. Then
D^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{G}
is a metric on
V
.

We let
D^{{\mathrm{geo}}}_{n}
be the
geodesic
or
graph
distance on all clusters
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n}
, where neighboring sites are considered to have distance 1. Finally, we let
D^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{n}
be the
resistance
metric on all
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n}
, where the conductances between neighboring sites are also set to 1. Let
({\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{{\mathrm{geo}}})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
and
({\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{{\mathrm{res}}})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}

be sequences of deterministic constants, to be specified later. Define the rescaled metrics

(1.3)

d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}_{n}:=({\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{{\mathrm{geo}}})^{-1}D^{{\mathrm{geo}}}_{n}\quad\text{and}\quad d^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{n}:=({\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{{\mathrm{res}}})^{-1}D^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{n}.

Lastly, we let

(1.4)

\mu^{(m)}_{n}:=\frac{1}{n^{2}A(1,n)}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n}}\delta_{x}

be the normalized counting measure on
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n}
, where
A(1,n)
is the
one-arm
probability as defined in Section
2.3
.

Let
\Gamma
be a (nested)
\CLE_{6}
in
(0,1)^{2}
. We define the set
\Lambda=\Lambda(\Gamma)
to be the set of
prime ends
1
1

1

Our notion of prime ends differs slightly from the classical notion of prime ends for domains
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
, however if
D
is a domain with locally connected boundary
\partial D
, then its prime ends in the classical sense correspond to the prime ends of
\partial D
in our notion.
of
\Gamma
, i.e.,
\Lambda
is the completion of the set of points in
(0,1)^{2}
that are not on a loop in
\Gamma
with respect to the metric
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}
defined below in (
1.5
). Let
\mathcal{X}^{(1)},\mathcal{X}^{(2)},\ldots\subset\Lambda
be the interior gaskets of
\Gamma
, ordered descending in Euclidean diameter, i.e. the connected components of
\Lambda
.

We also define the path metric on
\Lambda
as follows. For
x,y\in\Lambda
, let
P(x,y)
be the set of admissible paths in
\Lambda
between
x
and
y
, where we say a path is
admissible
if it is contained in
(0,1)^{2}
and does not cross any loops of
\Gamma
. Note that by our definition, if e.g.
\pi
is the natural map which projects a prime end to its corresponding point in Euclidean space and
\pi(x)=\pi(y)
is on a loop
\gamma
but
x
is a prime end inside of
\gamma
, and
y
is a prime end outside of
\gamma
, then no path between
x
and
y
is considered admissible. The topology of the clusters of
\Lambda
is encoded by the path metric
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}
defined by

(1.5)

d^{{\mathrm{path}}}(x,y)=\inf_{\gamma\in P(x,y)}\diam(\gamma).

Throughout the paper, we mostly consider
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n})
,
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n},d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}_{n})
, and
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n},d^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{n})
as abstract metric spaces, equipped with an embedding
\pi_{n}\colon\Lambda_{n}\to[0,1]^{2}
. Similarly, we consider
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)},d^{{\mathrm{path}}})
as an abstract metric space with an embedding
\pi\colon\Lambda\to[0,1]^{2}
. In the continuum, it follows from the results of
[
4
,
67
,
66
]
that we can define on
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}
a conformally covariant geodesic metric
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
(the geodesic
\CLE_{6}
metric) and a resistance metric
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
whose associated resistance form is conformally covariant (the
\CLE_{6}
resistance metric). Each of the metrics
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
and
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
is defined up to a global deterministic constant. We will review the basic properties of
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
and
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
in Section
2.5
. We also let
\mu^{(m)}
be (a multiple of) the canonical conformally covariant
\CLE_{6}
gasket measure on
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}
from
[
64
,
39
]
, whose properties we will recall in Section
2.4.2
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
,
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
, and
\mu
be suitable multiples of the geodesic CLE
6
metric, the CLE
6
resistance metric, and CLE
6
gasket measure, respectively. There exist sequences
({\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{{\mathrm{geo}}})_{n\in\mathbf{N}},({\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{{\mathrm{res}}})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
such that for all
m
,

(1.6)

\begin{split}(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n},\mu^{(m)}_{n},\pi_{n})&\rightarrow(\mathcal{X}^{(m)},d^{{\mathrm{path}}},\mu^{(m)},\pi),\\
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n},d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}_{n},\mu^{(m)}_{n},\pi_{n})&\rightarrow(\mathcal{X}^{(m)},d^{{\mathrm{geo}}},\mu^{(m)},\pi),\\
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n},d^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{n},\mu^{(m)}_{n},\pi_{n})&\rightarrow(\mathcal{X}^{(m)},d^{{\mathrm{res}}},\mu^{(m)},\pi)\\
\end{split}

weakly with respect to the Gromov-Hausdorff-Prokhorov-function topology as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

The definition of the normalizing constants
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
and
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{{\mathrm{res}}}
is given in Section
3.2.1
. As a result of this, we obtain existence of the scaling exponent for the intrinsic distance and effective resistance between the left and right sides of the parallelogram.

Corollary 1.2
.

Let
S_{n}
be the geodesic distance (resp. effective resistance) between the left and the right side of
\Lambda_{n}
which we denote by
\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n},\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n}
, respectively. Let
\beta\in\{\beta^{\mathrm{geo}},\beta^{\mathrm{res}}\}
be the scaling exponent of the CLE
6
geodesic (resp. resistance) metric defined in
[
67
,
66
]
. For each
q\in(0,1)
, we have

\mathbf{E}[S_{n}\mid\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n}]\asymp\operatorname{quant}_{q}[S_{n}\mid\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n}]\asymp{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}=n^{\beta+o(1)}\quad\text{as }n\to\infty,

where
\operatorname{quant}_{q}
denotes the
q
-quantile,
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}
denotes
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
(resp.
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{{\mathrm{res}}}
), and the multiplicative constants in
\asymp
depend on
q
.

Similarly, we obtain the existence of the exponents for the chemical distance and effective resistance on the IIC.

Corollary 1.3
.

Let
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{IIC}}
denote the law of the IIC on the entire lattice
\mathbf{T}
and let
S_{n}
be the geodesic distance (resp. effective resistance) between
0
and
\partial B(0,n)
. Let
\beta\in\{\beta^{\mathrm{geo}},\beta^{\mathrm{res}}\}
be the scaling exponent of the CLE
6
geodesic (resp. resistance) metric defined in
[
67
,
66
]
. Under
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{IIC}}
, almost surely,

S_{n}=n^{\beta+o(1)}\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

Moreover, for each
\varepsilon>0
and
\alpha>0
there exists
c(\varepsilon,\alpha)
such that

\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{IIC}}[S_{n}\notin[n^{\beta-\varepsilon},n^{\beta+\varepsilon}]]\leq cn^{-\alpha}.

In this paper,
B(0,n)
refers to the parallelogram of side length
2n
centered at
0
, but of course the statement is also true if
B(0,n)
is the Euclidean ball.

From the one-arm exponent for critical percolation established in
[
60
]
, it is known that the volume growth exponent equals
91/48
. By the results from
[
56
]
, this implies the following statements regarding (the existence of) the spectral dimension of the IIC.

Corollary 1.4
.

Let
\beta
be the scaling exponent of the CLE
6
resistance metric defined in
[
66
]
. Let
\mathrm{P}_{0}
denote the law of the simple random walk on the IIC starting at
0
, let
\mathrm{p}_{2t}(0,0)
denote its return probability after
2t
steps, and let
\tau_{n}
denote its hitting time of
\partial B(0,n)
. Under
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{IIC}}
, almost surely,

\displaystyle\mathrm{p}_{2t}(0,0)

\displaystyle=t^{-\frac{91/48}{91/48+\beta}+o(1)}\quad\text{as }t\to\infty,

\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{E}_{0}\tau_{n}

\displaystyle=n^{91/48+\beta+o(1)}\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

Note that Corollary
1.4
states that the Einstein relations hold for the IIC in planar critical percolation.

As shown in
[
31
]
, the Gromov-Hausdorff-Prokhorov convergence of a sequence of metric measure spaces where each metric is a resistance metric implies the convergence of the corresponding stochastic processes. Thus by combining Theorem
1.1
with
[
31
]
, we obtain the following convergence result for the simple random walk on
\mathcal{X}_{n}
.

Theorem 1.5
.

There exists a sequence
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
such that the following is true. Let
m\in\mathbf{N}
and sample
\rho_{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n}
and
\rho\in\mathcal{X}^{(m)}
such that
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n},\pi_{n},\rho_{n})\to(\mathcal{X}^{(m)},d^{{\mathrm{path}}},\pi,\rho)
in law in the rooted GHf topology. Then there exists a coupling such that the following holds almost surely. Let
X^{(n)}
be the continuous-time simple random walk on
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n}
that jumps to each open neighboring site at rate
b_{n}
, started from
\rho_{n}
. Then there exists a diffusion process
X
on
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}
with
X_{0}=\rho
such that
\pi_{n}(X^{(n)})
converges in law to
\pi(X)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
in the space of càdlàg processes on
[0,1]^{2}
equipped with the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.

We will see that the jump rate
b_{n}
can be taken to be
n^{2}A(1,n){\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{{\mathrm{res}}}=n^{91/96+\beta+o(n)}
. The limiting stochastic process
X
is the
\CLE_{6}
Brownian motion
as constructed in
[
4
,
66
]
. See Section
2.6
for a precise definition.

1.4.
Discussion

Let us now make some comments about our main theorem statements.

Choice of lattice

The theorems above are stated for critical site percolation on the triangular lattice, since this is the model for which the convergence towards
\SLE_{6}
/
\CLE_{6}
has been shown. However, the proofs of tightness in this paper only use certain arm exponents for percolation, which are also shown for the square lattice
\mathbf{Z}^{2}
. The
\SLE
/
\CLE
machinery is only used to prove the uniqueness of the subsequential limits. Therefore, our scaling limit result would apply to other lattices if the convergence to
\SLE_{6}
/
\CLE_{6}
were shown.

Random walk speed

In this paper, we show convergence of the random walk that jumps to each of its open neighbors at the same rate
b_{n}
. This is also known as the
variable-speed
random walk (VSRW), since the total jump rate at each site is proportional to the number of open neighbors. Another natural model is the
constant-speed
random walk (CSRW), where the total jump rate is constant at each site. The stationary measure of the VSRW is the uniform measure on
\mathcal{X}_{n}
, which is proportional to
\mu_{n}
. Or, in the language of resistance metrics: the VSRW is the unique
Hunt process
associated with
(\mathcal{X}_{n},d^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{n},\mu_{n})
(see Section
2.6
for more background). This allows us to use the results of
[
39
]
, which give the convergence of the uniform measure on critical percolation clusters. The stationary measure of the CSRW is the
degree-weighted
probability measure on
\mathcal{X}_{n}
, so one would have to adapt the results from
[
39
]
to this setting. This should not be too challenging, since both measures only differ by at most a factor of
6
(the maximum number of neighbors at a site) on each set.

Alexander–Orbach

The resistance estimates we show in this paper can be used to obtain bounds on the spectral dimension of large critical percolation clusters in two dimensions. Corollary
1.4
states that the spectral dimension of the IIC equals
\frac{2D}{D+\beta}
where
D=91/48
is the volume growth exponent and
\beta
is the resistance growth exponent.

We do not obtain a precise value for
\beta
, but it is known that
\beta\in[3/4,4/3]

[
66
]
, implying an upper bound on the spectral dimension of approximately
1.433
. So although this does not disprove the Alexander-Orbach conjecture, we do obtain an upper bound that is not far from the conjectured value of
4/3
. To disprove the conjecture, one would have to improve the lower bound of the resistance growth
\beta
to
91/96\approx 0.948
.

Explicit lengths

In this paper (see Corollary
1.2
), we show that distances in critical planar percolation scale like
n^{\beta+o(1)}
for some exponent
\beta
, thus partially answering
[
75
, Problem 3.3]
. We do not obtain an explicit expression for
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}
in terms of
n
and thus do not obtain any precise estimate for the exponent
\beta
.

Outline

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. We first state the preliminary results needed for the rest of the paper in Section
2
. In Section
3
, we give a detailed overview of the proofs of Theorems
1.1
and
1.5
and Corollaries
1.2
–
1.4
. The rest of the paper is devoted to the proofs of these theorems. In Section
4
, we show the convergence of the percolation clusters as metric spaces defined by
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}
. As a first step towards tightness, we prove in Section
5
polynomial tails for the geodesic distances and effective resistances in various setups. We then improve these results to superpolynomial tails in Section
6
using spatial independence arguments. We then conclude the proof of tightness in Section
7
. In Section
8
, we identify the subsequential limits with the geodesic CLE
6
metric and the CLE
6
resistance metric, respectively, yielding the full convergence of the metrics in Theorem
1.1
. In Section
9
, we explain how the results of
[
39
]
and
[
68
]
imply the convergence of
\mu_{n}^{(m)}
to
\mu^{(m)}
, completing the proof of Theorems
1.1
and
1.5
. Finally, in Section
10
, we deduce the existence of the exponents in the Corollaries
1.2
–
1.4
. We refer to Section
3
for a more detailed outline of the arguments from Sections
4
–
10
.

2.
Preliminaries

The purpose of this section is to collect a number of preliminaries. First, we will describe the notation that we will use in the rest of this article in Section
2.1
. In Section
2.2
we will review the variants of the Gromov-Hausdorff topology which we will consider. In Section
2.3
, we will recall the basic results about arm exponents for critical percolation. We will then give the statements of the results regarding the convergence of percolation in Section
2.4
. The purpose of Section
2.5
is to review the basic properties of
\CLE
metrics (both geodesic and resistance). We finally recall the relationship between resistance metrics and stochastic processes in Section
2.6
.

2.1.
Notation

Throughout the paper, we refer to points in
\mathbf{R}^{2}
by their triangular coordinates as explained at the beginning of Section
1.3
, in particular
[a,b)\times[c,d)
denotes a parallelogram with interior angles
\pi/3,2\pi/3
.

Let
R\in\mathbf{N}
and let
\mathcal{D}^{R}_{k}=\{(iR^{-k},jR^{-k}):0\leq i,j<R^{k}\}
be the set of
R
-adic points in
[0,1)^{2}
at level
k
. Define
\mathcal{B}_{k}^{R}:=\{[iR^{-k},(i+1)R^{-k})\times[jR^{-k},(j+1)R^{-k}):0\leq i,j<R^{k}\}
, i.e., the set of
R
-adic boxes at level
k
. Let
\mathcal{Q}_{k}^{R}:=\{\text{int}(B_{1}\cup B_{2}\cup\ldots)\text{ simply connected}:B_{1},B_{2},\ldots\in\mathcal{B}_{k}^{R}\}
. Further define
\mathcal{B}^{R}:=\bigcup_{k}\mathcal{B}_{k}^{R}
,
\mathcal{Q}^{R}:=\bigcup_{k}\mathcal{Q}_{k}^{R}
and
\mathcal{D}^{R}:=\bigcup_{k}\mathcal{D}_{k}^{R}
. We also let
\mathcal{B}^{R}_{k,n}:=\{[\frac{i}{n}R^{\kappa-k},\frac{i+1}{n}R^{\kappa-k})\times[\frac{j}{n}R^{\kappa-k},\frac{j+1}{n}R^{\kappa-k}):0\leq i,j<R^{k-1}\}
and
\mathcal{D}_{k,n}^{R}:=\{(\frac{i}{n}R^{\kappa-k},\frac{j}{n}R^{\kappa-k})\}_{0\leq i,j<R^{k-1}}
, where
\kappa\in\mathbf{N}
is such that
R^{\kappa-1}<n\leq R^{\kappa}
. The boxes in
\mathcal{B}^{R}_{k,n}
are also called
R
-adic boxes at level
k
. The distinction with
\mathcal{B}^{R}_{k}
should be clear from the context. When
R=2
, we suppress
R
from the notation and simply write
\mathcal{D}_{k},\mathcal{B}_{k},\mathcal{Q}\ldots
.

For
x\in[0,1]^{2}
and
r_{2}>r_{1}>0
, we write
B(x,r_{1})=x+[-r_{1},r_{1})^{2}
and
A(x;r_{1},r_{2})=B(x,r_{2})\setminus B(x,r_{1})
for the box centered at
x
of side length
2r_{1}
and for the parallelogram-shaped annulus of side lengths
2r_{1}
and
2r_{2}
centered at
x
respectively. For some box
B=B(x,r)
and
r^{\prime}>0
, we write
r^{\prime}B:=B(x,r^{\prime}\cdot r)
. Throughout the paper, we will sometimes let a box
B
denote either a subset of
[0,1]^{2}
as above or the subgraph of
\Lambda_{n}
induced by the vertices inside
B
. The exact meaning should be clear from the context.

For
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
, we let
\Lambda_{Q,n}
be the set of sites in
\mathbf{T}_{n}
that are contained in
Q
and
\Omega_{Q,n}:=\{0,1\}^{\Lambda_{Q,n}}
be the set of percolation configurations on
\Lambda_{Q,n}
. If
\Gamma_{Q}
is a collection of
\CLE_{6}
loops in
Q
, we let
\Lambda_{Q}=\Lambda_{Q}(\Gamma_{Q})
be the set of prime ends associated with
\Gamma_{Q}
. In the paper, we might sometimes write
Q
to denote
\Lambda_{Q}
by an abuse of notation when it is clear from the context what is meant. We refer to Section
2.4.1
for more background on CLE and the relationship between
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{Q}
.

We say two points
x,y\in\Lambda_{Q}
are in the same cluster if there exists a path from
x
to
y
in the interior of
Q
that does not cross a loop in
\Gamma_{Q}
, or in other words, if there exists a path from
x
to
y
that is continuous with respect to
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q}
. We label the clusters of
\Gamma_{Q}
in descending order of Euclidean diameter as
\mathcal{X}_{Q}^{(1)},\mathcal{X}_{Q}^{(2)},\ldots
. The (open and closed) percolation clusters of
\Gamma_{Q,n}
are labeled similarly as
\mathcal{X}_{Q,n}^{(1)},\mathcal{X}_{Q,n}^{(2)},\ldots
, breaking ties arbitrarily. We further denote by
\mathcal{X}
(resp.
\mathcal{X}_{n}
) the outermost open
\CLE_{6}
(resp. percolation) cluster surrounding the point
(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
.
2
2

2

We say that a point is surrounded by a loop
\gamma\in\Gamma
(or is inside
\gamma
) if the winding number of
\gamma
about
x
is
\pm 1
(depending on the choice of orientation).

Let
d_{Q,n}
be any (path, geodesic, or resistance) metric on
\Lambda_{Q,n}
. We say, e.g., the (collection of) metric space(s)
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d_{Q,n})
converges to
(\Lambda_{Q},d_{Q})
in some metric
\Delta
if
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n},d_{Q,n})
converge to
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q},d_{Q})
in
\Delta
for each
m
. This convergence is metrized by
\Delta^{\infty}((\Lambda_{Q,n},d_{Q,n}),(\Lambda_{Q},d_{Q}))=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}2^{-m}\!\left(\Delta((\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n},d_{Q,n}),(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q},d_{Q}))\wedge 1\right)
. In other words, we say
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d_{Q,n})
converges to
(\Lambda_{Q},d_{Q})
if, for every
m
, the
m
th largest (in Euclidean diameter) cluster of
\Lambda_{Q,n}
converges to the
m
th largest cluster of
\Lambda_{Q}
.

We let
\dist
,
\diam
denote the Euclidean distance and diameter on
[0,1]^{2}
.

2.2.
Variants of the Gromov-Hausdorff metric

We will now describe some variants of the Gromov-Hausdorff (GH) metric that we will consider in this paper. To begin with, we need to recall the definition of the ordinary GH metric (see
[
83
, Chapter 27]
for a more in depth review), which we recall defines a distance between compact metric spaces. The starting point is the definition of the ordinary Hausdorff metric. Suppose that
(W,d_{W})
is a metric space and
K_{1},K_{2}\subseteq W
are non-empty and compact. Then the Hausdorff distance between
K_{1},K_{2}
is given by

(2.1)

\Delta_{\Haus}(K_{1},K_{2})=\inf\{\epsilon>0:\forall x_{i}\in K_{i},\exists x_{3-i}\in K_{3-i}\text{ s.t.\ }d_{W}(x_{i},x_{3-i})<\epsilon\text{ for }i=1,2\}.

Now suppose that
(X_{i},d_{i})
for
i=1,2
are non-empty compact metric spaces. Then the GH distance between
(X_{1},d_{1})
and
(X_{2},d_{2})
is defined as

(2.2)

\Delta_{\GH}((X_{1},d_{1}),(X_{2},d_{2})):=\inf_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}\Delta_{\Haus}(\psi_{1}(X_{1}),\psi_{2}(X_{2})),

where
\Delta_{\Haus}
is the Hausdorff distance and the infimum is taken over all metric spaces
(W,d_{W})
and isometric embeddings
\psi_{i}\colon X_{i}\to W
for
i=1,2
. It is sometimes more convenient to express the GH distance in terms of
correspondences
. A correspondence is a subset
\mathcal{R}\subseteq X_{1}\times X_{2}
such that for every
x\in X_{1}
, there exists
y\in X_{2}
such that
(x,y)\in\mathcal{R}
and for every
y\in X_{2}
, there exists
x\in X_{1}
such that
(x,y)\in\mathcal{R}
. Then

(2.3)

\Delta_{\GH}((X_{1},d_{1}),(X_{2},d_{2}))=\frac{1}{2}\inf_{\mathcal{R}}\sup_{(x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}}|d_{1}(x,x^{\prime})-d_{2}(y,y^{\prime})|,

where the infimum is over all correspondences
\mathcal{R}
. Furthermore, since the
(X_{i},d_{i})
are compact, there exist isometric embeddings
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
and a correspondence
\mathcal{R}
such that
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
achieve the infimum in (
2.2
) and
\mathcal{R}
achieves the infimum in (
2.3
).
In addition, if
(X_{n},d_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
converges to some
(X,d)
in the GH distance, then there exists some compact metric space
(W,d_{W})
and isometries
\psi_{n}\colon X_{n}\to W
,
\psi\colon X\to W
such that
\psi_{n}(X_{n})
converges to
\psi(X)
in Hausdorff distance.

Lastly, we can still define the GH distance for pseudo-metric spaces. Then if
\Delta_{\GH}((X_{n},d_{n}),(X,d))\rightarrow 0
and
(X,d)
is a pseudo-metric space, then
(X_{n},d_{n})
converges in GH distance to the quotient metric space
(X/{\sim},d)
, where
x\sim y
in
X
if and only if
d(x,y)=0
.

Next, we define the
Gromov-Hausdorff-function
(GHf) distance (see
[
4
, Appendix A]
for more details). Let
(Y,d_{Y})
be a proper metric space (in the sense that every closed ball is compact). Suppose that
X_{i}\subseteq W
are non-empty compact subsets for
i=1,2
and let
f_{i}\colon X_{i}\to Y
be bounded continuous functions. Then we define
\Delta_{f}(f_{1},f_{2})
to be the infimum over
\delta>0
such that for each
x_{i}\in X_{i}
there is
x_{3-i}\in X_{3-i}
with
d_{W}(x_{i},x_{3-i})\leq\delta
and
d_{Y}(f_{i}(x_{i}),f_{3-i}(x_{3-i}))\leq\delta
. Now let
(X_{i},d_{i})
for
i=1,2
be general compact spaces (so not necessarily a subspace of the same space
W
). Then the GHf distance between
(X_{1},d_{1},f_{1})
and
(X_{2},d_{2},f_{2})
is defined as

(2.4)

\Delta_{\GHf}((X_{1},d_{1},f_{1}),(X_{2},d_{2},f_{2})):=\inf_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}\!\left\{\Delta_{\Haus}(\psi_{1}(X_{1}),\psi_{2}(X_{2}))+\Delta_{f}(f_{1}\circ\psi_{1}^{-1},f_{2}\circ\psi_{2}^{-1})\right\}.

We now turn our attention to metric spaces equipped with a measure. Suppose that we have the setting described just above and for each
i=1,2
, let
\mu_{i}
be a finite measure on
X_{i}
. Then the
Gromov-Hausdorff-Prokhorov
(GHP) distance between
(X_{1},d_{1},\mu_{1})
and
(X_{2},d_{2},\mu_{2})
is given by

(2.5)

\Delta_{\GHP}((X_{1},d_{1},\mu_{1}),(X_{2},d_{2},\mu_{2})):=\inf_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}\!\left\{\Delta_{\Haus}(\psi_{1}(X_{1}),\psi_{2}(X_{2}))+\Delta_{\Pro}(\mu_{1}\circ\psi_{1}^{-1},\mu_{2}\circ\psi_{2}^{-1})\right\},

where
\Delta_{\Pro}
is the Prokhorov distance. We also define the
Gromov-Hausdorff-Prokhorov-function
(GHPf) distance as

(2.6)

\begin{split}&\Delta_{\GHPf}((X_{1},d_{1},\mu_{1},f_{1}),(X_{2},d_{2},\mu_{2},f_{2}))\\
&:=\inf_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}\!\left\{\Delta_{\Haus}(\psi_{1}(X_{1}),\psi_{2}(X_{2}))+\Delta_{\Pro}(\mu_{1}\circ\psi_{1}^{-1},\mu_{2}\circ\psi_{2}^{-1})+\Delta_{f}(f_{1}\circ\psi_{1}^{-1},f_{2}\circ\psi_{2}^{-1}\right\}.\end{split}

Lastly, we define the rooted Gromov-Hausdorff-Prokhorov-function distance (rGHPf) between function-equipped metric measure spaces which are marked by a distinguished point (i.e., the root). Let
\rho_{i}\in X_{i}
for
i=1,2
. Then

(2.7)

\begin{split}&\Delta_{\rGHPf}((X_{1},d_{1},\mu_{1},f_{1},\rho_{1}),(X_{2},d_{2},\mu_{2},f_{2},\rho_{2})):=\inf_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}\big\{\Delta_{\Haus}(\psi_{1}(X_{1}),\psi_{2}(X_{2}))+\\
&\Delta_{\Pro}(\mu_{1}\circ\psi_{1}^{-1},\mu_{2}\circ\psi_{2}^{-1})+\Delta_{f}(f_{1}\circ\psi_{1}^{-1},f_{2}\circ\psi_{2}^{-1})+d_{W}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\big\}.\end{split}

2.3.
Arm exponents

We gather all the results on the arm exponents in annuli that we will later use. That is, consider the annulus determined by two boxes
b=B(0,r)
and
B=B(0,R)
, where
r<R
are positive integers. Recall that the boxes and annuli in this paper are always considered to be parallelograms. Let
j\in\mathbf{N}
and let
\sigma
be a sequence of
j
colors (open/closed). We consider the probability
A_{j,\sigma}(b,B)=A_{j,\sigma}(r,R)
of the event that when considering percolation on
\mathbf{T}
, there exist
j
disjoint crossings of the annulus
B\setminus b
that, in clockwise order, have the colors prescribed by
\sigma
. If
j=1
, we simply write
A_{1}(r,R)
and omit the
\sigma
. We refer to these crossings as
arms
.

We can similarly define the arm probabilities
A^{\CLE}_{j,\sigma}(r,R)
for
\CLE_{6}
and
0<r<R
. It is shown in
[
39
, Section 2.4]
that
A^{\CLE}_{j,\sigma}(r,R)=\lim_{n\to\infty}A_{j,\sigma}(nr,nR)
.

We will repeatedly use the following quasi-multiplicativity result shown in
[
70
, Proposition 16]
:

Proposition 2.1
.

Fix some
j\in\mathbf{N}
and let
\sigma
be any sequence of
j
colors. Then there exist
n_{0}(j)\in\mathbf{N}
and an absolute constant
C\in(0,1)
such that

C\cdot A_{j,\sigma}(n_{1},n_{2})\cdot A_{j,\sigma}(n_{2},n_{3})\leq A_{j,\sigma}(n_{1},n_{3})\leq A_{j,\sigma}(n_{1},n_{2})\cdot A_{j,\sigma}(n_{2},n_{3})

for all
n_{0}(j)\leq n_{1}<n_{2}<n_{3}.

The
n_{0}(j)
is there for technical reasons; the
j
arms need to have enough space to approach the box of radius
n_{1}
.

We are also interested in the probability
A^{h}_{j,\sigma}(b,B)=A^{h}_{j,\sigma}(r,R)
of the event that there are
j
arms of colors prescribed by
\sigma
that cross the annulus
A(0;r,R)
and stay in the upper half-plane
\{(x,y)\mid y>0\}
. Quasi-multiplicativity holds for half-plane events as well – the proof of Proposition
2.1
given in
[
70
]
also applies to this case.

For non-constant sequences
\sigma
, we know by
[
78
, Theorem 4]
that for
j>1

A_{j,\sigma}(r,R)=R^{-(j^{2}-1)/12+o(1)},\textrm{ as }R\rightarrow\infty.

Further,
[
78
, Theorem 3]
says that for
any

\sigma
we know that

A_{j,\sigma}^{h}(r,R)=R^{-j(j+1)/6+o(1)},\textrm{ as }R\rightarrow\infty.

Combining these results and Proposition
2.1
we get the following lemma.

Lemma 2.2
.

For every
\zeta>0
,
j\geq 1
, there exists
\rho_{\zeta,j}>0
big enough such that

(a)

If
j>1
, for every non-constant sequence of colors
\sigma
and
for all
R\geq r\geq\rho_{\zeta,j}

\!\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{-\alpha_{j}-\zeta}\leq\mathbf{P}(j\textrm{ arms of colors given by }\sigma\textrm{ in }A(r,R))\leq\!\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{-\alpha_{j}+\zeta},

where
\alpha_{j}=\frac{j^{2}-1}{12}
are the full-plane polychromatic exponents.

(b)

For every sequence of colors
\sigma
and for all
R\geq r\geq\rho_{\zeta,j}

\begin{split}\!\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{-\alpha^{h}_{j}-\zeta}\leq\mathbf{P}(j\textrm{ arms of colors given by }\sigma\textrm{ in }A(r,R){\textrm{ in the half-plane}})\leq\!\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{-\alpha^{h}_{j}+\zeta},\end{split}

where
\alpha^{h}_{j}=\frac{j(j+1)}{6}
are the half-plane exponents.

In the half-plane, there is no difference between polychromatic and monochromatic events – the reason is that one can explore the crossings from “below” one-by-one and flip all colors above some of them without changing the probabilities of configurations. However, we cannot do this in the full-plane case, as the lowest crossing is no longer well defined. The following lemma, given in
[
16
, Theorem 4 and Theorem 5]
, tells us that monochromatic exponents also exist and bounds them in terms of the polychromatic exponents.

Lemma 2.3
.

For every
\zeta>0
,
j\geq 2
, and a constant sequence of colors
\sigma
, there exists
\rho_{\zeta,j}^{\prime}>0
big enough such that for all
R\geq r\geq\rho_{\zeta,j}^{\prime}

\!\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{-\alpha_{j}^{\prime}-\zeta}\leq\mathbf{P}(j\textrm{ arms of colors prescribed by }\sigma\textrm{ in }A(r,R))\leq\!\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{-\alpha_{j}^{\prime}+\zeta},

where
\alpha_{j}^{\prime}
are the monochromatic exponents that satisfy
\alpha_{j}<\alpha_{j}^{\prime}<\alpha_{j+1}
.

2.4.
Percolation convergence results

We are now going to review prior convergence results for percolation that will be useful in this work.

2.4.1.
Convergence of interface loops and the quad-crossing space

Let
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
be a union of dyadic boxes (see Section
2.1
). Recall that
\Lambda_{Q,n}
is the set of sites in
\mathbf{T}_{n}
that are contained in
Q
, and let
\partial\Lambda_{Q,n}
be the set of sites in
\mathbf{T}_{n}
that are not contained in
Q
, but have a neighbor in
\Lambda_{Q,n}
. If we consider all the sites in
\partial\Lambda_{Q,n}
to be closed, we obtain a collection
\Gamma_{Q,n}
of loops which are contained in
Q
, where each loop
\gamma\in\Gamma_{Q,n}
is the interface loop of a percolation cluster (i.e., the interface between open and closed). If we let
W
be the (not necessarily simply connected) union of finitely many
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
, we define the collections
\Gamma_{W,n}
and
\Gamma_{W^{c},n}
analogously. If
Q=(0,1)^{2}
, we just write
\Gamma_{n}:=\Gamma_{(0,1)^{2},n}
.

For two curves
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\colon[0,1]\to[0,1]^{2}
, we define the uniform metric modulo parameterization as

(2.8)

\Delta_{\cur}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=\inf_{\psi}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\eta(\psi(t))-\eta^{\prime}(t)\|_{2},

where the infimum is over all homeomorphisms
\psi\colon[0,1]\to[0,1]
that fix 0 and 1 and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
refers to the Euclidean norm. For two loops
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\colon S^{1}\to[0,1]^{2}
, we also define the uniform metric modulo parameterization as

(2.9)

\Delta_{\cur}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})=\inf_{\psi}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\gamma(\psi(t))-\gamma^{\prime}(t)\|_{2},

where the infimum is now over all orientation preserving homeomorphisms
\psi\colon S^{1}\to S^{1}
from the unit circle to itself and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
refers to the Euclidean norm. For two collections of curves
\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}
, we let
\Delta_{\Hc}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})
be the Hausdorff distance between
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
with respect to
\Delta_{\cur}
.

Camia and Newman
[
26
]
showed that the percolation interface loops converge weakly to
\CLE_{6}
.

Theorem 2.4
.

The collection
(\Gamma_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
converges weakly to the collection of
\CLE_{6}
loops
\Gamma
with respect to
\Delta_{\Hc}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

For this paper, we will need to consider not only the percolation on the entire box
\Lambda_{n}
, but also on subboxes
\Lambda_{Q,n}
. A different way to encode a percolation configuration other than by its interface loops, is by considering the collection of
quads
it crosses, which are homeomorphisms
R\colon[0,1]^{2}\to\mathbf{C}
. A quad is crossed if there exists an open path within
R([0,1]^{2})
that intersects both the left-hand side (i.e.,
R(\{0\}\times[0,1])
) and right-hand side (i.e.,
R(\{1\}\times[0,1])
) of the quad. Similarly, each collection of
\CLE_{6}
-loops
\Gamma
also induces a collection of crossed quads. It was shown by Garban, Pete and Schramm in
[
39
, Section 2.3]
that the collection of percolation-quads also converges to the collection of
\CLE_{6}
-quads in an appropriate topology on the space of collections of quads. Furthermore, the quad-limit is almost surely measurable with respect to the
\CLE_{6}
loops
\Gamma
. Holden and Sun showed in
[
47
, Theorem 6.10]
that the converse is also true.

For a collection
\mathcal{S}
of quads and a simply connected open subdomain
U
, we can simply define the induced collection
\mathcal{S}_{U}
as all the quads in
\mathcal{S}
that lie inside
U
. This defines a
\CLE_{6}
in
U
in the quad-crossing sense. By the measurability results stated above, we can then identify this with a collection of
\CLE_{6}
loops
\Gamma_{U}
.
For general subdomains
U
,
one can still obtain a collection of loops by applying the measurability result to each simply connected subdomain of
U
. The following result proved in
[
73
, Theorem 1.19]
shows that
\CLE_{6}
is measurable with respect to any nice subdomain and its complement. For notational simplicity, we write
\Gamma_{U^{c}}=\Gamma_{\overline{U}^{c}}
. In
[
73
, Corollary 1.20]
, percolation is also characterized as a
black noise
as defined by Tsirelson in
[
80
,
81
,
82
]
. This implies that percolation in disjoint domains is independent.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
U\in[0,1]^{2}
be an open and simply connected domain such that its boundary
\partial U
is a rectifiable loop with finitely many double points. Then the
\CLE_{6}

\Gamma
is almost surely measurable with respect to the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\Gamma_{U}
and
\Gamma_{U^{c}}
. Furthermore,
\Gamma_{U}
and
\Gamma_{U^{c}}
are independent.

The following convergence result is shown in
[
39
, Section 2.3]
.

Theorem 2.6
.

There exists a coupling of percolation on
\Lambda_{n}
and the collection of
\CLE_{6}
loops
\Gamma
such that almost surely for all
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
, the sequences
(\Gamma_{Q,n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
and
(\Gamma_{Q^{c},n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
converge to
\Gamma_{Q}
and
\Gamma_{Q^{c}}
with respect to the quad-crossing topology introduced in
[
73
]
.

In Proposition
3.1
, we show that we can further couple percolation on
\Lambda_{n}
and
\Gamma
such that almost surely for all
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
, the sequence
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},\pi_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
converges to
(\Lambda_{Q},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q},\pi)
in the
\GHf
distance.

In this paper, we will not need the precise quad crossing machinery. Rather, we only need to know that we can define
\CLE_{6}
on subdomains in such a way that Lemma
2.5
and Theorem
2.6
hold. Therefore, we do not give the precise definitions regarding the space of quads and its topology. Instead, we refer to the cited works above for more background.

We finish this part with the following lemma, which will be used throughout the paper. The lemma states that loops that touch in the
\CLE_{6}
will also eventually touch in the percolation configuration under a suitable coupling.

Lemma 2.7
.

There exists a coupling such that almost surely
(\Lambda_{n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n},\pi_{n})\to(\Lambda,d^{{\mathrm{path}}},\pi)
in GHf and the following holds. Suppose that
(\Lambda_{n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n},\pi_{n})
and
(\Lambda,d^{{\mathrm{path}}},\pi)
are isometrically embedded into a common metric space
(W,d_{W})
such that the sequence converges under the
\Delta_{\Haus}+\Delta_{f}
metric in (
2.4
). Let
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
be two different
\CLE_{6}
loops that touch at a prime end
z\in\Lambda
that is outside the loops
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
. Let
\gamma_{n},\gamma^{\prime}_{n}
be percolation interface loops converging to
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
respectively. Then there exists
n_{0}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
, the loops
\gamma_{n}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{n}
touch (i.e., come within distance
1
of each other) at a point
z_{n}
such that
z_{n}\rightarrow z
as
n\rightarrow\infty
in
d_{W}
(and hence also
\pi_{n}(z_{n})\to\pi(z)
in
\mathbf{R}^{2}
). The analogous statement holds for a loop
\gamma
that touches itself at a point
z
, and for a loop
\gamma
that touches the boundary
\partial[0,1]^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{6}(x,\varepsilon,\rho)
denote a polychromatic
6
-arm event in the annulus
A(x;\varepsilon,\rho)
. By
[
39
, Lemma 2.9]
, we have that
(\Gamma_{n},\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{6}(x,\varepsilon,\rho)}(\Gamma_{n}))
converges in law to
(\Gamma,\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{6}(x,\varepsilon,\rho)}(\Gamma))
. Combining this with Proposition
3.1
, we can couple
(\Gamma_{n},\Gamma)
so that
((\Lambda_{n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n},\pi_{n}),(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{6}(x_{i},\varepsilon_{i},\rho_{i})}(\Gamma_{n}))_{i\in\mathbf{N}})
converges almost surely to
((\Lambda,d^{{\mathrm{path}}},\pi),(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{6}(x_{i},\varepsilon_{i},\rho_{i})}(\Gamma))_{i\in\mathbf{N}})
for a countable dense set of annuli.

Suppose there exist two
\CLE_{6}
loops
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
that touch at some point
z
, but such that their approximants
\gamma_{n},\gamma^{\prime}_{n}
do not touch for all
n
. Fix any
0<\rho<\min\{\diam(\gamma),\diam(\gamma^{\prime})\}
. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists
n_{0}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
,
\gamma_{n}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{n}
come within distance
\varepsilon
of
z
. Then the box
B(z,\varepsilon)
has
6
arms to distance
\rho/2
. Since the
6
-arm exponent is larger than
2
, the probability that there is such a box goes to
0
as we let
\varepsilon\searrow 0
and
n\to\infty
. In the coupling described above, almost surely, for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small and
n
sufficiently large, there is no such box in the percolation configuration. This is a contradiction, so
\gamma_{n}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{n}
must touch within distance
\rho
of
z
for all
n
sufficiently large. By considering smaller and smaller constants
\rho>0
, we can find touching points
z_{n}
that also satisfy
\|z-z_{n}\|_{2}\rightarrow 0
.

It remains to show that
z_{n}\rightarrow z
in
d_{W}
. Note that since
z
is a touching point of two loops, there also exist
z^{\prime}\in\Lambda
inside the loop
\gamma^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime\prime}\in\Lambda
inside the loop
\gamma
with
\pi(z)=\pi(z^{\prime})=\pi(z^{\prime\prime})
, i.e.,
z
is one of three
prime ends
. By definition of Gromov-Hausdorff convergence, each subsequence
I
has a further subsequence
J
such that
(z_{n})_{n\in J}
converges in
d_{W}
. This limit must be one of
z,z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime}
. Since none of the
z_{n}
are inside
\gamma_{n}
and
\gamma_{n}^{\prime\prime}
, we must have that the limit is
z
. We conclude that
z_{n}\rightarrow z
in
d_{W}
.

For a loop
\gamma
touching itself or the boundary, the proof is similar. In the boundary case, we use instead that the half-plane
3
-arm exponent is larger than
1
.
∎

2.4.2.
Convergence of the cluster measure

In order to prove the GHP convergence from Theorem
1.1
, we first prove GH convergence of the metric spaces and then use prior results on convergence of the one-arm measure on percolation clusters to strengthen this to GHP convergence. In this section, we state the required cluster measure results proved in
[
39
]
and
[
68
]
.

Let us start with the annulus counting measure. Let
A
be a topological annulus in
[0,1]^{2}
with piecewise smooth boundary. Let
A^{\inn}
(resp.
A^{\out}
) denote its inner (resp. outer) face, i.e. the bounded (resp. unbounded) connected component of
\mathbf{R}^{2}\setminus A
. Define

(2.10)

\mu_{n}^{A}:=\frac{1}{n^{2}A_{1}(1,n)}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{n}\cap A^{\inn}}\mathbf{1}_{\{x\leftrightarrow A^{\out}\}}\delta_{x},

i.e. the normalized counting measure of the set of points in
\Lambda_{n}\cap A^{\inn}
that are connected to the outer boundary of
A
.

Theorem 2.8

(
[
39
, Theorem 5.1]
)
.

There exists a coupling of
\Gamma_{n}
and
\Gamma
such that the sequence
(\mu^{A}_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
converges almost surely in Prokhorov distance to some measure
\mu^{A}
. The measure
\mu^{A}
is a function of the collection
\Gamma
of
\CLE_{6}
loops.

Let
B\subset[0,1]^{2}
be a domain with rectifiable boundary and let
Y^{A}_{k}(B)
and
Y^{A}_{k,n}(B)
be the number of boxes
Q
in
\mathcal{B}_{k}
that are contained in
B\cap A^{\inn}
and such that
2Q\leftrightarrow A^{\out}
in respectively the
\CLE_{6}
and the percolation configuration.
The following description of
\mu^{A}
is essentially proved in
[
39
, Section 4.6]
. Note that this result was stated for the pivotal measure rather than the cluster measure, but it was remarked in Section 5.1 of that paper that the analogous results also hold for the cluster measure.

Proposition 2.9
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that for each (deterministic) domain
B\subset[0,1]^{2}
with rectifiable boundary,

(2.11)

\mu^{A}(B)=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{cY^{A}_{k}(B)}{2^{2k}A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},1)}\qquad\text{in $L^{2}$},

where
A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},1)
is the
\CLE_{6}
one-arm probability as defined in Section
2.3
.

Proof.

It is shown in
[
39
, Section 4.6]
that there is a coupling under which the following hold. Firstly, there exists
\zeta(n,k)
that tends to 0 as
k\rightarrow\infty
and
\frac{n}{2^{k}}\rightarrow\infty
and such that

(2.12)

\left\|\mu^{A}_{n}(B)-\frac{cY^{A}_{k,n}(B)}{2^{2k}A_{1}(n2^{-k},n)}\right\|_{2}\leq\zeta(n,k).

Furthermore, for all
k
,

(2.13)

\frac{cY^{A}_{k,n}(B)}{2^{2k}A_{1}(n2^{-k},n)}\to\frac{cY^{A}_{k}(B)}{2^{2k}A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},1)}\quad\text{in probability,}\qquad n\rightarrow\infty.

Lastly,
(\frac{cY^{A}_{k}(B)}{2^{2k}A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},1)})_{k\in\mathbf{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{2}
, and so

(2.14)

\widetilde{\mu}^{A}(B):=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{cY^{A}_{k}(B)}{2^{2k}A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},1)}\qquad\text{in $L^{2}$}

exists. Therefore we just need to argue that
\mu^{A}(B)=\widetilde{\mu}^{A}(B)
almost surely. Combining the above, taking
n\rightarrow\infty
in (
2.12
) and then
k\rightarrow\infty
in (
2.14
), it follows that for all
B
,

(2.15)

\widetilde{\mu}^{A}(B)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mu_{n}^{A}(B)\qquad\text{in probability}.

Now recall that
\mu^{A}
is the a.s. limit of
(\mu^{A}_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
with respect to the Lévy-Prokhorov distance. It is shown in
[
39
, Corollary 4.16]
that under a suitable coupling, for each
B
with rectifiable boundary, we have the
L^{2}
-convergence
\mu_{n}^{A}(B)\to\mu^{A}(B)
and that
\mu^{A}(\partial B)=0
almost surely. This implies that
\mu^{A}(B)=\widetilde{\mu}^{A}(B)
almost surely and completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.10
.

Fix an annulus
A
and
r>0
. For each
\delta>0
, let
X_{\delta}
be the set of points
x\in A^{\inn}
such that there are two disjoint (open or closed) arms crossing
B(x,r)\setminus B(x,\delta)
. Then

\lim_{\delta\searrow 0}\mathbf{E}[\mu^{A}(X_{\delta})]=0.

In particular, the set of points with two disjoint arms to distance
r
has measure
0
almost surely.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{X}_{\delta}
be the union of boxes
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\delta}
such that there are two disjoint arms from
2B
to distance
r
. Then
X_{\delta}\subseteq\widetilde{X}_{\delta}
. By applying Proposition
2.9
to each of the finite number of boxes in
\mathcal{B}_{\delta}
we see for each fixed
\delta
that

\mathbf{E}[\mu^{A}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})]=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{c\mathbf{E}[Y^{A}_{k}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})]}{2^{2k}A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},1)}

By the BK inequality, we have
A^{\CLE}_{2}(\delta,r)\leq A^{\CLE}_{1}(\delta,r)^{2}
, and by Lemma
2.1
we have
A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},1)\asymp A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},\delta)A^{\CLE}_{1}(\delta,r)
. Combining everything gives

\begin{split}\mathbf{E}[Y^{A}_{k}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})]&=\sum_{B\in\mathcal{B}_{\delta}}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{B}_{k},Q\subseteq B}\mathbf{P}[B\subseteq\widetilde{X}_{\delta},\,Q\leftrightarrow A^{\out}]\\
&\asymp 2^{2k}A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},\delta)A^{\CLE}_{2}(\delta,r)\\
&\leq 2^{2k}A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},\delta)A^{\CLE}_{1}(\delta,r)^{2}\\
&\lesssim 2^{2k}A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},r)A^{\CLE}_{1}(\delta,r).\end{split}

Therefore we have

\mathbf{E}[\mu^{A}(X_{\delta})]\leq\mathbf{E}[\mu^{A}(\widetilde{X}_{\delta})]\lesssim A^{\CLE}_{1}(\delta,r),

which tends to
0
as
\delta\rightarrow 0
.
∎

Let us turn to the
\CLE_{6}
gasket measure. The conformally covariant volume measure on the
\CLE_{6}
gasket was first constructed in
[
64
]
. It is uniquely characterized up to a deterministic constant which we fix arbitrarily. For each cluster
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}
as defined in Section
1.3
, we let
\mu^{(m)}
denote the gasket measure on
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}
. The following characterization is shown in
[
68
]
. Let
B\subseteq[0,1]^{2}
be a box or ball and let
Y^{(m)}_{k}(B)
be the number of boxes
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{k}
such that
Q\subset B
and such that
2Q
has non-empty intersection with
\pi(\mathcal{X}^{(m)})
.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
\mu^{(m)}
be the
\CLE_{6}
gasket measure on
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}
. Then there exists a constant
c>0
such that for each box or ball
B\subseteq[0,1]^{2}
,

(2.16)

\mu^{(m)}(B)=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{cY^{(m)}_{k}(B)}{2^{(91/48)k}}\qquad\text{almost surely}.

Since the exact renormalization constant is shown in
[
66
]
, we obtain the following asymptotic expression for the CLE
6
one-arm probability by a comparison.

Corollary 2.12
.

There is a constant
c>0
such that

A^{\CLE}(2^{-k},1)=c2^{-(5/48)k}+o(2^{-(5/48)k})\quad\text{as }k\to\infty.

The proof of this statement is given in Section
9
.

2.5.
CLE metrics

The limiting measures
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}
and
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
are examples of a class of (pseudo-)metrics introduced in
[
4
]
called
\CLE
metrics
, which we define in this section. Note that the definitions in this paper are in fact slightly stronger than those in
[
4
]
. This is because
[
4
]
work in a very general framework, whereas we are only interested in continuous geodesic and resistance
\CLE_{6}
metrics.

We first generalize the metric
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}
from (
1.5
) to encode also the topologies of regions whose interior consist of collections of domains. Let
U\subset[0,1]^{2}
and let
\mathcal{G}
be any collection of loops in
\mathbf{R}^{2}
. Define
P(x,y;U;\mathcal{G})
to be the set of paths from
x\in U
to
y\in U
that stay inside
U
that do not cross any loop in
\mathcal{G}
. Define

(2.17)

d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{U,\mathcal{G}}(x,y)=\inf\{\diam(\gamma):\gamma\in P(x,y;U;\mathcal{G})\},

where
\diam
denotes diameter with respect to the Euclidean metric. If
\mathcal{G}
is the entire collection of
\CLE_{6}
loops
\Gamma
, then we simply write
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{U,\Gamma}=d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{U}
.

Recall that the convergence results in Section
1.3
were stated simultaneously for all clusters. In order to identify the limit with the
\CLE_{6}
metric, it suffices to consider a specific cluster which we denote by
\mathcal{X}
. The choice of
\mathcal{X}
is arbitrary, but for the sake of consistency with
[
4
,
66
,
67
]
, we choose
\mathcal{X}
to be the outermost open cluster in
\Gamma
surrounding the center
(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
. By the local absolute continuity between the different interior clusters, the
\CLE_{6}
metric on each cluster
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}
can be recovered from the
\CLE_{6}
metric on the cluster
\mathcal{X}
, see
[
67
]
. We define
\mathcal{X}_{n}
similarly for the percolation configuration. Therefore it suffices to prove the convergence of the geodesic and resistance metrics on
\mathcal{X}_{n}
to those on
\mathcal{X}
.

To be precise, let
\mathcal{L}
be the outermost loop in
\Gamma
surrounding the point
(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
(in the sense that its winding number about
(1/2,1/2)
is
\pm 1
) and let
\mathcal{X}
be the gasket of
\Gamma
whose exterior boundary is
\mathcal{L}
as defined in Section
1.3
. Write
\Gamma_{\mathcal{L}}
to be the collection of loops in
\Gamma
that are in the next nesting level inside
\mathcal{L}
. Let
D
be the regions surrounded by
\mathcal{L}
(i.e., the union of connected components of
(0,1)^{2}\setminus\mathcal{L}
with winding number 1). For each open, simply connected
U\subset[0,1]^{2}
, let
\Gamma_{U^{*}}
be the collection of loops in
\Gamma
that are inside
\mathcal{L}
and entirely contained in
U
, and let
U^{*}
be the set of points in
[0,1]^{2}
that are inside
\mathcal{L}
but not on or inside any other loop of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{L}}\setminus\Gamma_{U^{*}}
. We view
U^{*}
as the metric space equipped with the metric
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{\overline{U^{*}},\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{U^{*}}}
. Note that
\Gamma_{U^{*}}
is indeed a
\CLE_{6}
inside
U^{*}
as defined in Section
2.4.1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{G}=\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m}\}\subset\Gamma_{\mathcal{L}}
and
V\subset D
is a union of Euclidean connected components of
D\setminus\mathcal{G}
that are not inside the loops
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m}
. We let
\mathfrak{C}
be the collection of regions
V\subset D
as described above (for some choice of
\mathcal{G}
) such that
\overline{V}
is simply connected with respect to
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{\overline{V},\{\mathcal{L}\}\cup\mathcal{G}}
(where
\overline{V}
denotes the completion with respect to
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{\overline{V},\{\mathcal{L}\}\cup\mathcal{G}}
). For
U\subset[0,1]^{2}
open, we let
\mathfrak{C}_{U}=\{V\in\mathfrak{C}:\overline{V}\subset U\}
.

Let
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
be a collection of resistance or geodesic (pseudo-)metric spaces coupled with
\Gamma
. Here the notation
\mathcal{X}\cap U
is shorthand for the set of sites
x\in\mathcal{X}\subset\Lambda
such that
\pi(x)\in U
. We say it is a
\CLE_{6}
metric if the following hold almost surely.

Continuity:
For each
V\in\mathfrak{C}
, we have that
\mathfrak{d}_{V}
is continuous with respect to
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{\overline{V}}
.

Markovian property:
Let
U\subset[0,1]^{2}
be open and simply connected. The conditional law of the collection
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
given
\{\mathcal{L}\}\cup\Gamma_{\mathcal{L}}\setminus\Gamma_{U^{*}}
and
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V^{\prime}},\mathfrak{d}_{V^{\prime}})_{V^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}_{[0,1]^{2}\setminus\overline{U}}}
is almost surely measurable with respect to
U^{*}
.

Translation invariance:

Let
D_{1}
be the support of
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
and
D_{2}
be the support of
U^{*}
. Let
\mu_{U}(\cdot\mid\cdot)
be a probability kernel from
D_{2}
to
D_{1}
such that
\mu_{U}(\cdot\mid U^{*})
is a.s. equal to the conditional law of
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
given
U^{*}
. Then for each
z\in\mathbf{C}
such that
U+z\subset[0,1]^{2}
one can choose versions of
\mu_{U}
and
\mu_{U+z}
such that for all events
A
in
D_{1}
and
u\in D_{2}
we have

(2.18)

\mu_{U}(A\mid u)=\mu_{U+z}(A+z\mid u+z).

where
A+z
denotes the event that the translated metrics
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}-z,\mathfrak{d}_{V}(\cdot+z,\cdot+z))_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U+z}}
are in the event
A
.

Compatibility:
Let
V,V^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}
,
V\subset V^{\prime}
, and
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
such that for every
u\in V^{\prime}\setminus V
there is a point
z\in\overline{V}
that separates
u
from
x,y
in
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V^{\prime}}
. Then

(2.19)

\mathfrak{d}_{V^{\prime}}(x,y)=\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y).

Monotonicity:
Let
V,V^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}
,
V\subset V^{\prime}
. Then, for each
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
,

(2.20)

\mathfrak{d}_{V^{\prime}}(x,y)\leq\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y).

Series law:
Let
V\in\mathfrak{C}
and let
x,y,z\in V
such that
z
separates
x
from
y
in
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
. Then

(2.21)

\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y)=\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,z)+\mathfrak{d}_{V}(z,y).

Generalized parallel law:
Let
V\in\mathfrak{C}
and let
x,y,z_{1},\ldots,z_{M}\in V
such that
x
and
y
are separated in
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}\setminus\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{M}\}
. Let
K_{x}
be the connected component of
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}\setminus\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{M}\}
containing
x
, and let
V_{x}\in\mathfrak{C}
be the smallest region such that
\overline{V_{x}}\supseteq K_{x}
. Then

(2.22)

\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y)\geq\frac{1}{M}\min_{i}\mathfrak{d}_{V_{x}}(x,z_{i}).

The following is shown in
[
4
, Proposition 6.14]
.

Proposition 2.13
.

Let
(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
be a
\CLE
(pseudo-)metric. Then either
\mathfrak{d}_{V}=0
for each
V\in\mathfrak{C}
or it is in fact a true metric, i.e.,
\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y)>0
for each
V\in\mathfrak{C}
and
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
with
x\neq y
.

We now turn our attention to specifically geodesic and resistance
\CLE
metrics. We say a collection
(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
is a
geodesic
\CLE
metric
3
3

3

In
[
67
]
, this definition is called a
weak
geodesic CLE metric but it is shown that each weak geodesic CLE metric is a (strong) geodesic CLE metric, see Theorem
2.14
.
if it is a CLE
6
metric in the sense defined above and additionally the following holds. For every
V\in\mathfrak{C}
and
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
, we have

(2.23)

\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y)=\inf_{\gamma\in P(x,y;\overline{V})}L_{\mathfrak{d}}(\gamma),

where

(2.24)

L_{\mathfrak{d}}(\gamma)=\sup_{0\leq t_{0}\leq\ldots\leq t_{M}\leq 1}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathfrak{d}_{D}(\gamma(t_{i-1}),\gamma(t_{i}))

is the
length
of
\gamma
with respect to
\mathfrak{d}_{D}
.

The following is shown in
[
67
]
. Recall the conformally covariant geodesic metric
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
from
[
67
]
introduced in Section
1.3
.

Theorem 2.14
.

Suppose that we have the setup described above and
(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
is a geodesic CLE
6
metric. Then there exists a deterministic constant
c
such that
\mathfrak{d}_{D}=c\,d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}|_{\mathcal{X}}
almost surely.

We now define the
\CLE_{6}
resistance metric
. First, we recall the definition of a resistance metric
[
52
]
. A metric
R
on a non-empty set
X
is called a
resistance metric
if for each finite subset
A\subseteq X
there is a (unique) symmetric weight function
w\colon A\times A\to[0,\infty)
such that the restriction of
R
to
A
agrees with the effective resistance metric associated with the weighted graph
G=(A,w)
.

We say
(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
is a
\CLE_{6}
resistance metric
4
4

4

In
[
66
]
, this definition is called the resistance metric of a
weak
CLE resistance form but it is shown that each weak CLE resistance form is a (strong) CLE resistance form, see Theorem
2.15
.
if it is a CLE
6
metric in the sense defined above and additionally the following properties are satisfied.

(a)

For each
V\in\mathfrak{C}
, we have that
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})
is a resistance metric space.

(b)

Suppose that
V,V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}
are such that
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}=(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V_{1}})\cup(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V_{2}})
and
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V_{1}})\cap(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V_{2}})=\{a,b\}
. There exists a countable set
\mathcal{Z}_{V}=\{z_{1},z_{2},\ldots\}\subseteq\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
that contains
a,b
, is dense with respect to
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{\overline{V}}
, and such that the following holds for sufficiently large
m
. Let
A=\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}\}
,
A_{1}=A\cap\overline{V_{1}}
,
A_{2}=A\cap\overline{V_{2}}
, and let
w
(resp.
w_{1}
,
w_{2}
) be the weight function associated with
\mathfrak{d}_{V}(\cdot,\cdot)|_{A}
(resp.
\mathfrak{d}_{V_{1}}(\cdot,\cdot)|_{A_{1}}
,
\mathfrak{d}_{V_{2}}(\cdot,\cdot)|_{A_{2}}
). Then

•

w(x,y)=w_{1}(x,y)
for each
x,y\in A_{1}
;

•

w(x,y)=w_{2}(x,y)
for each
x,y\in A_{2}
;

•

w(x,y)=0
for each
x\in A_{1}\setminus\{a,b\}
,
y\in A_{2}\setminus\{a,b\}
;

•

w(a,b)=0
.

The following is shown in
[
66
]
. Recall the conformally covariant resistance metric
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
from
[

]
introduced in Section

.

Theorem 2.15
.

Suppose that we have the setup described above and
(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
is a CLE
6
resistance metric. Then there exists a deterministic constant
c
such that
\mathfrak{d}_{D}=c\,d^{{\mathrm{res}}}|_{\mathcal{X}}
almost surely.

2.6.
Resistance metrics and stochastic processes

An important tool in the proof of the scaling limit of random walk on
\mathcal{X}_{n}
is the connection between resistance metrics and stochastic processes. We refer to
[
30
]
for a definition of resistance metrics on general spaces. See
[
61
, Chapter 9]
for a more accessible perspective from the viewpoint of random walks. The following result was proved by Croydon in
[
30
]
. Let
(K,d,\mu,f,\rho)
be a rooted metric-measure-function space. We assume that
(K,d)
is non-empty and compact,
d
is a resistance metric,
5
5

5

Since
(K,d)
is compact,
d
is a
regular
resistance metric, see
[
53
, Corollary 6.4]
.

\mu
is a finite Borel measure with full support on
(K,d)
, and
\rho\in K
. See
[
30
]
for the definition of a regular resistance metric. Then there exists a unique
Hunt process

X
associated with
(K,d,\mu,\rho)
with
X_{0}=\rho
. A Hunt process is a strong Markov process that is càdlàg and
quasi-left continuous
, i.e., if
(\sigma_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
is an increasing sequence of stopping times with limit
\sigma
, then

(2.25)

\mathbf{P}(\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}X_{\sigma_{n}}=X_{\sigma}\mid\sigma<\infty)=1.

If
K
is the vertex set of a finite graph and
d
the associated resistance metric, then the associated Hunt process is the continuous-time random walk with jump rates such that
\mu
is its stationary measure.

Theorem 2.16

(
[
30
, Theorem 7.1]
)
.

Let
(K_{n},d_{n},\mu_{n},f_{n},\rho_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
be a sequence of rooted metric-measure-function spaces where
f_{n}\colon(K_{n},d_{n})\to(Y,d_{Y})
and
f\colon(K,d)\to(Y,d_{Y})
are continuous and
(Y,d_{Y})
is some compact metric space. Assume that

\Delta_{\rGHPf}((K_{n},d_{n},\mu_{n},f_{n},\rho_{n}),(K,d,\mu,f,\rho))\to 0.

Let
X_{n}
and
X
be the Hunt processes associated with
(K_{n},d_{n},\mu_{n},\rho_{n})
and
(K,d,\mu,\rho)
, respectively. Then
f_{n}(X_{n})
converge weakly to
f(X)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
in the space of cadlag processes on
Y
equipped with the Skorokhod
J_{1}
-topology.

Note that Croydon does not use the term
\GHPf
convergence, but rather calls it
spatial
\GHP
convergence.
The random walk convergence from Theorem
1.5
is thus a direct consequence of Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
2.16
. (Note that since the limiting process
X
is continuous, convergence in the Skorokhod
J_{1}
-topology is equivalent to the uniform convergence on compact time intervals.)

3.
Outline of the proof

The proof of Theorem
1.1
has three main components. First, we prove that the sequence of path metric spaces
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n},\pi_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
converges weakly with respect to the GHf topology. We give a sketch of this part in Section
3.1
. The majority of this paper is devoted to proving tightness of the normalized geodesic and resistance metrics with respect to the GHf topology, which is the second part. A sketch of this step is given in Section
3.2
. In that section, we also define the normalizing constants
({\mathfrak{q}}_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
. For the third part, we show that every subsequential weak limit is a
\CLE_{6}
metric by checking it has the properties stated in Section
2.5
. The uniqueness results from Theorems
2.14
and
2.15
then imply that all subsequential limits are in fact the same, which completes the proof of GHf convergence of the geodesic and resistance metrics. See Section
3.3
for an overview. To complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
, it only remains to strengthen the GHf convergence to GHPf convergence, using the measure convergence from Section
2.4.2
. Convergence of the random walk from Theorem
1.5
then follows from the GHPf convergence of the resistance metric combined with Theorem
2.16
. These last two steps are sketched in Sections
3.4
and
3.5
.

We give a unified proof of Theorem
1.1
that works for both the geodesic and resistance metric simultaneously. The proof relies only on the following properties of the metrics. Let
D_{n}
denote either
D_{n}^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
or
D_{n}^{{\mathrm{res}}}
and let
d_{n}
denote the rescaled metrics
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}_{n}
or
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{n}
respectively, as defined in Section
1
. Let
x,y\in\Lambda_{n}
be sites that belong to the same open cluster.

(A)

If there exists a site
a\in\Lambda_{n}
such that every open path from
x
to
y
has to pass through
a
, then
D_{n}(x,y)=D_{n}(x,a)+D_{n}(a,y)
.

(B)

If there exists
M\in\mathbf{N}
and a set of sites
a_{1},\ldots,a_{M}
such that every open path from
x
to
y
has to pass through at least one
a_{i}
, then we have that
D_{n}(x,y)\geq\frac{1}{M}\min_{1\leq i\leq M}D_{n}(x,a_{i})
.

(C)

If
G
is a subgraph of
\Lambda_{n}
containing both sites
x,y
, then
D_{G}(x,y)\geq D_{n}(x,y)
, where
D_{G}
denotes the induced geodesic/resistance metric on
G
.

The same statements hold for
d_{n}
. The three properties are clearly true for the geodesic metric. For the resistance metric, (
A
) is the series law, (
B
) follows from the parallel law, and (
C
) is Rayleigh’s monotonicity principle. For the majority of the paper, we will be using the notation
D_{n}
or
d_{n}
and not refer to the geodesic or resistance metrics specifically. Sometimes, we will drop the
n
and just write
D
when the underlying graph is clear from the context.

3.1.
Convergence of the path metric

Path metric convergence is the most straightforward and relies only on Camia and Newman’s percolation cluster interface convergence as stated in Theorem
2.4
.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
. Define

(3.1)

{}d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n}:=\inf_{\gamma\in P_{Q,n}(x,y)}\diam(\gamma),

where
P_{Q,n}(x,y)
is the set of all admissible (i.e., containing only all open or all closed vertices) paths from
x
to
y
inside
\Lambda_{Q,n}
. Note that if
P_{Q,n}(x,y)
is empty, then
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n}(x,y)=\infty
. For a collection of
\CLE_{6}
-loops
\Gamma_{Q}
in
Q
, define
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q}
analogously (recall (
2.17
)). We prove GHf convergence of the path metric (and the projection map). We note that although the definition of the path metric seems a bit artificial, it is useful as a reference metric in the proof of the tightness of the geodesic and the resistance metrics. In particular, given the Proposition
3.1
below, it will suffice to bound
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}_{n}
and
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{n}
in terms of
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}
.

Proposition 3.1
.

There exists a coupling between
\Gamma_{n}
and
\Gamma
such that the following holds almost surely. For each
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
, the sequence
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},\pi_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
converges to
(\Lambda_{Q},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q},\pi)
in the
\GHf
distance.

Recall from Section
2.1
that convergence of
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},\pi_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
in
\GHf
means that the individual clusters converge in
\GHf
.

In Section
2.4.1
, we described two different encodings of percolation: by interface loops and by quad crossings. A different way to encode a percolation configuration is by considering the collection of open paths in
[0,1]^{2}
, equipped with the topology
\Delta_{\cur}
. It was shown by Aizenman and Burchard in
[
1
]
that the collection of open percolation paths is tight with respect to
\Delta_{\cur}
. Proposition
3.1
