then follows from uniqueness of the subsequential limits. This is widely believed to follow from convergence of the interface loops, but the authors are not aware of any existing proof in the literature. Therefore, we provide a short proof of Proposition
3.1
. We first show that the sequence above is uniformly totally bounded with arbitrarily large probability, which implies that it is tight. This follows from the fact that each Euclidean ball in
[0,1]^{2}
cannot have too many disjoint open arms coming out of it. Thus, every Euclidean ball has a bounded number of points in it that are connected but only by a path of large diameter. This implies that we can cover each open cluster by a bounded number of
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}
-balls. To prove convergence of the sequence, we then show that the subsequential limiting path distance between two points
x
and
y
equals the
\CLE_{6}
path distance
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}(x,y)
. This is a consequence of the interface loop convergence proved by Camia and Newman as stated in Section
2.4.1
, but requires some precision. The full proof is given in Section
4
.

3.2.
Tightness of the geodesic and resistance metrics

We now focus our attention on proving convergence of the geodesic and resistance metrics. We first show that they are tight in an appropriate topology.

We will define a sequence of positive real numbers
{\mathfrak{q}}^{{\mathrm{geo}}}_{n}
resp.
{\mathfrak{q}}^{{\mathrm{geo}}}_{n}
. The precise definition is given in Sections
3.2.1
and
3.2.4
below; it is the
(1-p)
th quantile of an appropriate random variable defined in terms of the metric
D^{{\mathrm{geo}}}_{n}
resp.
D^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{n}
for a suitable
p\in(0,1)
.

Furthermore, for each
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
, we let
D_{Q,n}
denote either the geodesic or resistance metric on percolation on
\Lambda_{Q,n}
, where we set
D_{Q,n}(x,y)=\infty
if
x
and
y
are not in the same cluster. We define

(3.2)

d_{Q,n}:={\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{-1}D_{Q,n}

where
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}
denotes either
{\mathfrak{q}}^{{\mathrm{geo}}}_{n}
or
{\mathfrak{q}}^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{n}
.

We now specify the topology with respect to which we prove tightness. Let
Y=[0,1]^{2}\times[0,1]^{2}\times[0,\infty]
, let
d_{Y}
be the product metric on
Y
, and consider the functions
f_{Q,n}\colon\Lambda^{2}_{Q,n}\to Y
given by
f_{Q,n}(x,y)=(\pi_{n}(x),\pi_{n}(y),d_{Q,n}(x,y))
. To ease the notation, will view
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n})
as the tuple
(\Lambda^{2}_{Q,n},(d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n})^{2},f_{Q,n})
which lives in the space of compact metric spaces equipped with a continuous function. The majority of the paper will be devoted to proving tightness of the sequence
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
in the GHf topology. As in Section
2.1
, we mean by this the convergence of
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n})_{m\in\mathbf{N}}
in the product of the GHf topologies as
n\to\infty
.

Proposition 3.2
.

For each
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
, the sequence
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
is tight with respect to the GHf distance. Each subsequential limit is of the form
(\Lambda_{Q},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q},d_{Q},\pi)
, where
d_{Q}
is an almost surely
\eta
-Hölder continuous (pseudo-)metric with respect to
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q}
for some
\eta>0
.

Remark 3.3
.

It is shown in
[
67
,
66
]
that the metrics
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
resp.
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
are locally Hölder continuous for any exponent
\eta<\beta^{\mathrm{geo}}
resp.
\eta<\beta^{\mathrm{res}}
where the exponents satisfy
\beta^{\mathrm{geo}}\geq 1
,
\beta^{\mathrm{res}}\geq 3/4
. We will prove that it is in fact globally
\eta
-Hölder continuous (also near the boundary of
Q
) for any
\eta<5/8
.

From this, we obtain subsequential GHf convergence of
d_{Q,n}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

For each
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
and each subsequence of
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
, there exists a further subsequence that converges weakly with respect to the GHf distance.

Proposition
3.4
follows almost immediately from Proposition
3.2
. In the proof, we show in particular that the GHf convergence of
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},d_{Q,n})
implies GH convergence of
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d_{Q,n})
.

The proof of Proposition
3.2
has four main steps. To prove Proposition
3.2
, we want to show that
d_{n}
is Hölder continuous with respect to
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}
with a constant and exponent that is uniform in
n
(stated below in Proposition
3.11
). Then, since
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}
converges, we can conclude tightness of
d_{n}
.

The proof of the Hölder estimate follows a chaining argument. For each annulus
A(z,r,2r)\subseteq[0,1]^{2}
we will prove an upper bound of the type

(3.3)

\mathbf{P}(d_{n}(v_{i},v_{j})>r^{\eta})=O(r^{\alpha})

where
v_{i},v_{j}
are some pivotal points on open annulus crossings (see Section
3.2.2
). From this, we deduce a bound for
d_{n}(x,y)
in terms of
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}(x,y)
as follows. Let
\gamma
be a path between
x
and
y
with smallest diameter. We cover the path
\gamma
with annuli of sizes approximately proportional to their respective distances to
x
and
y
(with the largest one having size approximately comparable to
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}(x,y)
). The path
\gamma
then forms a series of crossings of these annuli. By applying the bound (
3.3
) and then ‘stringing together’ the distances across all annuli we obtain a bound of the type
d_{n}(x,y)\lesssim d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}(x,y)^{\eta}
on an event with large probability. This requires taking a union bound of (
3.3
) for all annuli covering the space
[0,1]^{2}
, and therefore we need to prove (
3.3
) for a sufficiently large exponent
\alpha
. In fact, we will prove that it has superpolynomial tails.

The first step in the proof of (
3.3
) is to prove an
a priori estimate
. Instead of bounding the distance between connected annulus crossings, we upper bound the distance between two points in a toy version of the problem. For the a priori bound, we obtain polynomial concentration on this distance. See Section
3.2.1
for a sketch of this step. In that section, we also explain how the normalizing constants
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}
are defined.
Informally,
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}
should be thought of as the median distance between two points in the same cluster at Euclidean distance of order
n
. The next step consists of turning this a priori bound into a polynomial right tail bound for the distance between two crossings of an annulus. See Section
3.2.2
for a sketch. We then show that any polynomial bound with some exponent
\alpha
, say, can be bootstrapped into a bound with exponent
\alpha^{\prime}>\alpha
. Thus, we obtain a bound that is stronger than any polynomial. We then also turn the a priori bound in the toy model into a bound on the distance between connected annulus crossings. This step is sketched in Section
3.2.3
. The final step is given by the path covering procedure to obtain our Hölder bound, see Section
3.2.4
. The corresponding proofs are contained in Sections
5.1
–
7.1
. The final proofs of Propositions
3.2
and
3.4
are given in Section
7.2
.

3.2.1.
A priori estimate

In this section, we define the auxiliary sequence
(\mathbf{q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
that depends on a parameter
p\in(0,1)
. The normalizing constant
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}
will be defined as
\mathbf{q}_{n}(p)
for a particular choice
p\in(0,1)
, which we will indicate as
\mathfrak{p}
. We also give a sketch of the first step of the proof of Proposition
3.2
, which we call the
a priori estimate
.

We first define the auxiliary normalizing constants
\mathbf{q}_{n}
as follows. Let
c_{1}^{(0)},\ldots,c_{4}^{(0)}
denote the midpoints of the sides of the box
[0,1]^{2}
, numbered clockwise starting from the top side. Let
\delta=1/10000
and define
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}(\Lambda_{n})
to be the event that the following events occur for percolation on
\Lambda_{n}
:

(i)

there exist 3 alternating left-to-right crossings such that the top and bottom crossings are closed and the middle crossing is open;

(ii)

the closed clusters of the top and bottom crossings come within (graph) distance 2 of each other;

(iii)

the closed cluster of the top crossing hits the top side within distance
\delta
of
c_{1}^{(0)}
and does not come within distance
2\delta
of
c_{2}^{(0)},c_{3}^{(0)},c_{4}^{(0)}
;

(iv)

the closed cluster of the bottom crossing hits the bottom side within distance
\delta
of
c_{3}^{(0)}
and does not come within distance
2\delta
of
c_{1}^{(0)},c_{2}^{(0)},c_{4}^{(0)}
;

(v)

the open cluster of the middle crossing hits the left and right sides within distance
\delta
of
c_{2}^{(0)}
and
c_{4}^{(0)}
respectively and does not come within distance
2\delta
of
c_{1}^{(0)}
and
c_{3}^{(0)}

We refer to Figure
3.1
for an illustration of this event. Note that the probability of
\mathcal{E}(\Lambda_{n})
is uniformly bounded away from
0
and
1
due to the RSW theorem (see Lemma
6
).

Figure 3.1.
An illustration of the event
\mathcal{E}
. The blue curve represents an open crossing from left to right. The red curves represent two closed left-to-right crossings that come within distance
2
but do not intersect.

Let
u=u\!\left(\Lambda_{n}\right)
and
v=v\!\left(\Lambda_{n}\right)
be the left-most and right-most points that neighbor both the top and bottom closed clusters of
\Lambda_{n}
. Define
X_{\Lambda_{n}}=D(u,v)
to be the distance between these points if they exist. Note that the items (
iii
)–(
v
) in the definition of the event
\mathcal{E}(\Lambda_{n})
imply that the distance
D(u,v)
does not depend on the percolation configuration within the
2\delta
-neighborhoods of
c_{1}^{(0)},c_{2}^{(0)},c_{3}^{(0)},c_{4}^{(0)}
. This will be useful later in Lemma
5.4
.
More generally, for any box
B\in\mathcal{B}^{R}_{k,n}
(recall Section
2.1
), we define the event
\mathcal{E}(B)
and random variable
X_{B}
analogously.

We let
X_{n}
be a random variable whose law is given by
X_{\Lambda_{n}}
conditional on the event
\mathcal{E}(\Lambda_{n})
. Let
p\in(0,1/2]
and let

(3.4)

\mathbf{q}_{n}=\mathbf{q}_{n}(p)=\inf\{x:\mathbf{P}(X_{n}\leq x)\geq 1-p\}

be the
(1-p)
-th quantile of
X_{n}
. We have
\mathbf{P}(X_{n}>\mathbf{q}_{n})\leq p
by construction.

We define normalizing constant
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}
as

(3.5)

{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}:=\mathbf{q}_{n}(\mathfrak{p}),

where
\mathfrak{p}\in(0,1)
is some small number specified in Section
3.2.4
.

So a priori, we prove Theorem
1.1
when
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}
is chosen to be a sufficiently large quantile of
X_{n}
.
A posteriori in the proof of Corollary
1.2
, we will see that in fact the scaling limit exists for all
p
, and choosing a different value for
p
only results in a deterministic factor in the limit.

Throughout the rest of the paper, we will often implicitly take
p
small enough that the desired results are true, and omit the dependence of
\mathbf{q}_{n}
on
p
. Recall that
\kappa
is defined to be the unique integer such that
R^{\kappa-1}<n\leq R^{\kappa}
. The immediate goal is to show the following.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. There exist
R_{0}=R_{0}(\varepsilon)
and
C=C(\varepsilon)
such that for each
R\geq R_{0}
there exists
p_{0}=p_{0}(\varepsilon,R)
such that for all
p<p_{0}
,
k\geq 1
, and
n>R^{k}
,

(3.6)

\mathbf{P}\!\left(X_{R^{\kappa-k}}>\mathbf{q}_{n}\right)\leq CR^{-\!\left(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon\right)k}.

For the proof, we divide
[0,1]^{2}
into smaller
R
-adic boxes in
\mathcal{B}^{R}_{k,n}
with
R^{\kappa-k}\times R^{\kappa-k}
vertices for some
k\geq 1
. We roughly say a box
B
is
important
if there are closed arms from the top and bottom sides of
B
to the centers of the top and bottom sides of
[0,1]^{2}
and open arms from the left and right sides of
B
to the centers of left and right sides of
[0,1]^{2}
. It is
bad
if inside
B
, the event
\mathcal{E}(B)
happens, the open and closed arms inside
B
defined by the event
\mathcal{E}(B)
link up with the open and closed arms outside that go to the sides of
[0,1]^{2}
, and furthermore that
X_{B}>\mathbf{q}_{n}
. Note that
X_{R^{\kappa-k}}
is distributed as
X_{B}
conditional on
\mathcal{E}(B)
. Points (
iii
)–(
v
) in the definition of the event
\mathcal{E}
guarantee that the clusters inside
B
link up with the arms outside of
B
with probability bounded away from 0. They also guarantee that
u
and
v
always have 4 long arms, which will be useful in the annulus covering parts of the paper. Combining all this, we show

(3.7)

\mathbf{P}(\text{$B$ is bad})\asymp\mathbf{P}(\text{$B$ is important})\mathbf{P}(X_{R^{\kappa-k}}>\mathbf{q}_{n}).

Furthermore, if a box
B
is bad, then
\mathcal{E}(\Lambda_{n})
occurs and
X_{\Lambda_{n}}>\mathbf{q}_{n}
. Thus, if the probability that there is at least one bad box is large, then the probability that
X_{n}>\mathbf{q}_{n}
must be large, but this probability is at most
p
by definition of
\mathbf{q}_{n}
. So in order to bound
\mathbf{P}(X_{R^{\kappa-k}}>\mathbf{q}_{n})
, we want to lower bound the number of important boxes.

Since the 4-arm exponent for planar critical percolation is
\frac{5}{4}
(see also Section
2.3
), the probability that a box at level
k
is important is roughly
R^{-\frac{5}{4}k}
. So the expected number of important boxes is
R^{2k}R^{-\frac{5}{4}k}=R^{\frac{3}{4}k}
. Since the probability that a box is bad is bounded from above by
p
, it follows that
\mathbf{P}(X_{R^{\kappa-k}}>\mathbf{q}_{n})\lesssim R^{-\frac{3}{4}k}
. To make this argument precise, we use a second moment method to estimate the number of important/bad boxes. We bound the second moment by a multiscale argument decorrelating the events that two boxes that are far away are both important.

3.2.2.
Annulus crossing bounds

We now want to transform the right tail bound on
X_{n}
into a more useful bound on the probability that the distance between two crossings of the annulus that are connected exceeds
\mathbf{q}_{n}
. Let
\varepsilon>0
and set
\alpha=\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon
. Let
R=R(\varepsilon)>8
and
p=p(\varepsilon,R)
be as in Proposition
3.5
. The goal is to prove the following result.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
f\in\mathbf{N}
and consider the annulus
A(0;2n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f},4n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f})
. Let
s<1
and define
G=G(s,f)
to be the event that for each pair of open annulus crossings that are that are connected inside
B(0,2n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f})
, the geodesic resp. resistance distance between them within
B(0,4n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f})
is at most
s\mathbf{q}_{n}
. Then for every
\zeta,p^{\prime}>0
, there exists
C=C(\zeta,p^{\prime})>0
independent of
f,s
and some event
F
depending only on the sites in
A(0;n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f},n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f+1})
such that
\mathbf{P}(F)>1-p^{\prime}
uniformly in
n,f
and for all
R
sufficiently large depending on
\zeta
, and
p
small enough depending on
R
,

(3.8)

\mathbf{P}(G^{c}\mid F)\leq Cs^{-(\frac{3}{4}+\zeta)\alpha}R^{-\alpha f}.

In fact, we prove something slightly stronger than the above. See Proposition
5.17
for a precise statement of the desired result. The idea behind the proof is as follows. Assume first that there are only two open crossings
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
of the annulus
A(0;2n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f},4n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f})
. Then there also exist two closed paths
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
that touch at
v_{1}\in\eta_{1}
and
v_{2}\in\eta_{2}
. So
\{v_{1}\}\cup\phi_{1}\cup\{v_{2}\}\cup\phi_{2}
form a loop inside
A(0;2n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f},4n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f})
surrounding
B(0;2n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f})
. Consider the event
E
that
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
are connected with open paths to the centers of the left- and right-hand sides of
B(0,\frac{1}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f+1})
respectively, and
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
are connected with closed paths to the top and bottom sides of
B(0,\frac{1}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f+1})
respectively. Then
E
implies that the event
\mathcal{E}(B(0,\frac{1}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f+1}))
occurs. Furthermore,
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are pivotal sites for global left-to-right crossing of
B(0,\frac{1}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f+1})
. So
X_{B(0,\frac{1}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f+1})}\geq D(v_{i},v_{j})
on
E
. Here
D(v_{i},v_{j})
refers to the (geodesic or resistance) distance inside the region enclosed by the loop
\{v_{1}\}\cup\phi_{1}\cup\{v_{2}\}\cup\phi_{2}
.

What if
E
does not occur? Then with high probability,
\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
are still almost connected to the sides of
B(0,\frac{1}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-f+1})
by bounded sequences of touching, but not intersecting, clusters. If we resample the percolation configuration in small boxes around these touch-points, then with positive probability all touching loops become intersecting and the event
E
occurs. Furthermore, we can show that if the configuration is nice (this is the event
F
), then the successful resampling probability is uniformly bounded away from
0
, and further the distance
D(v_{i},v_{j})
remains unchanged. Combining these two facts, we obtain

(3.9)

\mathbf{P}(D(v_{1},v_{2})>\mathbf{q}_{n},F)\lesssim\mathbf{P}(D(v_{1},v_{2})>\mathbf{q}_{n},F,E)\lesssim\mathbf{P}(X_{R^{\kappa-f+1}}>\mathbf{q}_{n})\lesssim R^{-\alpha f},

where the last inequality is precisely the a priori bound from Proposition
3.5
.

What if we have
m>2
open crossings? Then there are points
v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}
and closed paths
\phi_{1},\phi_{m}
such that
\{v_{1}\}\cup\phi_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\{v_{m}\}\cup\phi_{m}
form a loop in the annulus. If we resample in small boxes around the points
v_{3},\ldots,v_{m}
, then the paths
\phi_{3},\ldots,\phi_{m}
link up with probability bounded away from 0 like before and we end up with only two open crossings. Again, we can define
F
such that this resampling procedure does not affect
D(v_{i},v_{j})
on
F
. We can then proceeds as before.

The addition of the extra factor
s<1
will be important in later parts of the paper where we will sum the distances across multiple annuli. We first prove the upper bound
\mathbf{P}(X_{R^{\kappa-k}}>s\mathbf{q}_{n})\lesssim s^{-(\frac{3}{4}+\zeta)\alpha}R^{-\alpha k}
by showing that
\mathbf{q}_{R^{-k}n}\lesssim R^{-(3/4-\varepsilon)k}\mathbf{q}_{n}
. The rest of the proof then follows as before.

3.2.3.
Superpolynomial concentration

Having established the polynomial upper bound on the right tails of
X_{n}
with some exponent
\alpha
that is almost
\frac{3}{4}
, we then show that we can bootstrap this estimate and obtain concentration with some exponent
\alpha+\beta
. From this, we obtain a superpolynomial upper bound. More precisely, we prove the following. Recall that
\mathbf{q}_{n}=\mathbf{q}_{n}(p)
depends implicitly on
p
.

Proposition 3.7
.

There exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
\beta>0
,
R_{0}>0
, and a function
p_{0}(R)
such that the following holds for each
R\geq R_{0}
and
p<p_{0}(R)
. Assume that
for some
\alpha>\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon_{0}
, there exists
C_{0}=C_{0}(\alpha,R,p)
such that for all
k\in\mathbf{N}
and
n>R^{k}
, we have

(3.10)

\mathbf{P}(X_{R^{\kappa-k}}>\mathbf{q}_{n})\leq C_{0}R^{-\alpha k}.

Then
there exists
C_{1}=C_{1}(\alpha,R,p)
such that for all
k\in\mathbf{N}
and
n>R^{k}
, we have

(3.11)

\mathbf{P}(X_{R^{\kappa-k}}>\mathbf{q}_{n})\leq C_{1}R^{-(\alpha+\beta)k}.

Note that
\beta
does not depend on
\alpha
. Thus, we immediately obtain a superpolynomial right tail bound on the annulus crossing distance.

Corollary 3.8
.

The statement in equation (
3.10
) and hence Proposition
3.6
(and Proposition
5.17
) hold for any exponent
\alpha>0
, upon choosing some
R
large enough and
p=p(R)
small enough, independently of
\alpha
.

Throughout the proof of Proposition
3.7
, we fix some
\alpha>\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon_{0}
and take
\mathbf{q}_{n}=\mathbf{q}_{n}(p)
, where we suppose that
R
is sufficiently large and
p
sufficiently small such that Proposition
3.5
holds for
\varepsilon_{0}
. Furthermore, at certain steps of the proof, we will need to further increase
R
(and thus also further decrease
p
depending on
R
) such that certain estimates hold. Crucially, the necessary value of
R
does not depend on
\alpha
, so the bootstrapping procedure is valid for a fixed large
R
and small
p
.

We now give a sketch of the proof. Let
k\in\mathbf{N}
and consider percolation on
\Lambda_{n}
restricted to some box
B^{(k)}\in\mathcal{B}^{R}_{k,n}
. The goal is to bound
\mathbf{P}(X_{R^{\kappa-k}}>\mathbf{q}_{n})\lesssim\mathbf{P}(X_{B^{(k)}}>\mathbf{q}_{n},\mathcal{E}(B^{(k)}))
. We say a site
x
is
pivotal
if every open path from the left side of
B^{(k)}
to the right side passes through
x
. We will also call them
pinch points
. Assume the event
\mathcal{E}(B^{(k)})
holds, so that there exist pivotal sites. Let
u,v
be the left- and rightmost pivotal points and let
(x_{0}=u,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,v)
be the sequence of pivotal points from left to right. Write
X_{x_{i},x_{j}}=D(x_{i},x_{j})
to be the (geodesic or effective resistance) distance between
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
. In particular, on the event
\mathcal{E}(B^{(k)})
, we have
X_{B^{(k)}}=X_{u,v}
. Define
\tau_{0}=0
and for
\ell\geq 1
define

(3.12)

\tau_{\ell}=\min\{i\geq\tau_{\ell-1}\colon X_{x_{\tau_{\ell-1},x_{i}}}>\mathbf{q}_{n}\}.

If the set above is empty, set
\tau_{\ell}=\infty
. Let

(3.13)

N=\min\{\ell\geq 1\colon\tau_{\ell}=\infty\}.

Then, because the
x_{i}
are pivotal sites, we know that
D(u,v)=D(x_{\tau_{0}},x_{\tau_{1}})+D(x_{\tau_{1}},x_{\tau_{2}})+\cdots+D(x_{\tau_{N}-1},v)
by the series law (property (
A
) of the metric). In particular,

(3.14)

X_{u,v}\leq N\mathbf{q}_{n}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N-1}X_{{\tau_{\ell}-1},\tau_{\ell}}.

We bound both terms separately. We first show the following bound on the second term.

Proposition 3.9
.

There exist
\beta>0
and a constant
C(R,\alpha)>0
such that

(3.15)

\begin{split}\mathbf{P}\!\left(\exists i\colon X_{x_{i},x_{i+1}}>\mathbf{q}_{n}\right)\leq C(R,\alpha)\cdot R^{-(\alpha+\beta)k},\end{split}

for all
n>R^{k}
.

The intuition behind the proof is as follows. In between two consecutive pivotals
x_{i}
and
x_{i+1}
, there is a
bubble
bounded by two disjoint open interface paths
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
. Assume the bubble is roughly of size
R^{-d}
for
d\geq k
. If we cover the bubble with annuli of size a bit smaller than
R^{-d}
, then
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
form crossings of a string of annuli. For an annulus of size
R^{-f}
, the probability of the (geodesic resp. resistance) distance between two crossings being large (a bit less than
\mathbf{q}_{n}
) is just a bit more than
R^{-\alpha f}
. We say such an annulus is bad. So the probability of there being two disjoint bad annuli is just a bit more than
R^{-2\alpha f}
. This already gives us an exponent strictly larger than
\alpha
, so we may assume without loss of generality that there is at most one bad annulus. If, say,
\gamma_{1}
does not pass through this annulus, then
\gamma_{1}
can be covered by annulus crossings that all have small distance to each other (see Figure
6.1
), and we can ‘string together’ short distances between these crossings to conclude that
D(x_{i},x_{i+1})\leq\mathbf{q}_{n}
by choosing the correct exponents. We refer to the proof of Lemma
6.1
for an explanation of this stringing procedure. If both
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
pass through the bad annulus, then we have a 5-arm event occurring for that annulus. Multiplying the probability that an annulus is bad and the probability of the 5-arm event, we obtain a bound of
R^{-(\alpha+\beta^{\prime})f}
for some
\beta^{\prime}>0
. Summing over all annuli of level
f
slightly larger than
d
, we obtain that the probability that
X_{x_{i},x_{i+1}}>\mathbf{q}_{n}
for a bubble of size
d
is at most
R^{-(\alpha+\beta)d}
for some
\beta>0
. By a 4-arm exponent calculation, it can be shown that the number of bubbles of size
R^{-d}
is of order
R^{\frac{3}{4}d}
. Summing over all such bubbles and using that
\alpha+\beta>\frac{3}{4}
when
\alpha
is chosen close enough to
\frac{3}{4}
, we obtain the desired bound. Here we need that
\alpha
can be chosen arbitrarily close to
\frac{3}{4}
.

Having shown the above, we obtain a bound of
X_{u,v}\leq N\mathbf{q}_{n}+N\mathbf{q}_{n}=2N\mathbf{q}_{n}
in (
3.14
) with probability at least
1-O(R^{-(\alpha+\beta)k})
. The following proposition bounds
N
by
2
with probability at least
1-O(R^{-(\alpha+\beta)k})
.

Proposition 3.10
.

There exists
\beta>0
and
C=C(\alpha,R)
such that for all
k
and
n>R^{k}
,

\mathbf{P}(\tau_{2}<\infty)\leq CR^{-(\alpha+\beta)k}.

Note that if the
\tau_{i}-\tau_{i-1}
were i.i.d., then we would have
\mathbf{P}(\tau_{2}<\infty)=\mathbf{P}(\tau_{1}<\infty)^{2}\lesssim R^{-2\alpha k}
. We show that this is almost true, at least with some exponent that is strictly larger than
\alpha
. At time
\tau_{1}
, we can explore the domain that is enclosed by the top and bottom touching clusters up until
x:=x_{\tau_{1}}
. We call the complement of that domain
C
. We consider the
Whitney decomposition
of
C
, which is a covering of
C
with boxes such that the size of a Whitney box is proportional to its distance to the explored domain. We will first show that there exists a global event
H
that happens with very high probability, and on the event
H
it will be possible to find an open path
\gamma
from
x
to
v
that can be covered by a small number of
R
-adic Whitney boxes of each level
i\geq k+1
. The potential obstruction is that if
\gamma
comes close to the boundary of
C
, then the number of Whitney boxes that cover
\gamma
is large, since the Whitney boxes close to the boundary of
C
are small. However, the boxes close to the boundary of
C
are also close to the explored bubbles, which leads to these Whitney boxes having 6 so-called
semi-arms
. The event
H
bounds the number of Whitney boxes with 6 semi-arms. Following this, we will show that with high probability all of these boxes are good, i.e., open crossings of the boxes have short geodesic resp. resistance distance. This is possible since the number of boxes in the covering of
\gamma
is not too big. Finally, we will string together short crossings across each one of these boxes to obtain an upper bound on the remaining distance
X_{x,v}
. There is a technical issue in showing that the covering boxes are good. To bound the distance between annulus crossings for annuli that cover the path from
x
to
v
, we would like to use the annulus crossing bound from Proposition
3.6

conditionally on
\{\tau_{1}<\infty\}
(since this is what improves the exponent from
\alpha
to
\alpha+\beta
). However, as we explain in Section
6.2
, the conditioning on
\mathcal{E}(B^{(k)})\cap\{\tau_{1}<\infty\}
induces a bias, and the percolation in the domain
C
is no longer i.i.d. Bernoulli. Thus, we also need to prove some results to deal with decorrelating the events
F,G
from Proposition
3.6
for an annulus in
C
and the event
\mathcal{E}(B^{(k)})\cap\{\tau_{1}<\infty\}
.

3.2.4.
Hölder continuity

The last technical result we need for proving tightness is Hölder continuity of the geodesic/resistance metrics on percolation with respect to the path metric with a constant that is uniform over
n
.

Proposition 3.11
.

For all
\eta<\frac{5}{8}
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
p=p(\eta)
and
C=C(\eta,\varepsilon)>0
such that for all
n
,

(3.16)

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\forall x,y\in\Lambda_{n}\colon\,\mathbf{q}_{n}^{-1}D_{n}(x,y)\leq Cd^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}(x,y)^{\eta}\right)>1-\varepsilon.

We are now ready to define
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}
. To prove Theorem
1.1
, we will need that
d_{n}={\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{-1}D_{n}
is uniformly Hölder continuous with some Hölder exponent. Since the precise Hölder exponent is not important, we simply choose
\eta=1/4
and set
\mathfrak{p}:=p(1/4)
and
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}=\mathbf{q}_{n}(\mathfrak{p})
.

The proof of Proposition
3.11
uses many ingredients from previous proofs in this paper. To bound
D_{n}(x,y)
, we consider again a Whitney decomposition, this time of the set
\Lambda_{n}\setminus\{x,y\}
. Let
\gamma
be a path from
x
to
y
with minimal diameter. Again, we would like to show that we can cover
\gamma
in not too many Whitney boxes and then use the same stringing argument to conclude that
D_{n}(x,y)
is small. However, there is a technical issue: we can only apply Proposition
3.6
to annuli that are fully contained inside
\Lambda_{n}
. So the stringing argument will not work if our Whitney boxes covering
\gamma
are too close to the boundary of
\Lambda_{n}
. To deal with this, we take the size of the Whitney boxes to be proportional to the distance to
\partial\Lambda_{n}\cup\{x,y\}
.
This is expensive: if
\gamma
stays too close to the boundary then we need too many boxes, and this will result in a bound that is too weak. However, unless there is a closed blocking path coming close to the boundary, we can reroute
\gamma
to be away from the boundary. If there is such a blocking path, then the Whitney box must have 3 long arms. We can bound the number of such boxes by a 3-arm half-plane exponent calculation.

Let us now explain how to get the exponent
\frac{5}{8}
. Let
k_{0}=\lfloor-\log_{R}d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}(x,y)\rfloor
. If the path
\gamma
does not come near the boundary, the number of Whitney boxes covering
\gamma
at each level
k
would be bounded. From (
3.8
), we get that with high enough probability, the distance between two crossings of an annulus at
R
-adic level
k
is at most
R^{-(\frac{3}{4}-o(1))k}\mathbf{q}_{n}
. Since the number of such good annuli at level
k
is bounded for each level, we would obtain

(3.17)

d_{n}(x,y)\lesssim\sum_{k\geq k_{0}}[\textrm{$\#$ of Whitney boxes at level $k$}]\cdot R^{-(\frac{3}{4}-o(1))k}\lesssim R^{-(\frac{3}{4}-o(1))k_{0}}\approx d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}(x,y)^{\frac{3}{4}-o(1)}.

In the case
\gamma
comes close to the boundary of
\Lambda_{n}
, we cannot guarantee that the number of Whitney boxes of each level
k
is bounded, but since they are all contained in a box of Euclidean size
R^{-k_{0}}
, their number (i.e. boxes of size
R^{-k}
and Euclidean distance to the boundary proportional to
R^{-k}
) is trivially bounded by
R^{k-k_{0}}
. This is not enough to sum up the distance in (
3.17
), but as we mentioned before, if
\gamma
is forced to go through a small Whitney box near the boundary, then that box has
3
long arms. By an arm exponent calculation, we show that with high probability, there are at most
R^{k/3}
such Whitney boxes. Therefore, if we estimate the number of Whitney boxes by
R^{k-k_{0}}
for
k\leq\frac{3}{2}k_{0}
, say, and by
R^{k/3}
for
k>\frac{3}{2}k_{0}
, then we obtain in (
3.3
) the estimate
d_{n}(x,y)\lesssim R^{-\frac{5}{8}k_{0}}\approx d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}(x,y)^{\frac{5}{8}}
.

This estimate is not optimal, but for the results in this paper any exponent is good enough, so we made no effort to push the exponent any further.

3.3.
Identification of the limit

Let
({\mathfrak{q}}_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
be as defined in Sections
3.2.1
and
3.2.4
. By Propositions
3.2
and
3.4
, for every subsequence
I\subset\mathbf{N}
, we can choose a further subsequence
J\subset I\subset\mathbf{N}
such that
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n})
converge weakly in the GHf topology along
J
for all
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
.

We denote the limiting metric of
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}_{Q,n}
resp.
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{Q,n}
by
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}_{Q}
resp.
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{Q}
. If
Q=(0,1)^{2}
, we simply write
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}=d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}_{Q}
resp.
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}=d^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{Q}
. The goal of this section is to show that
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
and
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
are indeed the (unique up to multiplicative constants) conformally covariant geodesic and resistance metrics respectively on the clusters of
\Lambda
, as previously introduced in Section
1.3
.

In the following, we let
d=d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
or
d=d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
. Note that for both the geodesic and resistance metrics, we have
d_{Q}\leq d_{Q^{\prime}}
on
\Lambda_{Q^{\prime}}
if
Q^{\prime}\subset Q
(since we have
d_{Q,n}\leq d_{Q^{\prime},n}
by construction, and the inequality is preserved in the GHf limit by
[
4
, Lemma A.10]
). To identify the limit with the geodesic CLE
6
metric resp. the CLE
6
resistance metric, we verify the conditions in Section
2.5
. For this, we consider the restriction of
d
to the cluster
\mathcal{X}
(recall the definition of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathfrak{C}
from Section
2.5
) and define the collection of metrics
(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
as follows:

(3.18)

\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y)=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\Supset V\\
Q\in\mathcal{Q}\end{subarray}}d_{Q}(x,y),\quad x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}.

Upon verifying that the collection of metrics
(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
satisfy the definition in Section
2.5
, we obtain from Theorem
2.14
resp.
2.15
that the restriction of
d
to the cluster
\mathcal{X}
is (a multiple of) the geodesic CLE
6
metric resp. the CLE
6
resistance metric, and that the weak convergence of
(\mathcal{X}_{n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n})
holds for the full sequence. It follows by the local absolute continuity (see
[
67
, Section 7.2]
) that the global metric
d
(defined on all clusters) agrees with (a global multiple of) the geodesic CLE
6
metric resp. the CLE
6
resistance metric.

Theorem 3.12
.

The collection
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
as defined in (
3.18
) defines a
\CLE_{6}
metric. If
d=d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
, then
d
is indeed a geodesic
\CLE_{6}
-metric. Furthermore, if
d=d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
, then
d
is a resistance
\CLE_{6}
-metric.

For the proof of Theorem
3.12
, we show that
(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
satisfies all of the axioms of a
\CLE_{6}
metric. This is done in Section
8
. Analogues of the axioms are clearly true for the finite metrics
d_{Q,n}
, so it just remains to show that they hold in the GHf limit.
The continuity of
\mathfrak{d}_{V}
with respect to
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{\overline{V}}
follows immediately from Proposition
3.4
. The monotonicity follows immediately from (
3.18
). Compatibility is stated in Proposition
8.1
. The series and generalized parallel laws are stated in Proposition
8.2
and
8.3
. The Markovian Property is stated in Proposition
8.4
. Translation invariance is stated in Proposition
8.9
. This shows that
(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
is a
\CLE_{6}
-metric. If
d=d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
, it is shown in Section
8.6
that
d
is a geodesic
\CLE_{6}
-metric. If
d=d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
, it is shown in Section
8.7
that
d
is a resistance
\CLE_{6}
-metric.

We obtain convergence of the geodesic and resistance metrics to
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
and
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
as a corollary.

Corollary 3.13
.

There exist true metrics
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
and
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
on all clusters
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}
such that

(3.19)

\begin{split}(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n},d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}_{n},\pi_{n})&\rightarrow(\mathcal{X}^{(m)},d^{{\mathrm{geo}}},\pi),\\
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n},d^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{n},\pi_{n})&\rightarrow(\mathcal{X}^{(m)},d^{{\mathrm{res}}},\pi)\\
\end{split}

weakly with respect to
\Delta_{\GHf}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

For the cluster
\mathcal{X}
, this corollary follows almost immediately from Theorems
2.14
and
2.15
, which imply that all subsequential limits are multiples of each other. It remains to show that the subsequential limiting metric
d
is non-trivial and that all subsequential limits are actually equal and not just multiples. We can then carry over convergence for
\mathcal{X}
over to all clusters
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}
by a local absolute continuity argument. See Section
8.8
for a proof of Corollary
3.13
.

3.4.
Measure and projection convergence

Having proved Corollary
3.13
, the final ingredient to obtain Theorem
1.1
is the convergence of the counting measure on percolation clusters. This was essentially proved by Garban, Pete, and Schramm in
[
39
]
as stated in Section
2.4.2
. However, their result concerns the counting measure for an annulus defined in (
2.10
). Thus, we first need to express the counting measure on
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n}
in terms of counting measures for annuli. We are then able to transfer GHf convergence and measure convergence to the desired GHPf convergence. In order to show that the limiting measure equals the CLE
6
gasket measure
\mu
, we show that it also satisfies the characterization from Proposition
2.11
by making use of the analogous property of the annulus measure in Proposition
2.9
. This is stated as Proposition
9.5
. See Section
9
for the details.

3.5.
Random walk convergence

The proof of Theorem
1.5
is now straightforward, and so we give it here.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Let
X
be the Hunt process associated with
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)},d^{{\mathrm{res}}},\mu^{(m)},\rho)
, as described in Section
2.6
. Since
d^{{\mathrm{res}}},\mu^{(m)}
are measurable with respect to the
\Gamma
, we have that
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n},d^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{n},\mu^{(m)}_{n},\pi_{n},\rho_{n})
converges in law to
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)},d^{{\mathrm{res}}},\mu^{(m)},\pi,\rho)
, so that we can couple them such that the sequence converges in
\rGHPf
almost surely. The conclusion of Theorem
1.5
then follows from Theorem
2.16
.
∎

3.6.
Existence of distance, resistance, and displacement exponents

The only results left to prove are Corollaries
1.2
–
1.4
. The fact that
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}=n^{\beta+o(1)}
follows mostly from Theorem
1.1
and the scale-covariance
R^{\beta}d_{Q}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)=d_{RQ}(R\cdot,R\cdot;R\Gamma)
of the CLE
6
metrics proved in
[
67
,
66
]
. In order to show that
\mathbf{E}[S_{n}]\asymp\operatorname{quant}_{q}[S_{n}]\asymp{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}
, we need a more quantitative version of the Hölder bound in Proposition
3.11
, which we show in Section
7.1
. See Section
10
for a full proof of Corollary
1.2
. The existence of the spectral dimension then follows almost immediately from Kumagai and Misumi’s
[
56
]
results relating the spectral dimension of a random graph to its resistance and volume growth.

4.
Convergence of the path metric

In this section, we prove Proposition
3.1
.

4.1.
Tightness

We first show tightness of the path metric over all clusters and subdomains of
[0,1]^{2}
. Recall the definition of
\mathcal{X}_{Q,n}^{(m)}
from Section
2.1
, which is the
m
-th largest cluster by diameter of
\Lambda_{Q,n}
.

Proposition 4.1
.

The collection
\{(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},\pi_{n})\colon n\in\mathbf{N},\,m\in\mathbf{N},\,Q\in\mathcal{Q}\}
is tight with respect to the GHf topology.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\delta>0
. Let
N^{p}_{n}(\delta,m,Q)
be the minimum number of
\delta
-balls in the metric
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n}
needed to cover
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n}
. Let
N^{\prime}(\delta)
denote some integer-valued function of
\delta
. Let
E_{n}(N^{\prime})
be the event that for all
\delta>0
,
m\in\mathbf{N}
, and
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
, we have
N^{p}_{n}(\delta,m,Q)\leq N^{\prime}(\delta)
. Then for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a function
N^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
such that for all
n
,

(4.1)

\mathbf{P}(E_{n}(N^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}))>1-\varepsilon.

Proof.

Note that it suffices to prove the statement for all
\delta
of the form
\delta=\frac{1}{\ell}
for
\ell\in\mathbf{N}
. Consider some
n\in\mathbf{N}
and let
z_{1},\ldots,z_{\ell^{2}}\in\Lambda_{n}
such that the boxes
B(z_{i},\ell^{-1}/2)
cover
[0,1]^{2}
. Let
K>0
, whose exact value we will determine later. Let
F_{\ell}=F_{\ell}(K)
be the event that for all
1\leq i\leq\ell^{2}
, there are at most
K
disjoint open crossings of
A(z_{i};\ell^{-1}/2,\ell^{-1})
for percolation in
\Lambda_{n}
. Let
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
and
m\in\mathbf{N}
. Suppose that there exist
z_{i1},z_{i2},\ldots,z_{i(K+1)}\in\mathcal{X}_{Q,n}^{(m)}\cap B(z_{i},\ell^{-1}/2)
such that
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}(z_{ij},z_{ij^{\prime}})\geq\frac{2}{\ell}
for all
1\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq K+1
. Then the annulus
A(z_{i};\ell^{-1}/2,\ell^{-1})
must have
K+1
disjoint open crossings (see Figure
4.1
). So on the event
F_{\ell}(K)
, for each box
B(z_{i},\ell^{-1}/2)
, we can choose at most
K
sites
z_{i1},\ldots,z_{iK}
such that

(4.2)

\max_{v\in B(z_{i},\ell^{2})\cap\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n}}\min_{1\leq j\leq K}d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n}(v,z_{ij})\leq 2\ell^{-1}.

So on
F_{\ell}(K)
, the space
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n}
can be covered by at most
K\ell^{2}
many
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n}
-balls of radius
2\ell^{-1}
, i.e.,
N_{n}^{p}(2\ell^{-1},m,Q)\leq K\ell^{2}
. Let
0<p_{0}<1
be a uniform (in n) upper bound on the probability that there exists an open crossing of
A(z;\ell^{-1}/2,\ell^{-1})\cap[0,1)^{2}
in
\Lambda_{n}
. Then by the BK inequality,

(4.3)

\mathbf{P}(F_{\ell}(K)^{c})\leq\ell^{2}p_{0}^{K}<\varepsilon 2^{-\ell}

for
K=K(\ell,\varepsilon)
sufficiently large. Let
N^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(2\ell^{-1})=K\ell^{2}
. Then

(4.4)

\mathbf{P}(E_{n}(N^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})^{c})\leq\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\mathbf{P}(F_{\ell}(K)^{c})<\varepsilon,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Figure 4.1.
Four points
z_{i1},\ldots,z_{i4}
together with an annulus such that the pairwise path diameter distances are at least the size of the annulus. Because
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}(z_{i1},z_{i2})
is greater than the diameter of the annulus, it must be that any open path connecting these two points leaves the annulus. Moreover, since these points are in the same cluster, such an open path exists. In particular, we can find
2
disjoint open crossings of our annulus – the section of the path going from
z_{i1}
to the exterior of the annulus, and the section of the path going from the exterior to
z_{i2}
. These must be disjoint as otherwise we would be able to find an open path connecting
z_{i1}
and
z_{i2}
within
B(z_{i},\ell^{-1})
. Every subsequent point
(z_{i3},z_{i4},\ldots)
adds one more open crossing. Indeed, there is an open path between
z_{ij}
to
z_{i1}
that leaves the annulus. If it doesn’t create any additional crossings, then
z_{ij}
must be connected to one of
z_{i1},z_{i2},\ldots,z_{i,j-1}
within
B(z_{i},\ell^{-1})
, contradicting our original assumption.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Note that on the event
E_{n}(N^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})
defined in Lemma
4.2
, each
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n})
where
m\in\mathbf{N}
,
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
is totally bounded as quantified by the function
N^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
, and hence is contained in a compact set
\mathcal{K}(N^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})
in the Gromov-Hausdorff topology. Furthermore, for any two points
x_{n},y_{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n}
we have
\|\pi_{n}(x_{n})-\pi_{n}(y_{n})\|_{2}\leq d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n}(x_{n},y_{n})
. So by
[
4
, Proposition A.4]
, the collection
\{(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},\pi_{n})\colon(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n})\in\mathcal{K}(N^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})\}
is relatively compact with respect to the GHf topology. Tightness thus follows immediately from Lemma
4.2
.
∎

4.2.
Uniqueness

The previous proposition implies that
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},\pi_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
has subsequential limits. We now show that every limit in fact equals
(\Lambda_{Q},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q},\pi)
.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

By Proposition
4.1
, Theorem
2.4
, and the Skorokhod representation theorem, for each subsequence
I\subset\mathbf{N}
, there exists a further subsequence
J\subset I
and a coupling such that for all
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
,
(\Gamma_{Q,n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
converges to
\Gamma_{Q}
and such that
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},\pi_{n})_{n\in J}
has a GHf limit, which we call
(\tilde{\Lambda}_{Q},\tilde{d}^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q},\tilde{\pi})
. To complete the proof, we construct an isometry
\phi\colon\tilde{\Lambda}_{Q}\to\Lambda_{Q}
for each
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
. Throughout the proof, we assume that
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n})_{n\in J}
and
(\tilde{\Lambda}_{Q},\tilde{d}^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q})
are isometrically embedded in a common metric space in accordance with the GHf convergence. Further, by
[
1
, Theorem A.1]
, switching to a subsequence if necessary, we can assume that the family of open paths in
\Lambda_{Q,n}
converges in
\Delta_{\Hc}
.

Let
\tilde{x},\tilde{y}\in\tilde{\Lambda}_{Q}
be distinct points in the same component of
\tilde{\Lambda}_{Q}
. There exist sequences
(x_{n})_{n\in J}
and
(y_{n})_{n\in J}
where
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
are in the same component of
\Lambda_{Q,n}
that converge to
\tilde{x}
and
\tilde{y}
, respectively, and such that their projections converge.
Let
\eta_{n}
be a path between
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
with minimal diameter that lies entirely inside
Q
, i.e.
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n}(x_{n},y_{n})=\diam(\eta_{n})
.
Let
\eta
be any subsequential limit. By the convergence with respect to
\Delta_{\cur}
, we have that
\eta(0)=\tilde{\pi}(\tilde{x})
and
\eta(1)=\tilde{\pi}(\tilde{y})
. Furthermore, the diameter of
\eta
equals
\tilde{d}^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})
. By Lemma
2.7
,
\eta
can be chosen to lie entirely inside
Q
.
We show that
\eta
is an open path in the
\CLE_{6}
-gasket. Let
\gamma\in\Gamma
be a
\CLE_{6}
loop.
Let
\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma_{n}
converge to
\gamma
. Then for all
n
large enough,
x_{n}
is either on the inside of
\gamma_{n}
or on the outside. Assume the former. Since
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
are connected,
y_{n}
must also be on the inside of
\gamma_{n}
. So
\eta_{n}
lies entirely inside
\gamma_{n}
. Then
\eta
must also lie entirely inside
\gamma
, so
\gamma
does not cross
\eta
. Now assume
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
lie outside
\gamma_{n}
. Then by the same reasoning,
\eta
must lie outside
\gamma
and so
\gamma
does not cross
\eta
. It follows that
\eta
is an open
\CLE_{6}
-path. Furthermore, the endpoints
\eta(0),\eta(1)
define prime ends
x,y\in\Lambda
such that
\eta
is an open path from
x
to
y
. Let
x=\phi(\tilde{x})
and
y=\phi(\tilde{y})
. Note that the map
\phi
is indeed well-defined, i.e., the choice of
x
does not depend on
\tilde{y}
. Indeed, let
\tilde{y}^{\prime}\in\tilde{\Lambda}_{Q}
and let
(y^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in J}
converge to
\tilde{y}^{\prime}
in
\tilde{d}^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q}
. Let
\eta^{\prime}_{n}
be an open path between
x_{n}
and
y^{\prime}_{n}
with minimal diameter inside
Q
and let
\eta^{\prime}
be a subsequential limit. Then by the same argument as above,
\eta^{\prime}
is an open
\CLE_{6}
path. Moreover, the concatenation of
\eta_{n},\eta^{\prime}_{n}
does not cross any loop of
\Gamma_{Q,n}
, hence the concatenation of
\eta,\eta^{\prime}
does not cross any loop of
\Gamma_{Q}
. Therefore
\eta(0),\eta^{\prime}(0)
define the same prime end. Similarly, our definition of
\phi(\tilde{x})
does not depend on the choice of the sequence
(x_{n})
converging to
\tilde{x}
, since for a different sequence
\tilde{x}_{n}\to\tilde{x}
we can apply the same argument with a path
\tilde{\eta}_{n}
with
\diam(\tilde{\eta}_{n})=d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n}(x_{n},\tilde{x}_{n})\to 0
. We conclude that
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q}(\phi(\tilde{x}),\phi(\tilde{y}))\leq\tilde{d}^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})
.

We now show the opposite inequality. Let
x,y\in\Lambda
and let
\eta
be a simple
\CLE_{6}
-open path from
x
to
y
with smallest diameter. Let
\varepsilon>0
and consider the
\varepsilon
-fattening
B(\eta,\varepsilon):=\{z\in[0,1]^{2}:\dist(z,\eta)<\varepsilon\}
. We claim that for all
n
large enough, there exist
x^{\prime}_{n}(\varepsilon),y^{\prime}_{n}(\varepsilon)\in\Lambda_{Q,n}
such that
\dist(\pi_{n}(x^{\prime}_{n}(\varepsilon)),\pi(x))
,
\dist(\pi_{n}(y^{\prime}_{n}(\varepsilon)),\pi(y))<\varepsilon
and such that
x^{\prime}_{n}
and
y^{\prime}_{n}
are connected inside
B(\eta,\varepsilon)
. Indeed, assume that is not the case, i.e. for infinitely many
n
, the
\varepsilon
-neighborhoods of
\pi(x)
and of
\pi(y)
are disconnected within
B(\eta,\varepsilon)
by some percolation loop
\gamma_{n}
. In particular, there are
0<t_{1}<t_{2}<1
such that
\gamma_{n}
crosses
\eta[t_{1},t_{2}]
and exits
B(\eta,\varepsilon)
.

Without loss of generality (considering a subsequence if necessary), we may assume that
\gamma_{n}
converges to some non-empty loop
\gamma
. The loop
\gamma
must then cross
\eta
, which is a contradiction. Now, consider a sequence
\varepsilon_{k}\searrow 0
and let
n_{k}
be large enough so that there is a path
\eta_{n_{k}}
from
x^{\prime}_{n_{k}}(\varepsilon_{k})
to
y^{\prime}_{n_{k}}(\varepsilon_{k})
within
B(\eta,\varepsilon_{k})
. Considering a further subsequence if necessary, we may assume that
x^{\prime}_{n_{k}},y^{\prime}_{n_{k}}
converge in
\tilde{d}^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q}
to some
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\tilde{\Lambda}_{Q}
, respectively, and
\eta_{n_{k}}
converges in
\Delta_{\cur}
to
\tilde{\eta}
. We have
\pi(\tilde{\eta})=\pi(\eta)
, and since
\eta
is a simple path, it follows that
\tilde{\eta}=\eta
.
By the first part of the proof, we see that
x=\phi(x^{\prime})
,
y=\phi(y^{\prime})
, and we have
\tilde{d}^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\lim_{k\to\infty}d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n_{k}}(x^{\prime}_{n_{k}}(\varepsilon_{k}),y^{\prime}_{n_{k}}(\varepsilon_{k}))\leq\diam(\eta)=d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q}(x,y)
.

It follows from the above that
\phi
is surjective. The above also shows that
\phi
is injective. Indeed, let
\tilde{x},\tilde{y}\in\tilde{\Lambda}_{Q}
and assume that
\phi(\tilde{x})=\phi(\tilde{y})
. Then
\tilde{d}^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\leq d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q}(\phi(\tilde{x}),\phi(\tilde{y}))=0
, so
\tilde{x}=\tilde{y}
. Since we also showed that
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}(\phi(\tilde{x}),\phi(\tilde{y}))=\tilde{d}^{{\mathrm{path}}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})
, the proof is complete.
∎

5.
Polynomial tail estimates

5.1.
A priori estimate

In this section, we give a proof of Proposition
3.5
.

Recall the definition of the event
\mathcal{E}(\Lambda_{n})
from Section
3.2.1
. Before starting the proof of Proposition
3.5
, let us show that the event
\mathcal{E}(\Lambda_{n})
occurs with probability bounded away from 0.

Lemma 5.1
.

Fix
\delta\in(0,1/4)
. There exists a constant
c=c(\delta)>0
such that for all
n\in\mathbf{N}
with
\delta n\geq 2
, we have
\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{E}(\Lambda_{n}))\geq c
.
6
6

6

The restriction on
n
is simply there to ensure that the set of sites of the lattice
\Lambda_{n}
that are at most
\delta
away from the
c_{i}^{(0)}
’s is non-empty.

Proof.

Fix
\delta
and let
n\geq 2/\delta
. Define the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}(\Lambda_{n})
on
\Lambda_{n}
as follows:

\bullet

There is a closed left-to-right crossing inside
[0,1]\times\!\left[0,\frac{1}{6}\right]
;

\bullet

There is a closed top-to-bottom crossing inside
\!\left[\frac{1}{2}-\delta,\frac{1}{2}+\delta\right]\times\!\left[0,\frac{1}{6}\right]
;

\bullet

There is a closed circuit in the semiannulus
A(c_{3}^{(0)};\delta,2\delta)\cap\Lambda_{n}
;

\bullet

There is an open left-to-right crossing inside
[0,1]\times(1/6,1/3]
.

See Figure
5.1
for illustration. By the standard RSW estimates and Harris’ inequality, we see that there exists a constant
c_{1}(\delta)>0
such that
\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1}(\Lambda_{n}))\geq c_{1}
for all
n\geq 2\delta
. On this event, one can explore the closed cluster adjacent to the bottom side of
\Lambda_{n}
; let
\mathcal{S}
denote the set of explored sites - this includes the closed cluster adjacent to the bottom and all the neighboring sites which must be open. Condition on
\mathcal{S}=S
. The remaining percolation on
\Lambda_{n}\setminus S
is still i.i.d. critical percolation. Define the event
\mathcal{E}_{2}(\Lambda_{n},S)
on
\Lambda_{n}\setminus S
as follows:

\bullet

There exist open circuits in semiannuli
A(c_{2}^{(0)};\delta,2\delta)\cap\Lambda_{n}
and
A(c_{2}^{(0)};\delta,2\delta)\cap\Lambda_{n}
;

\bullet

There exist open left-to-right paths in
[0,2\delta]\times\!\left[\frac{1}{2}-\delta,\frac{1}{2}+\delta\right]
and
[1-2\delta,1]\times\!\left[\frac{1}{2}-\delta,\frac{1}{2}+\delta\right]
;

\bullet

There exist open top-to-bottom paths in
[0,2\delta]\times\!\left[0,\frac{1}{2}+\delta\right]\setminus S
and
[1-2\delta,1]\times\!\left[0,\frac{1}{2}+\delta\right]\setminus S
;

\bullet

There is a closed top-to bottom path in
[1/2-\delta,1/2+\delta]\times[0,1]\setminus S
;

\bullet

There is a closed left-to-right path in
[0,1]\times[5/6,1]
;

\bullet

There exists an open circuit inside the semiannulus
A(c_{1}^{(0)};\delta,2\delta)
.

Figure 5.1.
An illustration of events
\mathcal{E}_{1}(\Lambda_{n})
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}(\Lambda_{n},S)
. The red and blue interface on the bottom of the second picture belong to the set
S
- this is the boundary of the closed cluster adjacent to the bottom.

Note that for any possible
S
,
\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{E}_{2}(\Lambda_{n},S))\geq\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{E}_{2}(\Lambda_{n},\emptyset))
, and the latter is bounded away from zero by a constant
c_{2}=c_{2}(\delta)>0
, again by standard RSW estimates. Moreover, the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}(\Lambda_{n})\cap\{\mathcal{S}=S\}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(\Lambda_{n},S)
implies
\mathcal{E}(\Lambda_{n})
. Thus, because
\mathcal{E}_{1}(\Lambda_{n})\cap\{\mathcal{S}=S\}
can be verified on
S
, we have that

(5.1)

\begin{split}\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{E}(\Lambda_{n}))&\geq\sum_{S}\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1}(\Lambda_{n})\cap\{\mathcal{S}=S\}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(\Lambda_{n},S))\\
&=\sum_{S}\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{E}_{2}(\Lambda_{n},S)\,|\,\mathcal{E}_{1}(\Lambda_{n})\cap\{\mathcal{S}=S\})\cdot\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1}(\Lambda_{n})\cap\{\mathcal{S}=S\})\\
&=\sum_{S}\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{E}_{2}(\Lambda_{n},S))\cdot\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1}(\Lambda_{n})\cap\{\mathcal{S}=S\})\geq c_{2}\cdot\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1}(\Lambda_{n}))=c_{2}\cdot c_{1}>0,\end{split}

as wanted.
∎

Due to the nature of the event
\mathcal{E}
, it will be useful to have a result concerning the probability that there exist arms in an annulus that hit inner and outer boundaries of this annulus at prescribed boundary segments. The following lemma, which follows immediately from
[
70
, Theorem 10]
, addresses this situation. More precisely, it tells us that the probability that there are
4
arms in an annulus such that each arms has a small extension and that their endpoints are at prescribed intervals at the inner and outer boundaries of our annulus is comparable to the probability that we simply have
4
arms in this annulus when we consider critical percolation. Namely, let
b
and
B
be two boxes in the triangular grid of radii
r
and
R
respectively, such that
b\subseteq B
and
\textrm{dist}(b,\partial B)\geq\frac{R}{3}
. Consider the (non-centered) annulus determined by
b
and
B
.

For
\delta
as in the definition of the event
\mathcal{E}
, let
i_{1},\ldots,i_{4}
denote subintervals of the sides of box
b
, each of length
2\delta r
, centered at the midpoints of the respective sides and listed in clockwise order starting from the top side. Define
I_{1},\ldots,I_{4}
similarly for the box
B
. Suppose there exists a closed arm
\eta_{1}
going from
i_{1}
to
I_{1}
. Say
z_{1}
and
Z_{1}
are its endpoints on
b
and
B
respectively. We say that
\eta_{1}
has a small extension on the outside, if:

\bullet

There exists a closed horizontal crossing
C_{1}
of the parallelogram
Z_{1}+[-\delta R,\delta R]\times[0,\sqrt{\delta}R]

\bullet

C_{1}
is connected to
\eta_{1}
via a closed path fully contained within the box
B(Z_{1},\sqrt{\delta}R)
.

We can analogously define what it means for
\eta_{2},\eta_{3}
and
\eta_{4}
to have small extensions on the outside, as well as small extensions on the inside. See Figure
5.2
below.

Figure 5.2.
The figure above illustrates the event
\widehat{A_{4}}(b,B)
for a (not necessarily centered) annulus
B\setminus b
. Green segments on the outer (resp. inner) boundaries represent the segments of length
2\delta R
(resp.
2\delta r
) centered at the midpoints of the
4
sides of this boundary. Red and blue curves represent the closed (resp. open) arms crossing this annulus. Note that for all
1\leq j\leq 4
,
\eta_{j}
starts at
i_{j}
and ends at
I_{j}
. Moreover, each
\eta_{j}
has a small extension outside of
B
and within
b
. For the extension of
\eta_{1}
on the outside, we can see that there exists a closed left-to-right crossing of
Z_{1}+[-\delta R,\delta R]\times[0,\sqrt{\delta}R]
, and this crossing is connected to
\eta_{1}
within the larger box
B(Z_{1},\sqrt{\delta}R)
pictured above.

Having this definition in mind, we will consider the following two events:

(5.2)

\begin{split}A_{4}(b,B)&=\{\textrm{there are 4 arms of alternating colors between }\partial b\leftrightarrow\partial B\}\\
\widehat{A_{4}}(b,B)&=\!\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\textrm{there are 4 arms }\eta_{1},\ldots\eta_{4}\textrm{ of alternating colors between }\partial b\leftrightarrow\partial B\\
\textrm{ s.t.\ }\eta_{1}\textrm{ is closed, the endpoints of }\eta_{a}\textrm{ belong to }i_{a},I_{a}\textrm{ for }a=1,\ldots,4,\\
\textrm{and all }\eta_{a}\textrm{ have small extensions both on the inside and on the outside }\end{array}\right\}.\end{split}

In the remainder of the paper, we fix

\delta=\frac{1}{10000}.

For such
\delta
,
[
70
, Theorem 10]
tells us that:

Lemma 5.2
.

There exists a constant
C\in(0,1)
such that for all
\delta^{-2}\leq r<\frac{R}{3}
and boxes
b,B
as above we have

C\cdot\mathbf{P}(A_{4}(b,B))\leq\mathbf{P}(\widehat{A_{4}}(b,B))\leq\mathbf{P}(A_{4}(b,B)).

Throughout the remaining of this section and Section
6
, we will fix some
R=R(\varepsilon)\in\mathbf{N}
sufficiently large, so that the assumptions of all the necessary lemmas are satisfied.

5.1.1.
Proof of Proposition
3.5

Recall the definition of
\mathcal{B}^{R}_{k,n}
from Section
2.1
. Note that the set
\mathcal{B}^{R}_{k,n}
partitions the
(n+1)\times(n+1)
graph
\Lambda_{n}
into
R^{2(k-1)}
identical
R^{\kappa-k}\times R^{\kappa-k}
subgraphs, together with the strips
\Lambda_{n}\cap\!\left([n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1},1)\times[0,1)\right)
and
\Lambda_{n}\cap\!\left([0,1)\times[n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1},1)\right)
. Consider a fixed box
B\in\mathcal{B}^{R}_{k,n}
and let
B^{(k-1)},\ldots,B^{(1)},B^{(0)}
be its unique
R
-adic superboxes at levels
k-1,\ldots,1,0
.

In particular,
B^{(1)}=[0,n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1})^{2}
and we define
B^{(0)}:=[0,1)^{2}
.
We say that
B
is
away from the boundary
if each of its superboxes
B^{(i)}
is at distance at least
\frac{1}{4n}R^{\kappa-i+1}
away from the sides of the superbox
B^{(i-1)}
at the next level for
i\geq 2
. In the following, we will only consider boxes
B\in\mathcal{B}^{R}_{k,n}
that are away from the boundary.

We say
B
is
B^{(l)}
-
important
with
l\geq 1
if there exist 4 arms
\eta_{1}^{(k)},\ldots,\eta_{4}^{(k)}
of alternating colors from
\partial B
to
\partial B^{(l)}
such that

\bullet

\eta_{1}^{(k)}
is a closed/red crossing;

\bullet

for all
l\leq i\leq k
and
0\leq j\leq 4
,
\eta_{j}^{(k)}
passes through superbox
B^{(i)}
within distance
\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-i}
of
c_{j}^{(i)}
;

\bullet

for all
l\leq i\leq k
and
0\leq j\leq 4
, the (open/closed) cluster of
\eta_{j}^{(k)}
does not come within distance
2\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-i}
of
c_{a}^{(i)}
for
a\neq j
.

A box of level at least
2
is
B^{(0)}
-important if the above hold for all
2\leq i\leq k
, and, furthermore, every crossing
\eta_{j}^{(k)}
hits the boundary of
B^{(0)}=[0,1]^{2}
within distance
\delta
of
c_{j}^{(0)}
and the cluster of
\eta_{j}^{(k)}
does not come within distance
2\delta
of
c_{a}^{(0)}
for
a\neq j
.
We define
B
to simply be
important
if it is
B^{(0)}
-important and if there exist two closed left-to-right crossings that stay within the strip of width
\frac{1}{4}
from the top and bottom sides respectively. See Figure
5.3
.

Figure 5.3.
An illustration showing the event that a box
B
of level
3
is important. The blue and red curves represent the open (resp. closed) crossings of our annuli. Note that these curves come close to centers of all superboxes of
B
, except for
B^{(1)}
. The two dashed red curves represent the closed horizontal crossings in the outermost annulus. The two dashed red lines represent the closed horizontal crossings in the outermost annulus. Without them, the picture would represent the event that
B
is
B^{(0)}
-important.

We say that
B
is
B^{(l)}
-
bad
if

\bullet

B
is
B^{(l)}
-important;

\bullet

inside of
B
, the event
\mathcal{E}(B)
occurs;

\bullet

the top closed crossing inside
B
is connected to
\eta_{1}^{(k)}
;

\bullet

the bottom closed crossing inside
B
is connected to
\eta_{3}^{(k)}
;

\bullet

the open crossing in
B
is connected to
\eta_{2}^{(k)}
and
\eta_{4}^{(k)}
;

\bullet

X_{B}\geq\mathbf{q}_{n}
.

We say the “arms link up” if the third to fifth events occur. Finally, say
B
is
bad
if in the above definition we substitute the condition that
B
is
B^{(l)}
-important with the condition that
B
is important.

Figure 5.4.
An illustration of the event that a box
B
of level
3
is
B^{(1)}
-bad. If we wanted
B
to be
B^{(0)}
-bad, the crossings in the final annulus
B^{(1)}\setminus B^{(2)}
should be replaced by crossings in
B^{(0)}\setminus B^{(2)}
, which now need not come close to midpoints of the sides of
B^{(1)}
, but, they must come close to midpoints of the sides of
B^{(0)}
. If in addition we wanted
B
to be bad, we would need to include two red left-to-right crossings inside of
B^{(0)}
, as on Figure
5.3
.

Let
T_{k}
be the number of bad boxes at level
k\geq 2
. Note that if there exists a bad box
B
at some level
k\geq 2
, then the event
\mathcal{E}\!\left(B^{(0)}\right)
occurs and
X_{B^{(0)}}\geq X_{B}
. Indeed, the two extra closed crossings guarantee that the top and bottom closed arms are part of closed clusters that cross the box. The arms linking up implies that these clusters touch. Furthermore, every open path from
u\!\left(B^{(0)}\right)
to
v\!\left(B^{(0)}\right)
must pass through
u(B)
and
v(B)
. Thus, by property (
A
) of our metric
D
, we know that
D(u,v)\geq D(u(B),v(B))
, as wanted. Thus, for every
k\geq 1
,

(5.3)

\begin{split}p\geq\mathbf{P}(X_{n}>\mathbf{q}_{n})\geq\mathbf{P}\!\left(X_{B^{(0)}}>\mathbf{q}_{n},\mathcal{E}\!\left(B^{(0)}\right)\right)&\geq\mathbf{P}(T_{k}>0)\geq\frac{\mathbf{E}[T_{k}]^{2}}{\mathbf{E}[T_{k}^{2}]}.\end{split}

The goal is to obtain a lower bound for
\mathbf{E}[T_{k}]
and an upper bound for
\mathbf{E}[T_{k}^{2}]
. We first prove some lemmas.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\zeta>0
. Then there exists
R(\zeta)
such that for all
R\geq R(\zeta)
, there exists
c=c(R)
such that for all
k\geq 2
,
n>R^{k}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}^{R}_{k,n}
,

(5.4)

\begin{split}\mathbf{P}(B\textrm{ is important})\geq cR^{-\!\left(\frac{5}{4}+\zeta\right)k}.\end{split}

Proof.

We will subdivide the proof into four steps.

Step 1:
There exists
\tilde{R}(\zeta)
such that for all
R\geq\tilde{R}(\zeta)
, for
B\in\mathcal{B}^{R}_{2,n}
and every
n>R^{2}
, we have
\mathbf{P}(B\textrm{ is }B^{(1)}\textrm{-important})\geq R^{-\frac{5}{4}-\zeta}
.

The main ingredient in this step is Lemma
5.2
. Let
B^{(1)}_{\delta}\subseteq B^{(1)}
be the box obtained by removing 4 strips of width
\sqrt{\delta}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1}
from each side of
B^{(1)}
. Similarly, let
B^{(2)}_{\delta}\supseteq B^{(2)}
be the box obtained by adding 4 strips of width
\sqrt{\delta}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-2}
to each side of
B^{(2)}=B
. Let
c_{\delta,j}^{(1)},c_{\delta,j}^{(2)}
for
j=1,\ldots,4
be the midpoints of the sides of
B_{\delta}^{(1)}
and
B_{\delta}^{(2)}
. Recall the definition (
5.2
) of the event
\widehat{A_{4}}(B_{\delta}^{(2)},B_{\delta}^{(1)})
and assume that it holds. Suppose that
\eta_{1}
is the closed arm that connects the top sides of the boxes and let
x
be its endpoint on
B^{(1)}_{\delta,1}
. Then there exists a closed horizontal crossing
\gamma
of
x+\!\left[-\frac{\delta}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1},\frac{\delta}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1}\right]\times[0,\sqrt{\delta}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1})
that is connected to
\eta_{1}
inside of
B\!\left(c_{\delta,1}^{(1)},2\sqrt{\delta}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1}\right)
. Consider the parallelogram
c_{\delta,1}^{(1)}+[-2\sqrt{\delta}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1},2\sqrt{\delta}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1}]\times[0,\sqrt{\delta}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1})
. Suppose inside of it the following event occurs:

\bullet

Subdivide
c_{\delta,1}^{(1)}+[-\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1},\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1}]\times[0,\sqrt{\delta}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1})
into 3 disjoint vertical parallelograms of dimensions
\frac{2}{3}\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1}\times\sqrt{\delta}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1}
. Assume each of them has a vertical closed crossing.

\bullet

Assume that there is a closed circuit inside the semiannulus
A\!\left(c_{1}^{(1)};2\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1},4\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1}\right)\allowbreak\cap B^{(1)}
.

Call this event
H\!\left(c_{1}^{(1)}\right)
. Note that by RSW and positive correlation the probability of this event is bounded away from zero by an absolute constant
c(\delta)
, depending only on
\delta
. Define
H\!\left(c_{j}^{(1)}\right),H\!\left(c_{j}^{(2)}\right)
analogously. Since these
8
events depend on disjoint sets of sites, hence are independent, their intersection, called
H
, has probability bounded away from zero by
c^{8}
. See Figure
5.5
.

Figure 5.5.
An illustration of events
H
and
\widehat{A_{4}}\!\left(B_{\delta}^{(2)},B_{\delta}^{(1)}\right)
near the point
c_{1}^{(1)}
. The red curve represents the closed arm
\eta_{1}
with an extension
\gamma
coming from the event
\widehat{A_{4}}\!\left(B_{\delta}^{(2)},B_{\delta}^{(1)}\right)
. The green curve is the closed circuit inside of the annulus
A(c_{1}^{(1)};\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1},2\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1})
, whose existence is guaranteed by
H
.
H
also gives us the existence of the three purple curves, which represent the vertical closed crossings of the three parallelograms partitioning
c_{\delta,1}^{(1)}+[-\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1},\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1}]\times[0,\sqrt{\delta}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1})
. The dashed ones do not intersect
\gamma
, whereas the full one does. Choosing
R
big enough so that
R-\sqrt{\delta}>\frac{2}{3}R
guarantees that at least one of these three crossings intersect
\gamma
. Moreover the closed circuit ensures that the open clusters corresponding to
\eta_{2}
and
\eta_{4}
do not come
2\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-1}
-close to
c^{(1)}_{1}
. So indeed,
H\cap\widehat{A_{4}}\!\left(B_{\delta}^{(2)},B_{\delta}^{(1)}\right)
implies that
B
is
B^{(1)}
-important.

Note that on the event
\widehat{A_{4}}\!\left(B_{\delta}^{(2)},B_{\delta}^{(1)}\right)\cap H
,
B^{(2)}
is
B^{(1)}
-important. Moreover, conditional on the percolation configuration outside these
8
parallelograms, the events
H
and
\widehat{A_{4}}\!\left(B_{\delta}^{(2)},B_{\delta}^{(1)}\right)
are positively correlated. Denote by
\mathcal{A}
the set of sites in
B^{(1)}\setminus B^{(2)}
that do not belong to any of the
8
parallelograms constructed above, and let
\omega_{\mathcal{A}}
denote the percolation configuration on
\mathcal{A}
. Then

\begin{split}\mathbf{P}(B^{(2)}\textrm{ is }B^{(1)}\textrm{-important }|\,\omega_{\mathcal{A}})&\geq\mathbf{P}\!\left(\widehat{A_{4}}(B_{\delta}^{(2)},B_{\delta}^{(1)})\cap H\,|\,\omega_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\geq\mathbf{P}\!\left(\widehat{A_{4}}(B_{\delta}^{(2)},B_{\delta}^{(1)})\,|\,\omega_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\cdot\mathbf{P}\!\left(H\,|\,\omega_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\\
&=\mathbf{P}\!\left(\widehat{A_{4}}(B_{\delta}^{(2)},B_{\delta}^{(1)})\,|\,\omega_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\cdot\mathbf{P}\!\left(H\right)\geq c^{8}\cdot\mathbf{P}\!\left(\widehat{A_{4}}(B_{\delta}^{(2)},B_{\delta}^{(1)})\,|\,\omega_{\mathcal{A}}\right).\end{split}

Summing over all possible configurations
\omega_{\mathcal{A}}
and applying Lemmas
5.2
and
2.2
, we get that there exists
\tilde{R}=\tilde{R(\zeta)}
such that for all
R>\tilde{R}
we have

\begin{split}\mathbf{P}(B^{(2)}\textrm{ is }B^{(1)}\textrm{-important})&\geq c^{8}\cdot\mathbf{P}\!\left(\widehat{A_{4}}(B_{\delta}^{(2)},B_{\delta}^{(1)})\right)\geq c^{8}C\cdot\mathbf{P}\!\left(A_{4}(B_{\delta}^{(2)},B_{\delta}^{(1)})\right)\\
&\geq c^{8}C\cdot R^{-5/4-\zeta/2}\geq R^{-5/4-\zeta}.\end{split}

Step 2:
There exists
\tilde{R}(\zeta)
such that for all
R\geq\tilde{R}(\zeta)
, for all
k\geq 2
,
n>R^{k}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}^{R}_{k,n}
, we have
\mathbf{P}\!\left(B\textrm{ is }B^{(1)}\textrm{-important}\right)\geq R^{-(\frac{5}{4}+\zeta)(k-1)}
.

Conditional on the percolation configuration outside the
4(k+1)
boxes
B\!\left(c_{i}^{(j)},2\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-j}\right)\cap(\Lambda_{n}\setminus B)
for
j=1,\ldots,k
,
i=1,\ldots,4
, the event
\bigcap_{j=2}^{k}\!\left\{B^{(j)}\textrm{ is }B^{(j-1)}\textrm{-important}\right\}
and the event that there exist
4(k+1)
appropriately colored circuits in each
A\!\left(c_{i}^{(j)};\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-j},2\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-j}\right)
are positively correlated. Moreover, these two events imply that
B
is
B^{(1)}
-important. Hence, exactly as in Step 1 we get that

\begin{split}\mathbf{P}(B\textrm{ is }B^{(1)}\textrm{-important})&\geq\mathbf{P}\!\left(\bigcap_{j=2}^{k}\!\left\{B^{(j)}\textrm{ is }B^{(j-1)}\textrm{-important}\right\}\bigg|\,\omega_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\cdot\mathbf{P}(4(k+1)\textrm{ circuits}).\end{split}

Note again by RSW estimates that the probability of each circuit existing is bounded away from zero by an absolute constant
c
. Hence, using independence and applying Step 1
k-1
times, we get that there exists
R(\zeta)
big enough such that for all
R>R(\zeta)
,

\begin{split}\mathbf{P}(B\textrm{ is }B^{(1)}\textrm{-important})\geq{}&c^{4k}\cdot\prod_{j=2}^{k}\mathbf{P}\!\left(B^{(j)}\textrm{ is }B^{(j-1)}\textrm{-important}\right)\\
\geq{}&c^{4k}\cdot\!\left(R^{-5/4-\zeta/2}\right)^{k-1}\geq R^{-(5/4+\zeta)(k-1)}.\end{split}

Step 3:
There exists
\tilde{R}(\zeta)
such that for all
R\geq\tilde{R}(\zeta)
, there exists
c=c(R)
such that for all
k\geq 2
,
n>R^{k}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}^{R}_{k,n}
, we have
\mathbf{P}\!\left(B\textrm{ is }B^{(0)}\textrm{-important}\right)\geq cR^{-(\frac{5}{4}+\zeta)k}
.

An analogous proof to the one we have used in Step 1 shows that
\mathbf{P}(B^{(2)}\textrm{ is }B^{(0)}\textrm{-important})\geq R^{-5/4-\zeta}
. Moreover, an analogous argument to Step
2
then yields that

(5.5)

\mathbf{P}(B\textrm{ is }B^{(0)}\textrm{-important})\geq c_{1}R^{-(5/4+\zeta))(k-1)},

for some constant
c_{1}=c_{1}(R)
depending only on
R
.

Step 4:
The same formulas hold for
B
being important rather than
B^{(0)}
-important.

To ensure that
B
is important, we only need to add two horizontal closed crossings inside of
B^{(0)}
. We will add them in the strips of width
\frac{1}{4R}\leq\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\delta}
from the top and bottom sides of
B^{(0)}
(instead of
1/4
as in the definition) to ensure that this event has positive correlation with events
H\!\left(c_{1}^{(0)}\right),H\!\left(c_{3}^{(0)}\right)
and is independent of
H\!\left(c_{2}^{(0)}\right),H\!\left(c_{4}^{(0)}\right)
and
H\!\left(c_{j}^{(k)}\right)
for every
k\geq 2,j=1,\ldots,4
. Since the probability of these two closed crossings existing is bounded away from 0 by a constant that depends only on
R
, we have
\mathbf{P}(B\textrm{ is important})\gtrsim_{R}\mathbf{P}(B\textrm{ is }B^{(0)}\textrm{-important})
, which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
B\in\mathcal{B}^{R}_{k,n}
and let
B^{(i)}
be one of its superboxes. There exists a constant
c=c(\delta)>0
such that for all
R
sufficiently large,
k\geq 2
,
0\leq i<k
and
n>R^{k}
,

(5.6)

\begin{split}c\mathbf{P}(X_{R^{\kappa-k}}>\mathbf{q}_{n})\mathbf{P}(B\textrm{ is $B^{(i)}$-important})\leq{}&\mathbf{P}(B\textrm{ is $B^{(i)}$-bad})\\
\leq{}&\mathbf{P}(X_{R^{\kappa-k}}>\mathbf{q}_{n})\mathbf{P}(B\textrm{ is $B^{(i)}$-important})\end{split}

Proof.

By the definition of a bad box and independence of the percolation configuration inside and outside of
B
, we have

(5.7)

\begin{split}\mathbf{P}\!\left(B\textrm{ is $B^{(i)}$-bad}\right)&=\mathbf{P}(B\textrm{ is $B^{(i)}$-important})\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{E}(B))\mathbf{P}(X_{B}>\mathbf{q}_{n}\mid\mathcal{E}(B))\\
&\cdot\mathbf{P}(\textrm{arms link up}\mid\mathcal{E}(B),\,B\textrm{ is $B^{(i)}$-important},\,X_{B}>\mathbf{q}_{n}).\end{split}

Lemma
6
tells us that
\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{E}(B))
is bounded uniformly from below over all
n
and
B
by a constant depending only on
\delta
(take
R\geq 2/\delta
so that the lemma applies). Furthermore,
\mathbf{P}(X_{B}>\mathbf{q}_{n}\mid\mathcal{E}(B))=\mathbf{P}(X_{R^{\kappa-k}}>\mathbf{q}_{n})
. We now show that the last term in (
5.7
) is bounded from below by a universal constant.

Consider the box
B(c_{j}^{(k)},2\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-k})
of radius
2\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-k}
around
c_{j}^{(k)}
and let
A(c_{j}^{(k)};\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-k},\allowbreak 2\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-k})
be the annulus of radii
\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-k}
and
2\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-k}
. As in the proof of the previous lemma, we can show that conditional on the percolation configuration outside the 4 boxes
B(c_{j}^{(k)};2\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-k})
, the event that the arms link up and the event
\mathcal{E}(B)\cap\{B\textrm{ is }B^{(i)}\textrm{-important}\}\cap\{X_{B}>\mathbf{q}_{n}\}
are positively correlated. Here we use that the cluster of the top closed crossing does not come within distance
2\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-k}
of
c_{2}^{(k)},c_{3}^{(k)},c_{4}^{(k)}
and the similar holds for the other two crossings. Since
X_{B}
is the distance between two meeting points of the closed top and bottom clusters, this also implies that
X_{B}
is independent of the percolation configurations inside the balls
B(c_{j}^{(k)},2\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-k})
. We conclude the proof by noting that the arms link up if there exist loops of the correct color inside each annulus
A(c_{j}^{(k)};\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-k},2\delta n^{-1}R^{\kappa-k})
. The probability of this event is bounded from below by a universal constant by RSW estimates.
∎

We now bound the second moment of
T_{k}
. Let
B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathcal{B}^{R}_{k,n}
be
R
-adic boxes at level
k
, and let
B_{1,2}^{(d)}
be their smallest common
R
-adic superbox, which is at some level
d\geq 1
. Let
B_{1}^{(d+1)},B_{2}^{(d+1)}
be the
(d+1)
-level subboxes of
B_{1,2}^{(d)}
containing
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
respectively. Then

(5.8)

\begin{split}&\mathbf{P}(B_{1},B_{2}\textrm{ are bad})\\
&{=}\mathbf{P}(B_{1,2}^{(d)}\textrm{ is important})\mathbf{P}(B_{1},B_{2}\textrm{ are bad }|B_{1,2}^{(d)}\textrm{ is important})\\
&\leq\mathbf{P}(B_{1,2}^{(d)}\textrm{ is important})\mathbf{P}(B_{1}\textrm{ is }B_{1}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-bad, }B_{2}\textrm{ is }B_{2}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-bad}|B_{1,2}^{(d)}\textrm{ is important})\\
&=\mathbf{P}(B_{1,2}^{(d)}\textrm{ is important})\mathbf{P}(B_{1}\textrm{ is }B_{1}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-bad })\mathbf{P}(B_{2}\textrm{ is }B_{2}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-bad}).\end{split}

Furthermore,

(5.9)

\begin{split}\mathbf{P}(B_{1}\textrm{ is bad})={}&\mathbf{P}(B_{1}\textrm{ is bad }|B_{1}\textrm{ is }B_{1}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-bad, }B_{1}^{(d+1)}\textrm{ is important})\\
&\cdot\mathbf{P}(B_{1}\textrm{ is }B_{1}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-bad})\mathbf{P}(B_{1}^{(d+1)}\textrm{ is important})\\
={}&\mathbf{P}(\textrm{arms link up}|B_{1}\textrm{ is }B_{1}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-bad, }B_{1}^{(d+1)}\textrm{ is important})\\
&\cdot\mathbf{P}(B_{1}\textrm{ is }B_{1}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-bad})\mathbf{P}(B_{1}^{(d+1)}\textrm{ is important}).\end{split}

For the first equality, we use that the percolation configurations inside and outside
B_{1}^{(d+1)}
are independent. Now by the exact same argument as in the proof of Lemma
5.4
, we obtain

\begin{split}\mathbf{P}(B_{1}\text{ is bad})\geq&\,C_{0}\mathbf{P}(B_{1}\textrm{ is }B_{1}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-bad})\mathbf{P}(B_{1}^{(d+1)}\textrm{ is important})\\
\geq&\,C_{0}\cdot C(R)\cdot\mathbf{P}(B_{1}\textrm{ is }B_{1}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-bad})\mathbf{P}(B_{1}^{(d)}\textrm{ is important})\end{split}

for some absolute constant
C_{0}
and a constant
C(R)
depending only on
R
. The last inequality follows from Lemma
5.3
combined with another application of the argument in the proof of Lemma
5.4
.

It follows from equations (
5.8
) and (
5.1.1
) and Lemma
5.4
that there exist
C=C(R)>0
and
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(R)>0
such that for all
k,n
,

(5.10)

\begin{split}\mathbf{E}[T_{k}^{2}]={}&\mathbf{E}\!\left[\!\left(\sum_{i}\mathbf{1}_{B_{i}\textrm{ is bad}}\right)^{2}\right]\\
={}&\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbf{P}(B_{i},B_{j}\textrm{ are bad})+\sum_{i}\mathbf{P}(B_{i}\textrm{ is bad})\\
\leq{}&\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbf{P}(B_{i,j}^{(d)}\textrm{ is important})\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{i}\textrm{ is }B_{i}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-bad}\right)\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{j}\textrm{ is }B_{j}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-bad}\right)\\
&+\sum_{i}\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{i}\textrm{ is important}\right)\mathbf{P}\!\left(X_{R^{-k}n}>\mathbf{q}_{n}\right)\\
\leq{}&\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbf{P}(B_{i,j}^{(d)}\textrm{ is important})\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{i}\textrm{ is }B_{i}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-important}\right)\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{j}\textrm{ is }B_{j}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-important}\right)\\
&\cdot\mathbf{P}(X_{R^{-k}n}>\mathbf{q}_{n})^{2}+\sum_{i}\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{i}\textrm{ is important}\right)\mathbf{P}\!\left(X_{R^{-k}n}>\mathbf{q}_{n}\right)\end{split}

and

(5.11)

\begin{split}\mathbf{E}[T_{k}]^{2}={}&\!\left(\sum_{i}\mathbf{P}(B_{i}\textrm{ is bad})\right)^{2}\\
={}&\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbf{P}(B_{i}\textrm{ is bad})\mathbf{P}(B_{j}\textrm{ is bad})+\sum_{i}\mathbf{P}(B_{i}\textrm{ is bad})^{2}\\
\geq{}&C\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbf{P}(B_{i}\textrm{ is }B_{i}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-bad})\mathbf{P}(B_{i}^{(d)}\textrm{ is important})\mathbf{P}(B_{j}\textrm{ is }B_{j}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-bad})\mathbf{P}(B_{j}^{(d)}\textrm{ is important})\\
\geq{}&C^{\prime}\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbf{P}(B_{i,j}^{(d)}\textrm{ is important})^{2}\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{i}\textrm{ is }B_{i}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-important}\right)\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{j}\textrm{ is }B_{j}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-important}\right)\\
&\cdot\mathbf{P}\!\left(X_{R^{-k}n}>\mathbf{q}_{n}\right)^{2}.\end{split}

In the above, we sum over all
R
-adic subboxes
B_{i}
and
B_{j}
at level
k
that are away from the boundary. These equations give us an upper bound on
\mathbf{E}[T_{k}^{2}]
and a lower bound on
\mathbf{E}[T_{k}]^{2}
. The goal is now to substitute these bounds into (
5.3
) and obtain a bound on
\mathbf{P}(X_{R^{\kappa-k}}>\mathbf{q}_{n})
. In order to do so, we will need the following lemma telling us that two sums in (
5.10
) and (
5.11
) are comparable.

Lemma 5.5
.

There exists a constant
C=C(R)>0
such that for all
k\geq 2
and
n>R^{k}
,

(5.12)

\begin{split}&\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbf{P}(B_{i,j}^{(d)}\textrm{ is important})^{2}\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{i}\textrm{ is }B_{i}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-important}\right)\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{j}\textrm{ is }B_{j}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-important}\right)\\
&\geq C\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbf{P}(B_{i,j}^{(d)}\textrm{ is important})\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{i}\textrm{ is }B_{i}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-important}\right)\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{j}\textrm{ is }B_{j}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-important}\right).\end{split}

Proof.

Note first that if
d\geq 2
, then

(5.13)

\begin{split}&\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{i}\textrm{ is important}\right)\\
={}&\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{i}\textrm{ is }B_{i}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-important}\right)\cdot\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{i,j}^{(d)}\textrm{ is }\textrm{important}\right)\cdot\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{i}^{(d+1)}\textrm{ is }B_{i}^{(d)}\textrm{-important}\right)\\
&\cdot\mathbf{P}\!\left(\textrm{arms link up }\big|B_{i}\textrm{ is }B_{i}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-important, }B_{i,j}^{(d)}\textrm{ is }\textrm{important, }B_{i}^{(d+1)}\textrm{ is }B_{i}^{(d)}\textrm{-important}\right).\end{split}

By Lemma
5.3
and Lemma
2.2
, we know that

(5.14)

R^{-5/4-\zeta}\leq\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{i}^{(d+1)}\textrm{ is }B_{i}^{(d)}\textrm{-important}\right)\leq R^{-5/4+\zeta}.

Moreover, an argument similar to the one in Lemma
5.4
gives us that the linking probability is bounded away from zero by an absolute constant. Thus, for
d\geq 2
we have

(5.15)

\mathbf{P}(B_{i}\textrm{ is important})\asymp_{R}\mathbf{P}(B_{i}\textrm{ is }B_{i}^{(d+1)}\textrm{-important})\cdot\mathbf{P}(B_{i,j}^{(d)}\textrm{ is important}).

Using Lemma
5.3
, we can extend this formula to the case
d=1
.

With the above formula in hand, we can see that in order to show the desired inequality, it suffices to show that there exists a constant
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(R)
such that for all
n,k

(5.16)

\begin{split}&\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{i}\textrm{ is important}\right)\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{j}\textrm{ is important}\right)\\
\geq{}&C^{\prime}\cdot\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{i,j}^{(d)}\textrm{ is important}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{i}\textrm{ is important}\right)\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{j}\textrm{ is important}\right).\end{split}

Denote by
