and rearranging. Here and in the final line, we use that
\frac{3}{2}k_{0}\leq k_{1}\leq(\frac{3}{2}+5\nu)k_{0}+K\leq\kappa+K
for
\nu\leq\frac{5}{12\cdot 8}
.

Recall from Corollary
5.8
that there exists a constant
C=C(\nu,R)
such that
\mathbf{q}_{n}\geq CR^{(\frac{3}{4}-\nu)\kappa}
. Finally, because
R^{-k_{0}}\leq n^{-1}\cdot R^{\kappa-k_{0}}\leq{d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}}(x,y)
, we obtain that

{D_{n}}(x,y)\leq C(R,\alpha,\nu,K)\!\left({d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}}(x,y)\right)^{\frac{5}{8}-12\nu}\mathbf{q}_{n}.

Now assume that
(3/2+5\nu)k_{0}\geq\kappa
. Then for
k\geq\widehat{K}
, we bound the number of Whitney boxes at level
k
by
R^{k+9-k_{0}}
, like in the case
K<k_{0}\leq k_{1}
. Then using the same bounds as above, we obtain a bound of

(7.9)

\begin{split}D_{n}(x,y)\leq{}&C(R,\alpha,\nu,K)\left(R^{-(\frac{5}{8}-6\nu)k_{0}}\mathbf{q}_{n}+R^{-(\frac{5}{12}-5\nu)k_{1}}\mathbf{q}_{n}+\sum_{k=\widehat{K}}^{\kappa}R^{k-k_{0}}R^{\frac{4\nu}{1+\nu}\kappa}\right)\\
\leq{}&C(R,\alpha,\nu,K)\left(R^{-(\frac{5}{8}-8\nu)k_{0}}\mathbf{q}_{n}+R^{\kappa-k_{0}}R^{\frac{4\nu}{1+\nu}\kappa}\right)\\
\leq{}&C(R,\alpha,\nu,K)\left(R^{-(\frac{5}{8}-8\nu)k_{0}}\mathbf{q}_{n}+R^{(\frac{3}{4}-\nu)\kappa}R^{(\frac{1}{4}+5\nu)\kappa-k_{0}}\right)\\
\leq{}&C(R,\alpha,\nu,K)\left(R^{-(\frac{5}{8}-8\nu)k_{0}}\mathbf{q}_{n}+R^{(\frac{1}{4}+5\nu)(\frac{3}{2}+5\nu)k_{0}-k_{0}}\mathbf{q}_{n}\right)\\
\leq{}&C(R,\alpha,\nu,K)R^{-(\frac{5}{8}-12\nu)k_{0}}\mathbf{q}_{n}\\
\leq{}&C(R,\alpha,\nu,K)\!\left({d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n}}(x,y)\right)^{\frac{5}{8}-12\nu}\mathbf{q}_{n}\end{split}

as desired.
∎

We now prove (
7.2
).

Lemma 7.5
.

Using the same parameters as before, on the event

(7.10)

\bigcap_{k=K}^{\widehat{K}}{\bigcap_{B^{\nu}\in\mathcal{B}_{{\lceil(1+\nu)k\rceil+4},n}^{R,+}}}E_{1}(B^{\nu},s_{k}),

the following holds. Let
x,y\in\Lambda_{n}
and let
\gamma
be a path from
x
to
y
with minimal diameter. Assume that
\dist(\gamma,\partial\Lambda_{n})\geq R^{-L}
. Then

(7.11)

D_{n}(x,y)\leq C(R,\alpha,\nu)\!\left(R^{(\frac{5}{4}+3\nu)K}+R^{(\frac{1}{4}+3\nu)L}\right)\mathbf{q}_{n}.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of the previous lemma, except that we do not need to alter the path
\gamma
since it does not come near the boundary. Let
k_{0}
be such that
n^{-1}R^{\kappa-k_{0}}\leq\diam(\gamma)<n^{-1}R^{\kappa-k_{0}+1}
. As before, the number of Whitney boxes of level
K
needed to cover
\gamma
is at most
C(R)R^{2K}
. For
K<k\leq L+3
, the number of Whitney boxes of level
k
needed to cover
\gamma
is at most
C(R)R^{k}
. Finally, since
\dist(\gamma,\partial\Lambda_{n})\geq R^{-L}
, the only Whitney boxes of level
k>L+3
in the covering of
\gamma
are at distance at most
n^{-1}R^{\kappa+2-k}
from
\{x,y\}
. As stated before, there are at most
32R^{4}
such boxes. Thus, (recall (
7.1
)),

(7.12)

\begin{split}D_{n}(x,y)\leq{}&C(R,\alpha,\nu)\cdot R^{2\nu K}\cdot R^{2K}\cdot R^{-(\frac{3}{4}-\nu)K}\mathbf{q}_{n}+C(R,\alpha,\nu)\sum_{k=\max\{K,k_{0}-4\}}^{L+3}R^{2\nu k}\cdot R^{k}\cdot R^{-(\frac{3}{4}-\nu)k}\mathbf{q}_{n}\\
&+\sum_{k=L+4}^{\widehat{K}}C(R,{\alpha},\nu)\cdot R^{2\nu k}\cdot 32R^{4}\cdot R^{-\!\left(\frac{3}{4}-\nu\right)k}\mathbf{q}_{n}+\sum_{k=\widehat{K}+1}^{\kappa}C(R,\alpha,\nu)\cdot 32R^{4}\cdot R^{\frac{4\nu}{1+\nu}\kappa}\\
\leq{}&C(R,\alpha,\nu)\!\left(R^{(\frac{5}{4}+3\nu)K}+R^{(\frac{1}{4}+3\nu)L}\right)\mathbf{q}_{n},\end{split}

which completes the proof. For the last inequality, we use that
\mathbf{q}_{n}\gtrsim R^{(\frac{3}{4}-\nu)\kappa}
to bound the last sum as explained in the proof of Lemma
7.4
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.1
.

Let
\nu>0
,
\eta=\frac{5}{8}-12\nu
, and
\varepsilon>0
. Set
\alpha^{\prime}=1
(say), choose the parameters for the events
F,G
as in Lemma
7.3
, and let
R
be sufficiently large depending on
\nu
, and let
p
be sufficiently small depending on
\nu,R
as in Lemma
7.3
. Let
K
be large enough that
CR^{-\nu K}<\varepsilon/2
and
C_{1}R^{-K}<\varepsilon/2
. Then the proof of (
7.1
) follows immediately from Lemmas
7.2
,
7.3
and
7.4
.

For the proof of (
7.2
), let
\nu,\alpha^{\prime}>0
. Let
R
be large enough depending on
\nu,
and
p
sufficiently small depending on
\nu,R
as in Lemma
7.3
. Let
M>0
and let
K
be the largest integer such that
R^{(5/4+3\nu)K}\leq M
. Then by Lemmas
7.3
and
7.5
, with probability at least
1-C_{1}R^{-\alpha^{\prime}K}\geq 1-C_{1}M^{-\alpha^{\prime}(5/4+3\nu)^{-1}}
, every path
\gamma
satisfies
d_{n}(\gamma(0),\gamma(1))\leq C(R,\alpha^{\prime},\nu)(M+\dist(\gamma,\partial\Lambda_{n})^{-(1/4+3\nu)})
. Since
\alpha^{\prime},\nu
were arbitrary, the proof is complete.
∎

Remark 7.6
.

Note that using the above proofs we can obtain Hölder continuity of these two metrics in any domain with sufficiently nice boundary, not just
[0,1)^{2}
. In particular, the proof works to show the analogue of Proposition
7.1
for the metrics
d_{Q,n}
and
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n}
for
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
.

7.2.
Proof of tightness

In this section, we prove Propositions
3.2
and
3.4
.

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Let
N^{\prime}\colon[0,\infty)\to\mathbf{N}
be some function. Let
C,\eta>0
. Let
\mathcal{K}_{0}=\mathcal{K}_{0}(N^{\prime})
be the set of all compact metric spaces
(X,d)
such that for all
\varepsilon>0
,
N_{(X,d)}(\varepsilon)\leq N^{\prime}(\varepsilon)
, with
N_{(X,d)}(\varepsilon)
the number of
\varepsilon
-balls required to cover
K
. Define
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}(N^{\prime},C,\eta)
to be the set of all metric-function spaces
(X,d,f)
such that
(X,d)\in\mathcal{K}_{0}
,
f\colon X\to Y
, with
Y
as defined at the beginning of Section
3.2
, and for all
(x,y)\in X
, we have
d(x,y)\leq Cd_{Y}(f(x),f(y))^{\eta}
. Then since
\mathcal{K}_{0}
is relatively compact in the GH topology, the space
\mathcal{K}
is relatively compact in the GHf topology by
[
4
, Proposition A.4]
.

First note that by Proposition
3.1
, there exists a coupling and a random function
N^{\prime}
such that
((\mathcal{X}_{Q,n}^{(m)})^{2},(d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n})^{2})\in\mathcal{K}_{0}(N^{\prime})
for all
m,n
. Furthermore, the map
\pi_{n}
is trivially Hölder continuous with respect to
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n}
with exponent
1
. Recall that
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}
was chosen in Section
3.2.4
such that
d_{Q,n}
is uniformly Hölder continuous with a fixed exponent (e.g.
\eta=1/4
) with high probability.
It then follows from Proposition
3.11
and Remark
7.6
that for all
\varepsilon>0
,
there exists
C=C(\varepsilon,\eta)
such that
\mathbf{P}(((\mathcal{X}_{Q,n}^{(m)})^{2},(d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n})^{2},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n})\in\mathcal{K}(N^{\prime},C,\eta))>1-\varepsilon
. This implies that
(\Lambda_{Q,n}^{2},(d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n})^{2},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n})
is tight.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
, let
m\in\mathbf{N}
and let
I\subset\mathbf{N}
be a subsequence such that the sequence
((\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n})^{2},(d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n})^{2},f_{Q,n})_{n\in I}
converges almost surely in GHf under a coupling with limit
((\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q})^{2},(d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q})^{2},f_{Q})
, where
f_{Q}(x,y)=(\pi(x),\pi(y),d_{Q}(x,y))
, with
\pi
the embedding of
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q}
into
[0,1]^{2}
and
d_{Q}
some function. We show that
d_{Q}
is a pseudo-metric and that
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n})_{n\in I}
converges to
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q},d_{Q},\pi)
in GHf distance.

We claim that by the GHf convergence of
((\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n})^{2},(d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n})^{2},f_{Q,n})_{n\in I}
and the
\GH
convergence of
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
, upon switching to a further subsequence, there exist isometric embeddings
\psi\colon\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q}\to W
,
\psi_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n}\to W
such that for each
x,y\in\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q}
there are sequences
(x_{n})_{n\in I},(y_{n})_{n\in I}
such that
x_{n},y_{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n}
,
d_{W}(\psi_{n}(x_{n}),\psi(x))\to 0
,
d_{W}(\psi_{n}(y_{n}),\psi(y))\to 0
, and
\lim_{n\in I}d_{Q,n}(x_{n},y_{n})=d_{Q}(x,y)
. To see this, let
(W,d_{W})
be a compact space and
\psi_{n},\psi
isometric embeddings such that
\Delta_{\Haus}(\psi_{n}(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n}),\psi(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q}))\to 0
(cf.
[
4
, Lemma A.2]
). Then the sequence
17
17

17

Here the notation
(f,\psi(u),\psi(v))
is shorthand for the function
(u,v)\mapsto(f(u,v),\psi(u),\psi(v))
.

((\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n})^{2},(d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n})^{2},f_{Q,n},\psi_{n}(u),\psi_{n}(v))_{n\in I}

is relatively compact, and converges in GHf along a further subsequence to

((\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q})^{2},(d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q})^{2},f_{Q},\psi(u),\psi(v)).

This implies the claim. Further, note that the continuity of
d_{Q}
implies that

d_{Q}(x,y)=\lim_{n\in I}d_{Q,n}(x_{n},y_{n})

for
any
sequence
x_{n}\to x
,
y_{n}\to y
in
d_{W}
. It follows that
d_{Q}
satisfies the triangle inequality. So
d_{Q}
is indeed a pseudo-metric.

We now show the GHf convergence
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n})\to(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q},d_{Q},\pi)
along
I
. Since the sequence
((\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n})^{2},(d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n})^{2},f_{Q,n})_{n\in I}
converges to
((\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q})^{2},(d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q})^{2},f_{Q})
, it implies in particular that the sequence
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n})_{n\in I}
is relatively compact in GHf.
We argue that any subsequential limit is isometric to
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q},d_{Q},\pi)
. By the argument in the previous paragraph, we have that

((\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n})^{2},(d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n})^{2},f_{Q,n},\psi_{n}(u),\psi_{n}(v))\to((\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q})^{2},(d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q})^{2},f_{Q},\psi(u),\psi(v))

along a further subsequence. Let
n
be sufficiently large that the GHf distance is at most
\varepsilon
. Then for each
(x_{n},y_{n})\in(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n})^{2}
, there exists
(x_{(x_{n},y_{n})},y_{(x_{n},y_{n})})\in(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q})^{2}
such that we have
d_{W}(\psi_{n}(x_{n}),\psi(x_{(x_{n},y_{n})}))<\varepsilon
,
d_{W}(\psi_{n}(y_{n}),\psi(y_{(x_{n},y_{n})}))<\varepsilon
,
|\pi_{n}(x_{n})-\pi(x_{(x_{n},y_{n})})|<\varepsilon
,
|\pi_{n}(y_{n})-\pi(y_{(x_{n},y_{n})})|<\varepsilon
, and
|d_{Q,n}(x_{n},y_{n})-d_{Q}(x_{(x_{n},y_{n})},y_{(x_{n},y_{n})})|<\varepsilon
.

Let
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n}\times\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q}
be the correspondence given by

\mathcal{R}=\{(x_{n},x):d_{W}(\psi_{n}(x_{n}),\psi(x))\leq\varepsilon,\,|\pi_{n}(x_{n})-\pi(x)|\leq\varepsilon\}.

Suppose that
(x_{n},x),(y_{n},y)\in\mathcal{R}
. Let
(x_{(x_{n},y_{n})},y_{(x_{n},y_{n})})\in(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q})^{2}
be as in the paragraph above, and note that in particular
(x_{n},x_{(x_{n},y_{n})})\in\mathcal{R}
,
(y_{n},y_{(x_{n},y_{n})})\in\mathcal{R}
, so that
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q}(x,x_{(x_{n},y_{n})})\leq 2\varepsilon
,
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q}(y,y_{(x_{n},y_{n})})\leq 2\varepsilon
. Then

(7.13)

\begin{split}&|d_{Q,n}(x_{n},y_{n})-d_{Q}(x,y)|\\
&\quad\leq|d_{Q,n}(x_{n},y_{n})-d_{Q}(x_{(x_{n},y_{n})},y_{(x_{n},y_{n})})|+|d_{Q}(x_{(x_{n},y_{n})},y_{(x_{n},y_{n})})-d_{Q}(x,y)|\\
&\quad\leq\varepsilon+d_{Q}(x_{(x_{n},y_{n})},x)+d_{Q}(y_{(x_{n},y_{n})},y)\\
&\quad\leq o(1)\quad\text{as }\varepsilon\searrow 0\end{split}

where in the last line we used that
d_{Q}
is continuous on
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q})
(in fact, we have
d_{Q}(u,v)\leq C(\omega)d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q}(u,v)^{\eta}
by Proposition
3.11
and Remark
7.6
). This shows that
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q,n},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n})
converges to
(\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{Q},d_{Q},\pi)
in GHf along the subsequence and uniquely identifies any subsequential limit.
∎

8.
Identification of the limit

In this section, we prove Theorem
3.12
and Corollary
3.13
. Let
(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
be a collection of subsequential limiting metrics as defined in Section
3.3
. We show that this is a
\CLE_{6}
metric as defined in Section
2.5
. We show compatibility in Section
8.1
. In Section
8.2
, we show the series and generalized parallel laws. Finally, Markovianity and translation invariance are proved in Sections
8.3
and
8.4
respectively. This completes the proof that
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
and
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
are
\CLE_{6}
metrics. We then show that
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
is a geodesic
\CLE_{6}
metric in Section
8.6
and show that
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
is a resistance
\CLE_{6}
metric in Section
8.7
by showing that it satisfies the edge weight locality property. This completes the proof of Theorem
3.12
. We give the proof of Corollary
3.13
in Section
8.8
, thus completing the proof of GH convergence.

Throughout this section, we let
J\subseteq\mathbf{N}
be a subsequence so that for each
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
, the sequence
((\Lambda_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n}))_{n\in J}
converges weakly to
(\Lambda_{Q},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q},d_{Q},\pi)
in the GHf topology as defined in Section
3.2
. In the proofs we will work with some coupling so that
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n})\to(\Lambda_{Q},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q},d_{Q},\pi)
almost surely along
J
for each
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
. Recall from Section
2.2
and from the proof of Proposition
3.4
that this means that there are isometric embeddings
\psi_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{n}\to W
,
\psi\colon\mathcal{X}\to W
in some ambient space
(W,d_{W})
so that they converge in
\Delta_{f}
with respect to
d_{W}
. In the following, we will say ‘
x_{n}
converges to
x
’ if they converge in
d_{W}
under an embedding. Finally, we will often assume that the coupling satisfies the property in Lemma
2.7
.

8.1.
Compatibility

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
V,V^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}
,
V\subset V^{\prime}
, and
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
such that for every
u\in V^{\prime}\setminus V
there is a point
z\in\overline{V}
that separates
u
from
x,y
in
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V^{\prime}}
. Then

(8.1)

\mathfrak{d}_{V^{\prime}}(x,y)=\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y).

Proof.

Let
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
Q\Supset V
,
Q^{\prime}\Supset V^{\prime}
, and
Q\Subset Q^{\prime}
. By the definition of
\mathfrak{C}
, the boundary of
V^{\prime}
is part of finitely many loops of
\Gamma
. Therefore, if
Q^{\prime}
is sufficiently close to
V^{\prime}
, then every
u\in\mathcal{X}\cap Q^{\prime}\setminus\overline{V^{\prime}}
is separated from
V^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{X}\cap Q^{\prime}
by a single point
z\in\partial V^{\prime}
. In particular, every
u\in\mathcal{X}\cap Q^{\prime}\setminus Q
is separated from
x,y
in
\mathcal{X}\cap Q^{\prime}
by a single point
z\in\overline{V}
. Indeed, if
u\in\overline{V^{\prime}}
, then by the assumption there is a point
z\in\overline{V}
separating
u
from
x,y
in
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V^{\prime}}
. Since
\mathcal{X}\cap Q^{\prime}\setminus\overline{V^{\prime}}
consists only of ‘dead ends’, the point
z
also separates
u
from
x,y
in
\mathcal{X}\cap Q^{\prime}
. On the other hand, if
u\in Q^{\prime}\setminus\overline{V^{\prime}}
, then it lies in one of the ‘dead ends’, so the same property holds again. See Figure
8.1
below.

Let
x_{n},y_{n}\in\mathcal{X}_{n}\cap Q
be converging to
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
, respectively, so that
d_{Q}(x,y)=\lim_{n}d_{Q,n}(x_{n},y_{n})
and
d_{Q^{\prime}}(x,y)=\lim_{n}d_{Q^{\prime},n}(x_{n},y_{n})
. (Note that by the GHf convergence and since
d_{Q},d_{Q^{\prime}}
are continuous, the limits do not depend on the choice of the approximating sequence of
x_{n},y_{n}
.) Ultimately, we want to use the compatibility property on the discrete metrics
d_{Q,n}
and
d_{Q^{\prime},n}
to say that the appropriate distances between
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
match. This will allow us to deduce compatibility for the limit metrics
d_{Q}
and
d_{Q^{\prime}}
, and in turn for
\mathfrak{d}_{V}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{V^{\prime}}
. To do so, we need that for every point
u\in\mathcal{X}_{n}\cap Q^{\prime}\setminus Q
, there exists a point
z\in\mathcal{X}_{n}\cap Q
so that every open path from
x_{n}
or
y_{n}
to
u
passes through
z
.

By the previous paragraph, this property holds in the continuum. We would like to use Lemma
2.7
to argue that it holds also in the discrete for
n
large enough. Since
V\Subset Q
, it is possible to find a
finite
set of cut points
z_{1},z_{2},\ldots\in\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
such that every
u\in\mathcal{X}\cap Q^{\prime}\setminus Q
is separated from
x,y
in
\mathcal{X}\cap Q^{\prime}
by some
z_{i}
(otherwise there would be infinitely many crossings of interface loops between
\partial V
and
\partial Q
which is impossible).

Therefore, by Lemma
2.7
, under a suitable coupling, there are points
z_{1,n},z_{2,n},\ldots\in\mathcal{X}_{n}\cap Q
such that the same property holds for
\mathcal{X}_{n}
for all
n
sufficiently large. Therefore, by the analogous compatibility property for the geodesic resp. resistance metric on percolation, we have
d_{Q^{\prime},n}(x_{n},y_{n})=d_{Q,n}(x_{n},y_{n})
for sufficiently large
n
, hence
d_{Q^{\prime}}(x,y)=d_{Q}(x,y)
. Since this holds for all
Q,Q^{\prime}
sufficiently close to
V,V^{\prime}
, respectively, the result follows by (
3.18
).

Figure 8.1.
This figure illustrates the compatibility property for regions
V\subseteq V^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{C}
. The black loops in the picture above represent the loops of
\Gamma
. The set
V
is represented by the green area; its boundary consists of segments of finitely many loops
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\Gamma
. The set
V^{\prime}\supseteq V
consists of the blue and green areas above; we can see that any point
u\in V^{\prime}\setminus V
is separated from
x
and
y
in
V^{\prime}
by exactly one of the red points pictured above. The purple and yellow shaded areas represent two sets
Q,Q^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{Q}
such that
V\Subset Q
,
V^{\prime}\Subset Q^{\prime}
and
Q\Subset Q^{\prime}
.
Because the boundaries of
V
and
V^{\prime}
consist of finitely many loops
\mathcal{G}
resp.
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, all boundary points except for the finitely many intersection points of distinct loops in
\mathcal{G}
resp.
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
(indicated in green resp. blue) lead to ‘dead ends’ in
\mathcal{X}
. Further, since
\overline{V}
and
\overline{V^{\prime}}
are required to be simply connected with respect to
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{\overline{V},\mathcal{G}}
(recall Section
2.5
), these intersection points have positive Euclidean distance from another. This ensures that if the Euclidean Hausdorff distance of
Q
resp.
Q^{\prime}
to
V
resp.
V^{\prime}
is sufficiently small, the regions that lie behind these intersection points (indicated in gray) are also ‘dead ends’ in
\mathcal{X}\cap Q
resp.
\mathcal{X}\cap Q^{\prime}
. Note that the total set of ‘dead ends’ need not be finite since the loops comprising the boundary of
V
resp.
V^{\prime}
have infinitely many self-intersection points. However, since
V\Subset Q
, only finitely many of them will exit
Q
. Only these are relevant for comparing
d_{Q,n}
and
d_{Q^{\prime},n}
– the dead ends fully contained in
Q
are also fully contained in
Q^{\prime}
, so their discrete approximations do not incur a change between
d_{Q,n}(x_{n},y_{n})
and
d_{Q^{\prime},n}(x_{n},y_{n})
.

∎

8.2.
Series and generalized parallel laws

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
V\in\mathfrak{C}
and let
x,y,z\in\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
such that
z
separates
x
from
y
in
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
. Then

(8.2)

\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y)=\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,z)+\mathfrak{d}_{V}(z,y).

Proof.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
. We first prove the series law for
d_{Q}
. Let
x_{n},y_{n}\in\Lambda_{n}\cap Q
such that
x_{n}\rightarrow x
and
y_{n}\rightarrow y
. In the proof of Proposition
8.1
, it was shown that if
x,y\in Q
are separated by
z\in Q
, then almost surely under a suitable coupling, there exists
n_{0}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
, there exists
z_{n}\in\Lambda_{n}\cap Q
such that
z_{n}
separates
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
and
z_{n}\rightarrow z
. It then immediately follows from the series law for
d_{n}
that

(8.3)

\begin{split}d_{Q}(x,y)&=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}d_{Q,n}(x_{n},y_{n})\\
&=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}d_{Q,n}(x_{n},z_{n})+d_{Q,n}(z_{n},y_{n})\\
&=d_{Q}(x,z)+d_{Q}(z,y).\end{split}

The statement for
V\in\mathfrak{C}
follows from (
3.18
).
∎

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
V\in\mathfrak{C}
and let
x,y,z_{1},\ldots,z_{M}\in V
such that
x
and
y
are separated in
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}\setminus\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{M}\}
. Let
K_{x}
be the connected component of
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}\setminus\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{M}\}
containing
x
, and let
V_{x}\in\mathfrak{C}
be the smallest region
such that
\overline{V_{x}}\supseteq K_{x}
.
18
18

18

In other words,
\overline{V_{x}}
is the union of
\overline{K_{x}}
with the components bounded by the loops within
\overline{K_{x}}
.
Then

(8.4)

\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y)\geq\frac{1}{M}\min_{i}\mathfrak{d}_{V_{x}}(x,z_{i}).

Proof.

Let
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
Q\Supset V_{x}
and
Q^{\prime}\Supset V
. By the argument in the first paragraph of the proof of Proposition
8.1
, for all
Q^{\prime}
small enough,
x
and
y
are also separated in
\mathcal{X}\cap Q^{\prime}\setminus\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{M}\}
. Then under a suitable coupling, there exist sequences
x_{n},y_{n},z_{1,n},\ldots,z_{M,n}\in\mathcal{X}_{n}
that converge to
x,y,z_{1},\ldots,z_{M}
respectively. Furthermore, by Lemma
2.7
, the
z_{i}
can be chosen such that
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
are separated by
z_{1,n},\ldots,z_{M,n}\in Q
for all
n
sufficiently large. Assume also that
Q
contains the connected component of
\mathcal{X}_{n}\cap Q^{\prime}\setminus\{z_{1,n},\ldots,z_{M,n}\}
containing
x_{n}
(by making
Q^{\prime}
smaller if necessary, and letting
n
be sufficiently large).
Then by the parallel law for the percolation geodesic and resistance metrics, and the monotonicity,

(8.5)

d_{Q^{\prime}}(x,y)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}d_{Q^{\prime},n}(x_{n},y_{n})\geq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{M}\min_{i}d_{Q,n}(x,z_{i})=\frac{1}{M}\min_{i}d_{Q}(x,z_{i}).

Since this holds for all sufficiently small
Q,Q^{\prime}
, the proof is complete.
∎

8.3.
Markovian Property

We prove the Markovian property. Let
U\subset[0,1]^{2}
be open and simply connected and recall the definitions of
\mathcal{L}
,
\Gamma_{\mathcal{L}}
,
\mathfrak{C}_{U}
,
U^{*}
and
\Gamma_{U^{*}}
from Section
2.5
.

Proposition 8.4
.

Almost surely, the conditional law of the collection
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
given
\{\mathcal{L}\}\cup\Gamma_{\mathcal{L}}\setminus\Gamma_{U^{*}}
and
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V^{\prime}},\mathfrak{d}_{V^{\prime}})_{V^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}_{[0,1]^{2}\setminus U}}
is measurable with respect to
U^{*}
.

Note that the characterization of the CLE
6
metric in Section
2.5
is given in terms of the non-nested CLE
6
corresponding to the cluster
\mathcal{X}
, but in the proof of Proposition
8.4
we will make use of the
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
resp.
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
metric defined on all clusters. This has the advantage that their local laws in disjoint regions are exactly independent (see Lemma
8.7
below). The main technical work is to deduce from this the stated Markovian property for
(\mathfrak{d}_{V})
.

We will need a few preparations. Suppose that
U\in\mathcal{Q}
. For each
\ell\in\mathbf{N}
we let

\displaystyle W_{1}^{*}(\ell)

\displaystyle:=\bigcup\{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}:Q\subset U^{*}\},

\displaystyle W_{2}^{*}(\ell)

\displaystyle:=W_{1}^{*}(\ell)\bigcup\{Q\cap U:Q\in\mathcal{B}_{\ell},\,Q\cap\partial U^{*}\neq\varnothing\}.

Note that for
\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}
, we have
W_{1}^{*}(\ell_{1})\subset W_{1}^{*}(\ell_{2})\subset W_{2}^{*}(\ell_{2})\subset W_{2}^{*}(\ell_{1})
. Furthermore, as
\ell\rightarrow\infty
, the Hausdorff distance between
W_{1}^{*}(\ell)
and
W_{2}^{*}(\ell)
tends to
0
. We define the following sigma-algebras:

\mathcal{G}_{\ell}=\sigma(\Gamma_{W_{2}^{*}(\ell)\setminus W_{1}^{*}(\ell)},U^{*}),\qquad\mathcal{G}=\bigcap_{\ell}\mathcal{G}_{\ell}.

Lemma 8.5
.

We have
\mathcal{G}=\sigma(U^{*})
up to a.s. equivalence.

Proof.

A slight technical point in the proof is that because we selected
\mathcal{L}
to be the outermost loop surrounding the point
(1/2,1/2)
, this induces a non-trivial conditioning in the unbounded complementary connected component of
\mathcal{X}
. To deal with this, we introduce a conditioning that leaves the law of
(\Gamma_{U},U^{*})
absolutely continuous.
Let us first consider the restriction of the law to the event that
U^{*}
touches
\partial U
. Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}
be the law of
\mathbf{P}
conditional on the event that
\mathcal{L}
surrounds
U
. By resampling the connections of the loops outside
U
(see
[
3
, Section 5.2]
), the
\mathbf{P}
-law of
(\Gamma_{U},U^{*})
restricted to event that
U^{*}
touches
\partial U
is absolutely continuous with respect to its
\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}
-law. Therefore it suffices to prove the lemma for
\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}
. Under
\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}
, each complementary connected component of
\{\mathcal{L}\}\cup\Gamma_{\mathcal{L}}
intersecting
U
is bounded, and the conditional law of
\Gamma
in each of them is just a conditionally independent CLE
6
. Therefore (see Lemma
2.5
) the conditional law of
\Gamma_{U}
in the connected components of
U\setminus\partial U^{*}
is a conditionally independent collection of CLE
6
, in particular, it depends only on
U^{*}
.

Since
\mathcal{G}
is measurable with respect to
U^{*}
and
\Gamma_{U}
, it suffices to show that
\Gamma_{U}
is conditionally independent of
\mathcal{G}
given
U^{*}
. The measurability of CLE
6
with respect to the quad-crossing sigma algebra (see Section
2.4.1
and
[
73
, Theorem 1.13(2)]
) implies that
\sigma(\Gamma_{U},U^{*})
is generated by
\sigma(U^{*})
and the collections
(\Gamma_{Q_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{Q_{i}\Subset U\setminus\partial U^{*}})_{i=1,\ldots,n}
19
19

19

Here,
\Gamma_{Q}\mathbf{1}_{Q\Subset U\setminus\partial U^{*}}
denotes the collection
\Gamma_{Q}
if
Q\Subset U\setminus\partial U^{*}
, and the empty collection otherwise.
where
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{n}\in\mathcal{Q}
and
Q_{i}\subseteq U
. By Lemma
2.5
and the previous paragraph (since the relative boundaries of
W_{1}^{*}(\ell),W_{2}^{*}(\ell)
in
U
are piecewise smooth), we have
\sigma(\Gamma_{U},U^{*})=\mathcal{G}_{\ell}\vee\sigma(\Gamma_{W_{1}^{*}(\ell)})\vee\sigma(\Gamma_{U\setminus W_{2}^{*}(\ell)})
, and the latter three are conditionally independent given
\sigma(U^{*})
. Therefore for each choice of
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{n}\in\mathcal{Q}
with
Q_{i}\subseteq U
, we have that
(\Gamma_{Q_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{\dist(Q_{i},\partial U^{*})>2^{-\ell}})_{i=1,\ldots,n}
is conditionally independent of
\mathcal{G}
. Since this holds for each
\ell\in\mathbf{N}
, the claim follows.

Finally, let us address the case when
\mathcal{L}\subseteq U
. Let
D_{1}
be the collection of bounded Euclidean connected components of
\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathcal{L}
, and let
D_{2}
be the unbounded component. For
j=1,2
, let
\Gamma_{D_{j}}
denote the collection of loops of
\Gamma
in
D_{j}
. It suffices to argue that for each
E\in\mathcal{G}
and each
j=1,2
we have
\mathbf{P}[E\mid\mathcal{L},\,\Gamma_{D_{3-j}}]\in\{0,1\}
almost surely. For
j=1
, since the conditional law of
\Gamma_{D_{1}}
is just a conditionally independent collection of CLE
6
, the claim follows already from the previous paragraph. For
j=2
, we use another change of measure where we sample a ball
B(z,r)
according to some probability measure (independently of
\Gamma
) and consider the innermost loop
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
surrounding
B(z,r)
. Then, given
\mathcal{L}\subseteq U
, the probability of selecting
B(z,r)
so that
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}=\mathcal{L}
is positive. Now, the conditional law of
\Gamma
outside
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
is exactly a CLE
6
, hence a similar argument as before completes the proof of this case.
∎

Lemma 8.6
.

For each
\ell\in\mathbf{N}
, the collection
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
is measurable with respect to the random variable
(U^{*},\Gamma_{W_{2}^{*}(\ell)},(d_{Q})_{Q\Subset W_{2}^{*}(\ell),Q\in\mathcal{Q}})
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathcal{X}\cap U
consists of the points in
\overline{U^{*}}
that are connected to
\partial U^{*}
by an open path. Also, the loops of
\Gamma_{U^{*}}
in the next level inside
\mathcal{L}
are exactly the ones that are connected to
\partial U^{*}
by an open path in
\overline{U^{*}}
.
These loops together with
\partial U^{*}
determine the collection
\mathfrak{C}_{U}
. Therefore the collection
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
is measurable with respect to
U^{*}
and
\Gamma_{U^{*}}
, which is measurable with respect to
U^{*}
and
\Gamma_{W_{2}^{*}(\ell)}
. Furthermore, by (
3.18
) the random variable
\mathfrak{d}_{V}
is measurable with respect to
(d_{Q})_{Q\Subset W_{2}^{*}(\ell),Q\in\mathcal{Q}}
, which completes the proof.
∎

We begin by noting that the metrics
d_{Q}
on the full CLE
6
configurations
\Gamma_{Q}
are exactly spatially independent (unlike their restrictions to the cluster
\mathcal{X}
which introduces a bias).

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{k}\in\mathcal{Q}
and define
W:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}Q_{i}
. Let
Q^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Q^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\Subset W^{c}
. Then the random variables
d_{Q_{1}},\ldots,d_{Q_{k}}
and
\Gamma_{W}
are independent of
\Gamma_{W^{c}}
and
d_{Q^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,d_{Q^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}
.

Proof.

The percolation configurations in
W
and
W^{c}
are independent, so the joint law of the random variables
(\Gamma_{W,n},d_{Q_{1},n},\ldots,d_{Q_{k},n})
and
(\Gamma_{W^{c},n},d_{Q^{\prime}_{1},n},\ldots,d_{Q^{\prime}_{k},n})
is given by some product measure
\mu_{n}\otimes\nu_{n}
. Without loss of generality and using Proposition
3.2
and Theorem
2.6
,
we can choose a subsequence
J
such that along
J
we have that
\mu_{n}
and
\nu_{n}
converge weakly to the laws of
(\Gamma_{W},d_{Q_{1}},\ldots,d_{Q_{k}})
and
(\Gamma_{W^{c}},d_{Q^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,d_{Q^{\prime}_{k}})
respectively in the product topology of the topologies induced by the quad-crossing topology and
\Delta_{\GHf}
(recall Sections
2.4.1
and
3.2
for the precise definition of the topologies).
Since these topologies are separable, it follows that
\mu_{n}\otimes\nu_{n}
converges to a product measure (see, e.g.
[
22
, Theorem 2.8]
), which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
W_{1}\subset W_{2}
be finite unions of sets in
\mathcal{Q}
. Let
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{k},Q^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Q^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
Q_{i}\subset W_{2}
and
Q^{\prime}_{i}\subset W_{2}^{c}
. Then
(\Gamma_{W_{2}},(d_{Q_{i}})_{i})
and
(\Gamma_{W_{1}^{c}},(d_{Q^{\prime}_{i}})_{i})
are conditionally independent given
\Gamma_{W_{2}\setminus W_{1}}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.5
,
\Gamma_{W_{1}^{c}}
is measurable with respect to
(\Gamma_{W_{2}^{c}},\Gamma_{W_{2}\setminus W_{1}})
. Moreover, by Lemma
8.7
,
(\Gamma_{W_{2}^{c}},(d_{Q^{\prime}_{i}})_{i})
is independent of
(\Gamma_{W_{2}},(d_{Q_{i}})_{i})
, and since
\Gamma_{W_{2}\setminus W_{1}}
is determined by
\Gamma_{W_{2}}
, they are also conditionally independent given
\Gamma_{W_{2}\setminus W_{1}}
. Combining these facts gives the result.
∎

We are now ready to complete the proof of Proposition
8.4
.

Proof of Proposition
8.4
.

We can and will assume that
U\in\mathcal{Q}
since the collection
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
is determined by
\{(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U_{1}}}\}_{U_{1}\in\mathcal{Q},\,U_{1}\Subset U}
. Indeed, upon showing this, for general
U
we have that
(U_{1}^{*})_{U_{1}\in\mathcal{Q},\,U_{1}\Subset U}
is a function of
U^{*}
and
\Gamma_{U^{*}}
, and therefore conditionally independent of
\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{U^{*}}
. This implies also that the metrics outside
U
are independent of
\Gamma_{U^{*}}
given
U^{*}
. Therefore
(U_{1}^{*})_{U_{1}\in\mathcal{Q},\,U_{1}\Subset U}
and hence also the collection of metrics in all the
U_{1}
are conditionally independent of the metrics outside
U
given
U^{*}
, concluding the proof in the general case.

Note that the collection
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V^{\prime}},\mathfrak{d}_{V^{\prime}})_{V^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}_{U^{c}}}
is determined by the countable collection of random variables
(\mathcal{X}\cap Q^{\prime},d_{Q^{\prime}}|_{\mathcal{X}})_{Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q},Q^{\prime}\cap\overline{U}=\varnothing}
. This is generated by the finite collections
(\mathcal{X}\cap Q_{i}^{\prime},d_{Q_{i}^{\prime}}|_{\mathcal{X}})_{i=1}^{k}
for
Q^{\prime}_{i}\cap\overline{U}=\varnothing
and is intersection stable. Thus, it suffices to show that
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
and
(\mathcal{X}\cap Q_{i}^{\prime},d_{Q_{i}^{\prime}}|_{\mathcal{X}})_{i=1}^{k}
are conditionally independent given
U^{*}
(note that the collection of loops
\{\mathcal{L}\}\cup\Gamma_{\mathcal{L}}\setminus\Gamma_{U^{*}}
is already determined by
(U^{*},\mathcal{X}\setminus\overline{U})
).

Let
\ell
be large enough such that
\dist(Q^{\prime}_{i},U)>2^{-\ell}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
. We first show that
\mathcal{X}\cap Q^{\prime}_{i}
is measurable with respect to
\Gamma_{W_{1}^{*}(\ell)^{c}}
. Note that
x\in Q^{\prime}_{i}
is in
\mathcal{X}
if and only if
x
is inside
\mathcal{L}
and
x
is not surrounded by any loop in
\Gamma_{\mathcal{L}}
. Note that the random variable
\mathcal{L}
is measurable with respect to
\Gamma_{W_{1}^{*}(\ell)^{c}}
. Indeed, since
U^{*}
is surrounded by
\mathcal{L}
, we must have
\mathcal{L}\subset W_{1}^{*}(\ell)^{c}
. Then
\mathcal{L}
is simply the outermost loop in
\Gamma_{W_{1}^{*}(\ell)^{c}}
that surrounds
(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
. Furthermore, if
\gamma
surrounds
x
, then
\gamma
is a loop that is not fully contained in
U
, so
\gamma\subset W_{1}^{*}(\ell)^{c}
. We conclude that the set of loops surrounding
x
is in
\Gamma_{W_{1}^{*}(\ell)^{c}}
, which suffices to show that
\mathcal{X}\cap Q^{\prime}_{i}
is measurable with respect to
\Gamma_{W_{1}^{*}(\ell)^{c}}
.

By Lemma
8.6
,
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
is measurable with respect to
(U^{*},\Gamma_{W_{2}^{*}(\ell)},d_{Q}\mathbf{1}_{Q\Subset W_{2}^{*}(\ell)})_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}
. This is generated by the finite collections
(d_{Q_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{Q_{i}\Subset W_{2}^{*}(\ell)})_{i=1}^{k}
for
Q_{i}\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
Q_{i}\Subset U
and is intersection stable.

Let
I^{*}
be the set of
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
for which
Q_{i}\Subset W_{2}^{*}(\ell)
. We claim that
(U^{*},\Gamma_{W_{2}^{*}(\ell)},(d_{Q_{i}})_{i\in I^{*}})
is conditionally independent of
(\Gamma_{W_{1}^{*}(\ell)^{c}},(d_{Q^{\prime}_{i}})_{i})
given
(\Gamma_{W_{2}^{*}(\ell)\setminus W_{1}^{*}(\ell)},U^{*})
. Note that there are finitely many possible choices of
W_{1}^{*}(\ell),W_{2}^{*}(\ell)
. Let
W_{1},W_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}
be a possible choice. By Lemma
8.8
, the random variables
(\Gamma_{W_{2}},(d_{Q})_{Q\subset W_{2}})
and
(\Gamma_{W_{1}^{c}},(d_{Q^{\prime}_{i}})_{i})
are conditionally independent given
\Gamma_{W_{2}\setminus W_{1}}
. Note that the event
\{W_{2}^{*}(\ell)\setminus W_{1}^{*}(\ell)=W_{2}\setminus W_{1}\}
is measurable with respect to
\Gamma_{W_{1}^{c}}
, and on this event
U^{*}
is measurable with respect to
\Gamma_{W_{1}^{*}(\ell)^{c}}=\Gamma_{W_{1}^{c}}
and
I^{*}
is measurable with respect to
U^{*}
. It follows that on the event
\{W_{2}^{*}(\ell)\setminus W_{1}^{*}(\ell)=W_{2}\setminus W_{1}\}
, the random variables
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
and
(\Gamma_{W_{1}^{*}(\ell)^{c}},(d_{Q^{\prime}_{i}})_{i})
are conditionally independent given
(\Gamma_{W_{2}^{*}(\ell)\setminus W_{1}^{*}(\ell)},U^{*})
. This holds for each possible choice of
W_{1},W_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\ell}
, implying the claim.

Now let
X=(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
,
Y=(\mathcal{X}\cap Q^{\prime}_{i},d_{Q^{\prime}_{i}}|_{\mathcal{X}})_{i})
. By the above,
X
and
Y
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
. Let
f,g
be bounded measurable functions of
X
and
Y
respectively. By conditional independence of
X
and
Y
given
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
, we have

\mathbf{E}[f(X)g(Y)\mid\mathcal{G}_{\ell}]=\mathbf{E}[f(X)\mid\mathcal{G}_{\ell}]\,\mathbf{E}[g(Y)\mid\mathcal{G}_{\ell}].

We have that
(\mathcal{G}_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbf{N}}
is a reverse filtration since
\Gamma_{A}
is measurable with respect to
\Gamma_{B}
for
A\subset B
. Further, by Lemma
8.5
, we have
\bigcap_{\ell\in\mathbf{N}}\mathcal{G}_{\ell}=\sigma(U^{*})
. Then by Lévy’s reverse martingale convergence theorem, we get

\begin{split}\mathbf{E}[f(X)g(Y)\mid U^{*}]&=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\mathbf{E}[f(X)g(Y)\mid\mathcal{G}_{\ell}]\\
&=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\mathbf{E}[f(X)\mid\mathcal{G}_{\ell}]\,\mathbf{E}[g(Y)\mid\mathcal{G}_{\ell}]\\
&=\mathbf{E}[f(X)\mid U^{*}]\,\mathbf{E}[g(Y)\mid U^{*}]\end{split}

which was what we wanted to show.
∎

8.4.
Translation invariance

Proposition 8.9
.

Let
U\subset[0,1]^{2}
be open and simply connected. Let
D_{1}
be the support of
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
and
D_{2}
be the support of
U^{*}
. Let
\mu_{U}(\cdot\mid\cdot)
be a probability kernel from
D_{2}
to
D_{1}
such that
\mu_{U}(\cdot\mid U^{*})
is a.s. equal to the conditional law of
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
given
U^{*}
. Then for each
z\in\mathbf{C}
such that
U+z\subset[0,1]^{2}
one can choose versions of
\mu_{U}
and
\mu_{U+z}
such that for all events
A
in
D_{1}
and
u\in D_{2}
we have

\mu_{U}(A\mid u)=\mu_{U+z}(A+z\mid u+z).

where
A+z
denotes the event that the translated metrics
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}-z,\mathfrak{d}_{V}(\cdot+z,\cdot+z))_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U+z}}
are in the event
A
.

As in Section
8.3
, we will make use of the
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
resp.
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
metric defined on all clusters. This has the advantage that their local laws are exactly translation invariant which we argue in Lemma
8.10
below.

Recall from Section
2.5
that
\mathfrak{C}_{U}
contains only regions whose boundary loops are in
\{\mathcal{L}\}\cup\Gamma_{\mathcal{L}}
and that the metric
\mathfrak{d}_{V}
is only defined on the set
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
. For the following, it will be useful to consider the set
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{U}
of regions that is defined like
\mathfrak{C}_{U}
, except that the boundary loops
\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m}\}
of
V\in\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{U}
can be any set in
\Gamma
. That is, the regions
V\in\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{U}
can belong to any of the clusters of the nested
\CLE_{6}

\Gamma
and not just
\mathcal{X}
.
Furthermore, we extend the definition of
\mathfrak{d}_{V}
to each
V\in\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{U}
by

\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y)=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\Supset V\\
Q\in\mathcal{Q}\end{subarray}}d_{Q}(x,y),\quad x,y\in\overline{V}.

Below, we will write
\mathfrak{d}-z
to denote the metric
\mathfrak{d}(\cdot+z,\cdot+z)
.

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}
and
z\in\mathbf{R}^{2}
be such that
U^{\prime}+z\subseteq[0,1]^{2}.
Then

(8.6)

(\Gamma_{U^{\prime}},(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}})\overset{d}{=}(\Gamma_{U^{\prime}+z},(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}+z}})-z.

This result is intuitively obvious, since the percolation configuration itself is translation invariant. However, there are two subtleties. First of all,
\Lambda_{U^{\prime},n}
and
\Lambda_{U^{\prime}+z,n}
may actually not be the same graphs, but instead instead differ by one row or column. This extra row or column does not matter for the
\CLE
convergence: both configurations can be coupled to converge to the same limit because the boundary
3
-arm exponent is larger than
1
. Furthermore, for a
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
with
Q\Subset U^{\prime}
, it is actually not true that
Q+z\in\mathcal{Q}
if
z
is not a dyadic number. However, we can still approximate
V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U^{\prime}}
and
V+z\in\mathfrak{C}_{U^{\prime}+z}
by
Q_{1}\in\mathcal{Q}
,
Q_{1}\Subset U^{\prime}
and
Q_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}
,
Q_{2}\Subset U^{\prime}+z
in such a way that the limiting metrics
\mathfrak{d}_{V}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{V+z}
satisfy
\mathfrak{d}_{V}=\mathfrak{d}_{V+z}-z
.

Proof of Lemma
8.10
.

Let
z\in[0,1]^{2}
. Note that
\Lambda_{U^{\prime},n}
and
\Lambda_{U^{\prime}+z,n}
are polygon-shaped grids whose side lengths differ by at most 1 vertex. Couple them such that the percolation configurations agree except for possibly the rightmost or topmost strip, where percolation is sampled i.i.d. Then
\Gamma_{U^{\prime},n}
and
\Gamma_{U^{\prime}+z,n}
can be chosen such that they converge almost surely to
\Gamma_{U^{\prime}}
and
\Gamma_{U^{\prime}+z}
, with
\Gamma_{U^{\prime}}=\Gamma_{U^{\prime}+z}-z
because there are almost surely no boundary pivotals since the half-plane
3
-arm exponent is larger than
1
(cf. Lemma
2.2
and the proof of Lemma
2.7
).

Now let
V\in\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}}
. Then
V+z\in\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{U^{\prime}+z}
. Let
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
with
U^{\prime}\Supset Q\Supset V
, and let
Q_{\pm}\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
U^{\prime}+z\Supset Q_{+}\supset Q+z\supset Q_{-}\Supset V+z
. Note that if
Q+z\in\mathcal{Q}
, we can simply choose
Q_{\pm}=Q+z
, but this is not guaranteed to be the case if
z
is not dyadic. Now, let
x,y\in V
. Then there exist
x_{n},y_{n}\in\Lambda_{U^{\prime},n}
and
(x+z)_{n},(y+z)_{n}\in\Lambda_{U^{\prime}+z,n}
such that
x_{n},y_{n},(x+z)_{n},(y+z)_{n}
converge to
x,y\in\Lambda_{U^{\prime}}
and
x+z,y+z\in\Lambda_{U^{\prime}+z}
respectively. Note that
x_{n}+z
is not necessarily in the grid
\Lambda_{U^{\prime}+z,n}
, so we choose
(x+z)_{n}
to be the vertex in
\Lambda_{U^{\prime}+z,n}
that is coupled with
x_{n}
, and similarly for
y_{n}
(recall that every vertex in the interior of
\Lambda_{U^{\prime}+z,n}
(resp.
\Lambda_{U^{\prime},n}
) is coupled with some vertex in
\Lambda_{U^{\prime},n}
(resp.
\Lambda_{U^{\prime}+z,n}
)).
Then by monotonicity we get that

d_{Q_{+},n}((x+z)_{n},(y+z)_{n})\leq d_{Q,n}(x_{n},y_{n})\leq d_{Q_{-},n}((x+z)_{n},(y+z)_{n}).

Since
Q_{\pm}\in\mathcal{Q}
, we can take a subsequential limit and obtain

d_{Q_{+}}(x+z,y+z)\leq d_{Q}(x,y)\leq d_{Q_{-}}(x+z,y+z).

Taking
Q\downarrow V
, we can also let
Q_{\pm}\downarrow V+z
, which shows that

\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y)=\mathfrak{d}_{V+z}(x+z,y+z),

which completes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to prove that the conditional law of
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
is translation invariant. For the proof, we first note that
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
is a translation invariant measurable function of
(\Gamma_{U^{\prime}},(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{U}})
and
U^{*}
. The former is translation invariant by Lemma
8.10
. To deal with the conditioning on
U^{*}
, we first define a selection procedure of a local cluster
that depends only on
\Gamma_{U^{\prime}}
and is translation-invariant, and then use that the conditional law given
U^{*}
is the same as the conditional given
\widetilde{U}^{*}
on the positive probability event that
\widetilde{U}^{*}=U^{*}
.

Proof of Proposition
8.9
.

First, we note that it suffices to assume that
\dist(U,\partial[0,1]^{2})\geq\varepsilon
and
\dist(U+z,\partial[0,1]^{2})\geq\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon>0
. Indeed, this is because the collection
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
is determined by
\{(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U_{1}}}\}_{U_{1}\in\mathcal{Q},\,U_{1}\Subset U}
, and the conditional law of
(U_{1}^{*})_{U_{1}\in\mathcal{Q},\,U_{1}\Subset U}
given
U^{*}
is translation-invariant.

Let
U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}
be such that
U\subseteq U^{\prime}
and
U^{\prime}+z\subseteq[0,1]^{2}
, and let
A^{\prime}=\{u\in[0,1]^{2}\mid 0<\dist(u,U^{\prime})<\varepsilon\}
. Let
\Gamma_{U^{\prime}},\Gamma_{A^{\prime}}
be as defined in Section
2.4.1
. By Lemma
8.10
, we have that the law of
(\Gamma_{A^{\prime}},\Gamma_{U^{\prime}},(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{U}})
agrees with the law of the translation of
(\Gamma_{A^{\prime}+z},\Gamma_{U^{\prime}+z},(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{U+z}})
.
Since CLE is locally finite,
\Gamma_{A^{\prime}}
a.s. contains only finitely many strands that cross
A^{\prime}
. These strands divide the annulus
A^{\prime}
into a finite number of components which are alternatingly open and closed (in the percolation sense). Let us select a random collection
\mathcal{I}
of open components in a translation-invariant manner (e.g. by flipping a coin for each open component). We let
\widetilde{U}^{*}
be defined analogously to
U^{*}
but for the clusters that are connected to
\mathcal{I}
inside
U^{\prime}
, i.e.
\widetilde{U}^{*}
is the region containing the loops of
\Gamma
that are entirely contained in
U
and are connected to
\mathcal{I}
by an open path within
U^{\prime}
. (In the case
\mathcal{I}=\varnothing
, we can e.g. take instead the open cluster inside
U^{\prime}
whose outer boundary has the smallest Hausdorff distance to
\partial U^{\prime}
.) Further, we let
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{U}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{U}
be the regions contained in
\widetilde{U}^{*}
where
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{U}
is defined above Lemma
8.10
. Also let
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
be the set of points inside
\overline{\widetilde{U}^{*}}
that are connected to
\mathcal{I}
by an open path within
U^{\prime}
. Since the function that associates to
(\Gamma_{A^{\prime}},\Gamma_{U^{\prime}},\mathcal{I})
the region
\widetilde{U}^{*}
is translation-invariant, we have that the law of
(\Gamma_{A^{\prime}},\Gamma_{U^{\prime}},\widetilde{U}^{*},(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{U}})
is translation-invariant. Hence, its disintegration along
\widetilde{U}^{*}
is also translation-invariant. See Figure
8.2
for an illustration.

Figure 8.2.
The figure above illustrates the construction of the translation-invariant set
\widetilde{U}^{*}
that equals
U^{*}
with positive probability. The figure portrays a set
U
whose boundary is represented by the bold black curve above. The set
U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}
is such that
U\subseteq U^{\prime}
, and
A^{\prime}
is the set of all points that are not in
U^{\prime}
but are within ‘triangular’
L^{1}
-distance
\varepsilon
from
U^{\prime}
. The outermost loop
\mathcal{L}
surrounding the origin is represented by the red curve above. The yellow area inside the set
U
represents the set
U^{*}
. The areas shaded in green and pink inside the annulus
A^{\prime}
represent the open (in a percolation sense) clusters that cross
A^{\prime}
. In the case above, for the set
\widetilde{U}^{*}
to be equal to
U^{*}
, we need to select
\mathcal{I}
to be precisely the collection of the green crossing clusters above. Note that in contrast to
U^{*}
, the law of
\widetilde{U}^{*}
is translation-invariant since it does not ‘see’ which of the loops is
\mathcal{L}
.

Let
F(U^{\prime})
be the event that
\mathcal{X}\cap U^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
and
\mathcal{X}
is either not entirely contained in
U^{\prime}
or that
\mathcal{X}\subseteq U^{\prime}
but is the cluster whose outer boundary has the smallest Hausdorff distance to
\partial U^{\prime}
(note that the case when
\mathcal{X}\cap U^{\prime}=\varnothing
is trivial). On
F(U^{\prime})
, given
U^{*}
and
\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{U^{*}}
, the conditional probability of the event
E=\{\widetilde{U}^{*}=U^{*}\}
is positive. Therefore, on
E
, the conditional law of
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
given
\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{U^{*}}
agrees a.s. with the conditional law of
(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{U}}
given
\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{\widetilde{U}^{*}}
which is the same as the conditional law given just
\Gamma_{A^{\prime}\cup U^{\prime}}\setminus\Gamma_{\widetilde{U}^{*}}
. But since
\mathcal{I}
is sampled conditionally independently of
\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{U^{*}}
, the conditional law of
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
given
\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{U^{*}}
does not depend on
E
. By Proposition
8.4
, this is the same as the conditional law of
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
given just
U^{*}
. Therefore, its translation invariance follows from the translation invariance of the conditional law of
(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{U}}
given
\Gamma_{A^{\prime}\cup U^{\prime}}\setminus\Gamma_{\widetilde{U}^{*}}
. This shows the result on the event
F(U^{\prime})
. The general case follows since the collection
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U}}
is determined by
\{(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{U_{1}}}\mathbf{1}_{F(U_{1})}\}_{U_{1}\in\mathcal{Q},\,U_{1}\Subset U}
, and the conditional law of
(U_{1}^{*})_{U_{1}\in\mathcal{Q},\,U_{1}\Subset U}
given
U^{*}
is translation-invariant.
∎

8.5.
Non-degeneracy

We have now verified that
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
is a CLE
6
metric in the sense defined in Section
2.5
. Note that the definition of a CLE
6
metric does not rule out the case that
\mathfrak{d}_{V}=0
for each
V
. We now show that this is not the case, and in fact each
\mathfrak{d}_{V}
is a true metric defined on
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
.

Lemma 8.11
.

Let
(\Lambda_{Q},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q},d_{Q},\pi)_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}
be a subsequential limit of the
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{Q,n},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n})_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}
, and let
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
be defined as in (
3.18
). We almost surely have
\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y)>0
for each
V\in\mathfrak{C}
and
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
with
x\neq y
.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.13
, we only need to rule out that
\mathfrak{d}_{V}=0
for each
V\in\mathfrak{C}
. Suppose that this is the case. By a local absolute continuity argument (see
[
67
, Section 7.2]
), this implies also that the global metric
d
satisfies
d=0
a.s. So we just need to rule out this case.

Let
\mathcal{E}(\Lambda)
be the analogous event to
\mathcal{E}(\Lambda_{n})
, but for the
\CLE_{6}
configuration rather than percolation (see Section
3.2.1
). It is straightforward to show that this has positive probability.
Assume this event holds. Then by Lemma
2.7
, under a suitable coupling, almost surely there exists
n_{0}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
, the event
\mathcal{E}(\Lambda_{n})
holds. Let
u_{n}
and
v_{n}
be the left- and rightmost touching points as described in Section
3.2.1
. Then, again by Lemma
2.7
,
u_{n}
and
v_{n}
converge in
d
to the left- and rightmost touching points of the closed touching
\CLE_{6}
clusters that are connected to the top and bottom sides, which we call
u
and
v
. By definition of the constants
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}
, there exists a constant
p\in(0,1)
such that
1
is the conditional
p
-th quantile of
d_{n}(u_{n},v_{n})
. Since
d(u,v)=\lim_{n\in J}d_{n}(u_{n},v_{n})
, we have that
1
is also the conditional
p
-th quantile of
d(u,v)
. In particular, the latter is not identically
0
.
∎

8.6.
Geodesic metric

We have shown that
(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
is a non-degenerate
\CLE
metric. We now show that if
d=d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
, then it is a geodesic
\CLE
metric. We first show in Lemma
8.12
that
\mathfrak{d}_{V}
is a geodesic metric in the usual sense of the word, i.e., the distance between two points equals the
\mathfrak{d}_{V}
-length of the shortest path. We then show in Proposition
8.13
that the
\mathfrak{d}_{V}
-length in fact equals the
d
-length. Recall that
d=d_{[0,1]^{2}}
simply denotes the subsequential limit of the global metrics
d_{n}
. Also recall the notation
P(x,y;\overline{V})=P(x,y;\overline{V};\Gamma)
for the set of admissible paths in
\overline{V}
from
x
to
y
(equivalently, continuous paths in
(\overline{V},d^{{\mathrm{path}}})
) introduced above (
2.17
).

Lemma 8.12
.

Let
d=d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
. For each
V\in\mathfrak{C}
, the metric
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})
is a geodesic metric space, i.e., for all
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
,

(8.7)

\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y)=\inf_{\gamma\in P(x,y;\overline{V})}L_{\mathfrak{d}_{V}}(\gamma),

and the infimum above is attained by some path
\gamma\in P(x,y;\overline{V})
.

Proof.

Throughout this proof, we write
d=d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
. Let
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
. We say a metric is a length metric if (
8.7
) holds but if the infimum is not necessarily attained for all pairs of points in the space. Then, since the GH limit of length metrics is a length metric, it follows that
(\mathcal{X}\cap Q,d_{Q})
is a length metric space.
By Proposition
3.2
, Lemma
8.11
, and the compactness of
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},d^{{\mathrm{path}}})
by
[
4
, Lemma 1.10]
, we have that
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
is topologically equivalent to
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}
,
hence the continuous paths in
d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
are the same as the continuous paths in
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}
.
Additionally, we have argued in the proof of Proposition
8.1
that for
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
with
Q\Supset V
in a small enough neighborhood of
V
, all simple admissible paths in
Q
from
x\in\overline{V}
to
y\in\overline{V}
must lie entirely inside
\overline{V}
. Thus, by the exact same proof as in Proposition
8.1
we have that
d_{Q}(x,y)=\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y)
. It follows that
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})
is also a compact space and

(8.8)

\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y)=d_{Q}(x,y)=\inf_{\gamma\in P(x,y;Q)}L_{d_{Q}}(\gamma)=\inf_{\gamma\in P(x,y;\overline{V})}L_{\mathfrak{d}_{V}}(\gamma).

The Hopf-Rinow theorem then implies that it is in fact a geodesic metric space.
∎

Proposition 8.13
.

Let
d=d^{{\mathrm{geo}}}
.
For every
V\in\mathfrak{C}
and
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
, we have

(8.9)

\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y)=\inf_{\gamma\in P(x,y;\overline{V})}L_{d}(\gamma).

Proof.

Let
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
. By Lemma
8.12
, it suffices to show that for every
\gamma\in P(x,y;\overline{V})
,
L_{\mathfrak{d}_{V}}(\gamma)=L_{d_{[0,1]^{2}}}(\gamma)
. In particular, by (
8.8
), it suffices to show that for every
Q\Supset V
,
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
, we have
L_{d_{Q}}(\gamma)=L_{d_{[0,1]^{2}}}(\gamma)
.

We have shown in Lemma
8.11
that
d_{[0,1]^{2}}\big|_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathfrak{d}_{D}
is a true metric on
\mathcal{X}
. Therefore
\delta:=d_{[0,1]^{2}}(\gamma,\partial Q)>0
.
Let
t_{0},\ldots,t_{M}
such that
d_{[0,1]^{2}}(\gamma(t_{i-1}),\gamma(t_{i}))<\delta/2
for all
1\leq i<M
. Let
x^{0}_{n},\ldots,x^{M}_{n}\in\Lambda_{n}
that such that
(x^{i}_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
converges to
\gamma(t_{i})
. Then for
n
sufficiently large, we have
d_{Q,n}(x^{i-1}_{n},x^{i}_{n})<\delta/2
and
d_{Q,n}(x_{n}^{i},\partial\Lambda_{Q,n})>3\delta/4
. This means the graph-length minimizing path between
x^{i-1}_{n}
and
x_{n}^{i}
lies entirely inside
Q
and in particular
d_{Q,n}(x_{n}^{i-1},x_{n}^{i})=d_{n}(x_{n}^{i},x_{n}^{i-1})
. Thus,

(8.10)

\sum_{i=1}^{M}d_{Q}(\gamma(t_{i-1}),\gamma(t_{i}))=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{M}d_{Q,n}(x^{i-1}_{n},x^{i}_{n})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{M}d_{n}(x^{i-1}_{n},x^{i}_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{M}d_{[0,1]^{2}}(\gamma(t_{i-1}),\gamma(t_{i})).

This implies that
L_{d_{Q}}(\gamma)=L_{d_{[0,1]^{2}}}(\gamma)
, which completes the proof.
∎

8.7.
Resistance metric

We show that if
d=d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
, then
(\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
is a
\CLE_{6}
resistance metric. We first show in Proposition
8.14
that
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})
is a resistance metric space. We then prove the edge weight locality property in Proposition
8.15
.

Proposition 8.14
.

Let
d=d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
. For each
V\in\mathfrak{C}
, the metric
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})
is a resistance metric space.

Proof.

Throughout this proof, we write
d=d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
. Let
V\in\mathfrak{C}
and
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
with
Q\Supset V
be small enough such that for all
x,y\in\overline{V}
, each simple admissible path in
Q
from
x
to
y
must lie entirely inside
\overline{V}
, as in the previous proof. Then
(Q,d_{Q})
is a resistance metric space, since the GH limit of resistance metric spaces is a resistance metric space
[
48
]
. Furthermore, the exact same proof as in Proposition
8.1
shows that
\mathfrak{d}_{V}(x,y)=d_{Q}(x,y)
on
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
, and by Lemma
8.11
we have that
\mathfrak{d}_{V}
is indeed a metric on
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
.
So
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})
is a resistance metric space.
∎

Proposition 8.15
.

Let
d=d^{{\mathrm{res}}}
. Suppose that
V,V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}
are such that
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}=(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V_{1}})\cup(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V_{2}})
and
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V_{1}})\cap(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V_{2}})=\{a,b\}
. There exists a countable set
\mathcal{Z}_{V}=\{z_{1},z_{2},\ldots\}\subseteq\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
that contains
a,b
, is dense with respect to
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{\overline{V}}
, and such that the following holds for sufficiently large
m
. Let
A=\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}\}
,
A_{1}=A\cap\overline{V_{1}}
,
A_{2}=A\cap\overline{V_{2}}
, and let
w
(resp.
w_{1}
,
w_{2}
) be the weight function associated with
\mathfrak{d}_{V}(\cdot,\cdot)|_{A}
(resp.
\mathfrak{d}_{V_{1}}(\cdot,\cdot)|_{A_{1}}
,
\mathfrak{d}_{V_{2}}(\cdot,\cdot)|_{A_{2}}
). Then

•

w(x,y)=w_{1}(x,y)
for each
x,y\in A_{1}
;

•

w(x,y)=w_{2}(x,y)
for each
x,y\in A_{2}
;

•

w(x,y)=0
for each
x\in A_{1}\setminus\{a,b\}
,
y\in A_{2}\setminus\{a,b\}
;

•

w(a,b)=0
.

Proof.

Recall that
(\overline{V},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{\overline{V}})
is compact and therefore separable. Let
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
be loops inside
\overline{V}
. Then the sets of touching points
\gamma\cap\gamma^{\prime}
and
\gamma\cap\partial V
are separable. Further, for each
\gamma
, the set of self-intersection points is separable. Let
\mathcal{Z}_{V}
be any countable set containing a dense subset of
\gamma\cap\gamma^{\prime}
and of
\gamma\cap\partial V
for each such
\gamma\neq\gamma^{\prime}
and a dense subset of the set of self-intersection points of each
\gamma
. We show that this set satisfies the properties stated in the lemma.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
with
Q\Supset V
be small enough so that
\mathfrak{d}=d^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{Q}
on
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
, as in the previous proof. Recall that if
A\subseteq\mathcal{X}\cap Q
is a finite set and
w
is the weight function associated with
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{Q}|_{A}
, then
w(x,y)=\lim_{n}w_{n}(x_{n},y_{n})
where
x_{n},y_{n}
converge respectively to
x,y
in the coupling described at the beginning of the section and
w_{n}
is the weight function associated with
d^{{\mathrm{res}}}_{Q,n}|_{A_{n}}
where
A_{n}=\{x_{n}\mid x\in A\}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{n}\cap Q
. (See the proof of
[
66
, Lemma 3.29]
.)

Let
V_{1},V_{2}
and
\{a,b\}
be as in the lemma and let
m
be large enough such that
\{a,b\}\subset A
and such that
a
and
b
are separated by points in
A\setminus\{a,b\}
in both
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V_{1}}
and
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V_{2}}
.
Note that
w(x,y)=0
if
x
and
y
are separated by
A\setminus\{x,y\}
(by the previous paragraph and the analogous property on graphs together with Lemma
2.7
).
Then the fourth point is automatically true. Furthermore, for each
x\in A_{1}\setminus\{a,b\}
and
y\in A_{2}\setminus\{a,b\}
, the points
x
and
y
are separated inside
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
by
a
and
b
. So the third point is also true. For the first and second points, note that
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V_{1}}
and
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V_{2}}
are separated in
\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V}
by
a
and
b
, and again apply Lemma
2.7
.
∎

8.8.
Proof of uniqueness

Proof of Corollary
3.13
.

By Proposition
3.4
, for each subsequence
I\subset\mathbf{N}
, there exists a further subsequence
J\subset I\subset\mathbf{N}
such that on a suitable coupling
(\Lambda_{Q,n},d_{Q,n},\pi_{n})_{n\in J}
converges for each
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
. Define the subsequential limits
(\mathcal{X}\cap\overline{V},\mathfrak{d}_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{C}}
as in (
3.18
). Then by Theorem
3.12
, this collection is a
\CLE_{6}
metric. Now let
(\Lambda,d)
and
(\Lambda,\widetilde{d})
be two subsequential limits of
(\mathcal{X}_{n},d_{n})
with corresponding subsequences
J
and
\widetilde{J}
, coupled with the same
\CLE_{6}
. By Theorems
2.14
and
2.15
, there exists a constant
c
such that
\mathfrak{d}_{V}=c\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{V}
for each
V\in\mathfrak{C}
. By a local absolute continuity argument (see
[
67
, Section 7.2]
), this implies also that
d=c\widetilde{d}
.
We have seen in the proof of Lemma
8.11
that
1
is the conditional
p
-th quantile of both
d(u,v)
and
\widetilde{d}(u,v)
on the event
\mathcal{E}(\Lambda)
. Therefore
c=1
.
∎

9.
Measure convergence

In this section, we prove the convergence of the vertex counting measures
\mu^{(m)}_{n}
defined in (
1.4
) to the CLE
6
gasket measure
\mu^{(m)}
on
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}
, thus completing the last step of the proof of Theorem
1.1
. Throughout this entire section, we write
\mathcal{X}
(resp.
\mathcal{X}_{n}
) to denote any of the clusters
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}
(resp.
\mathcal{X}^{(m)}_{n}
) in order to ease the notation, and do the same thing for
\mu
(resp.
\mu_{n}
). We first show that
(\mathcal{X}_{n},d_{n},\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
converges in the GHP distance to some limiting metric-measure space
(\mathcal{X},d,\widetilde{\mu})
in Section
9.1
. We then prove that
\widetilde{\mu}
is a multiple of the CLE
6
gasket measure
\mu
by showing that it satisfies (
2.16
), and conclude the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

9.1.
GHP convergence

Recall from Section
2.4.2
that the measure convergence results from
[
39
]
were only stated for measures of the form
\mu^{A}
, where
A
is some annulus. Therefore, we first express the desired measures on
\mathcal{X}_{n}
and
\mathcal{X}
in terms of annulus measures. In order to do this, we introduce the following tiling of
[0,1]^{2}
by annuli.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
be the set of annuli of the form
2^{-\ell}\!\left([i-1,i+2]\times[j-1,j+2]\right)\setminus([i,i+1]\times[j,j+1])
for
0\leq i,j<2^{\ell}
. Then
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
is a tiling of
[0,1]^{2}
, i.e., each point in
[0,1]^{2}
is contained in the inner face of exactly one annulus in
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
.
Define
\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}:=\sum_{A\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\mu_{n}^{A}
, where
\mu_{n}^{A}
is defined as in Section
2.4.2
. It follows from the convergence in Theorem
2.8
that there is a coupling such that
\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}
converges almost surely in Prokhorov distance on
[0,1]^{2}
to some limiting measure
\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}
that is a function of the
\CLE_{6}
configuration
\Gamma
. Define the measure
\widetilde{\mu}^{\ell}:=\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\circ\pi
on
\mathcal{X}
where
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]^{2}
is the embedding map. (Note that this measure is well-defined since the set of double points of
\pi(\mathcal{X})
has measure
0
by Lemma
2.10
.)

Recall the definition of the measure
\mu_{n}=\frac{1}{n^{2}A(1,n)}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{n}}\delta_{x}
on
\mathcal{X}_{n}
. Suppose that
\ell
is large enough so that
2^{-\ell+1}<\diam(\mathcal{X}_{n})
. Then
\mu_{n}=\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\circ\pi_{n}
where
\pi_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{n}\to[0,1]^{2}
is the embedding map. Indeed, note that since the map
\pi_{n}
is defined only on the cluster
\mathcal{X}_{n}
, the measure
\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\circ\pi_{n}
counts only the points in
\mathcal{X}_{n}
). Further, a point
x
in
\mathcal{X}_{n}
is counted by
\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\circ\pi_{n}
if the cluster
\mathcal{X}_{n}
crosses the annulus in
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
whose inner face contains
x
. If
\diam(\mathcal{X}_{n})>2^{-\ell+1}
, then every
x\in\mathcal{X}_{n}
is counted, so it follows that
\mu_{n}=\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\circ\pi_{n}
.

Suppose that we are on the event that
\diam(\mathcal{X})>2^{-\ell+1}
. Since
\mathcal{X}_{n}
converges to
\mathcal{X}
in Hausdorff distance, it follows that
\diam(\mathcal{X}_{n})>2^{-\ell+1}
for
n
sufficiently large almost surely. The goal of this section is to deduce the convergence of
\mu_{n}
to
\widetilde{\mu}^{\ell}
in the GHP distance, and in Section
9.2
we identify
\widetilde{\mu}^{\ell}
with the CLE
6
gasket measure
\mu
defined in
[
64
]
. Note that this implies in particular that
\widetilde{\mu}^{\ell}
does not depend on
\ell
as long as we are on the event that
\diam(\mathcal{X})>2^{-\ell+1}
.

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
d\in\{d^{{\mathrm{path}}},d^{{\mathrm{geo}}},d^{{\mathrm{res}}}\}
. There exists a coupling under which, on the event
\{\diam(\mathcal{X})>2^{-\ell+1}\}
, the sequence
(\mathcal{X}_{n},d_{n},\mu_{n})_{n\in I}
converges to
(\mathcal{X},d,\widetilde{\mu}^{\ell})
in the GHP distance.

By the weak convergence of
(\mathcal{X}_{n},d_{n},\pi_{n})\to(\mathcal{X},d,\pi)
in the GHf topology (Corollary
3.13
), we can further couple the
\mathcal{X}_{n}
and
\mathcal{X}
so that we have both
\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\to\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}
and
(\mathcal{X}_{n},d_{n},\pi_{n})\to(\mathcal{X},d,\pi)
almost surely. In particular, there exists a metric space
(Y,d_{Y})
and isometries
\psi_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}_{n}\to Y
and
\psi\colon\mathcal{X}\to Y
such that
(\psi_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{n}))_{n\in I}
converges to
\psi(\mathcal{X})
in Hausdorff distance. We show that the pushforward measure
(\mu_{n}\circ\psi^{-1}_{n})_{n}
converges to
\widetilde{\mu}^{\ell}\circ\psi^{-1}
in the Lévy-Prokhorov distance. This then implies convergence of
(\mathcal{X}_{n},d_{n},\mu_{n})_{n}
to
(\mathcal{X},d,\widetilde{\mu}^{\ell})
in the GHP distance. The proposition follows immediately from the following.

For a set
A
and some metric
d
, we define
A^{\varepsilon}:=\{x:d(x,A)<\varepsilon\}
to be the
\varepsilon
-fattening
of
A
.

Lemma 9.2
.

The following holds almost surely on the event
\{\diam(\mathcal{X})>2^{-\ell+1}\}
under the coupling described above. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists
n_{0}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
and all measurable
A\subset Y
,

\mu_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A))\leq\widetilde{\mu}^{\ell}(\psi^{-1}(A^{\varepsilon}))+\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\mu}^{\ell}(\psi^{-1}(A))\leq\mu_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A^{\varepsilon}))+\varepsilon.

We first prove the following result. We point out that in the statements below, the notation
A^{\delta}
refers to the
d_{Y}
-metric whereas
\pi(A)^{\delta}
refers to the Euclidean metric.

Lemma 9.3
.

For every
\delta>0
, there exists
n_{0}=n_{0}(\delta)
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
and measurable
A\subset Y
,

\pi_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A))\subset\pi(\psi^{-1}(A^{\delta}))^{\delta},\qquad\pi(\psi^{-1}(A))\subset\pi_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A^{\delta}))^{\delta}.

Proof.

It follows from the GHf convergence
(\mathcal{X}_{n},d_{n},\pi_{n})\to(\mathcal{X},d,\pi)
that there exists
n_{0}=n_{0}(\delta)
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{X}_{n}
, there exists
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
such that
d_{Y}(\psi_{n}(x),\psi(x^{\prime}))<\delta
and furthermore,
\|\pi_{n}(x)-\pi(x^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq\delta
. So for
\psi_{n}(x)\in A
, we have
\psi(x^{\prime})\in A^{\delta}
and
\pi_{n}(x)\in\pi(\psi^{-1}(A^{\delta}))^{\delta}
, which shows the first inclusion. Furthermore, for all
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
, there exists
x\in\mathcal{X}_{n}
such that
d_{Y}(\psi_{n}(x),\psi(x^{\prime}))<\delta
and furthermore,
\|\pi_{n}(x)-\pi(x^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq\delta
. So for
\psi(x^{\prime})\in A
, we have
\psi_{n}(x)\in A^{\delta}
and
\pi(x^{\prime})\in\pi_{n}(\psi^{-1}(A^{\delta}))^{\delta}
, which shows the second inclusion.
∎

Before we prove Lemma
9.2
, we need another auxiliary lemma. This is because we have assumed
\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\to\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}
in the Prokhorov distance with respect to the Euclidean metric, but for the statement of Lemma
9.2
we need the upper bound in terms of the mass of
\pi(\psi^{-1}(A^{\varepsilon}))
where the fattening is in the
d_{Y}
-metric, so its mass can be smaller than that of
\pi(\psi^{-1}(A))^{\delta}
. The next lemma ensures that for
\delta>0
sufficiently small it is not much smaller.

Lemma 9.4
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon)>0
and
n_{0}=n_{0}(\varepsilon)
such that for all measurable
A\subset Y
and
n\geq n_{0}
, we have

\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\!\left(\pi(\psi^{-1}(A))^{\delta}\setminus\pi(\psi^{-1}(A^{\varepsilon}))\right)<\varepsilon,\qquad\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\!\left(\pi_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A))^{\delta}\setminus\pi_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A^{\varepsilon}))\right)<\varepsilon

Proof.

We first prove the first inequality. The idea is as follows. Suppose that
x\in\pi(\psi^{-1}(A))^{\delta}
is in the support of
\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}
, and let
x^{\prime}\in\pi(\psi^{-1}(A))
be the closest point in Euclidean distance. In case (the preimage of)
x
is also close to (the preimage of)
x^{\prime}
in the
d
-distance, then it is contained in
A^{\varepsilon}
when
\delta
is small enough.
If not, there are at least two disjoint open arms from
x,x^{\prime}
, respectively, that are separated by two closed arms. However, the measure
\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}
assigns zero mass to
4
-arm points.

Assume without loss of generality that
\psi^{-1}(A)
is non-empty. Let
x\in[0,1]^{2}
and let
B\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
be the annulus with inner face containing
x
. Using Proposition
2.9
it is straightforward to check that
\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}
is indeed only supported on points that have an open arm to the boundary of
B
. Assume that
x
has an open arm to the outer boundary of
B
, and
\|x-\pi(\psi^{-1}(A))\|_{2}<\delta
, and
x\not\in\pi(\psi^{-1}(A^{\varepsilon}))
.

Consider the Euclidean box
B(x;\delta)
of radius
\delta
around
x
. Then
B(x;\delta)
intersects
\pi(\psi^{-1}(A))
, so
B(x;\delta)
has at least one open arm to distance

\diam(\pi(\mathcal{X}))/2-\delta\geq 2^{-\ell-1}-\delta
via
\mathcal{X}
. Recall that
x
was assumed to have an open arm to the boundary of
B
. Note that
x
can be on the same cluster
\mathcal{X}
or on a different cluster with Euclidean diameter at least
2^{-\ell}
. Assume first that
x\not\in\pi(\mathcal{X})
. Then
B(x;\delta)
has another disjoint open arm to distance
2^{-\ell}\geq 2^{-\ell-1}-\delta\geq\varepsilon-\delta
for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small. Assume now that
x\in\pi(\mathcal{X})
. Then since
x\not\in\pi(\psi^{-1}(A^{\varepsilon}))
,
x
has another disjoint open arm that goes at least distance
\varepsilon/2
with respect to the metric
d
. By Hölder continuity of
d
with respect to
d^{{\mathrm{path}}}
, there exists some constant
c\geq 1
and exponent
\alpha>0
such that almost surely for all
x
, this arm goes at least Euclidean distance
c\varepsilon^{\alpha}
. We conclude that for
\varepsilon,\delta
sufficiently small, the box
B(x,\delta)
has two disjoint open arms that go at least Euclidean distance
c\varepsilon^{\alpha}-\delta
(which are separated by two closed arms).

Since the 2-arm exponent is strictly larger than the 1-arm exponent, it follows from Lemma
2.10
that

\begin{split}\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\!\left(\pi(\psi^{-1}(A))^{\delta}\setminus\pi(\psi^{-1}(A^{\varepsilon}))\right)\leq\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\!\left({\{x:B(x,\delta)\text{ has 2 arms to distance $c\varepsilon^{\alpha}-\delta$}\}}\right)\rightarrow 0\end{split}

as
\delta\downarrow 0
, which completes the proof.

The proof of the the second inequality is similar. Let
n
be large enough so that the GHf distance between
(\mathcal{X}_{n},d_{n},\pi_{n})
and
(\mathcal{X},d,\pi)
is smaller than
\delta
. Let
x_{n}
be such that
x_{n}
has an open arm in
\Lambda_{n}
to the outer boundary of
B
, and
\|x_{n}-\pi_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A))\|_{2}<\delta
, and
x_{n}\not\in\pi_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A^{\varepsilon}))
. Then there are points
x,x^{\prime}\in B(x_{n};2\delta)
with
d(x,x^{\prime})>\varepsilon-\delta
, and we have two
arms from
B(x;2\delta)
to distance
c\varepsilon^{\alpha}-2\delta
.

Therefore

\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\!\left(\pi_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A))^{\delta}\setminus\pi_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A^{\varepsilon}))\right)\leq\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\!\left(\{x:B(x;2\delta)\text{ has 2 arms to distance $c\varepsilon^{\alpha}-\delta$}\}\right).

for some constants
c,\alpha>0
. Again, using Lemma
2.10
and the convergence
\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\to\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}
, we can make the last part arbitrarily small by choosing
\delta
small enough and
n
large enough.
∎

We are now ready to prove Lemma
9.2
.

Proof of Lemma
9.2
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and let
A\subset Y
be measurable. Then by Prokhorov convergence of
\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}
to
\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}
, on the event that
\diam(\mathcal{X})>2^{-\ell+1}
, we have that for all
\delta>0
, there exists
n_{0}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}(\delta)
,

\begin{split}\mu_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A))=\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(\pi_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A)))\leq\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(\pi_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A))^{\delta})+\delta\end{split}

and

\widetilde{\mu}^{\ell}(\psi^{-1}(A))=\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(\pi(\psi^{-1}(A)))\leq\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(\pi(\psi^{-1}(A))^{\delta})+\delta.

Now by Lemma
9.3
, there exists
n_{1}=n_{1}(\delta)
such that for all
n\geq n_{1}(\delta)
,

\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(\pi_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A))^{\delta})\leq\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(\pi(\psi^{-1}(A^{\delta}))^{2\delta})

and

\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(\pi(\psi^{-1}(A))^{\delta})\leq\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}_{n}(\pi_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A^{\delta}))^{2\delta}).

Choosing
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon/2)\wedge\varepsilon/2
and
n_{2}=n_{2}(\varepsilon/2)
from Lemma
9.4
, we obtain that for all
n\geq n_{2}

\begin{split}\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(\pi(\psi^{-1}(A^{\delta}))^{2\delta})\leq\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(\pi(\psi^{-1}(A^{\varepsilon/2+\delta})))+\varepsilon/2\leq\widetilde{\mu}^{\ell}(\psi^{-1}(A^{\varepsilon}))+\varepsilon\end{split}

and

\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(\pi_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A^{\delta}))^{2\delta})\leq\mu_{n}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(\pi_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A^{\varepsilon/2+\delta})))+\varepsilon/2\leq\mu_{n}(\psi_{n}^{-1}(A^{\varepsilon}))+\varepsilon

which completes the proof.
∎

9.2.
Characterization of the limit

Let
B\subset[0,1]^{2}
be a ball or a box, let
k\in\mathbf{N}
and recall the definitions of
Y_{k}^{A}(B)
and
Y^{(m)}_{k}(B)
from Section
2.4.2
. For the latter, we again drop the superscript and just write
Y_{k}(B)
for the sake of brevity.
Recall that we have defined the measure
\widetilde{\mu}^{\ell}
in Section
9.1
from the annulus measures
\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}
, and shown that it is the weak limit of the counting measure
\mu_{n}
on the event
\{\diam(\mathcal{X})>2^{-\ell+1}\}
. In particular, we can consistently define the measure
\widetilde{\mu}:=\widetilde{\mu}^{\ell}
where
\ell
can be chosen to be any random number with
\ell>-\log_{2}(\diam(\mathcal{X}))+1
.

Our goal now is to identify
\widetilde{\mu}
with the conformally covariant CLE
6
gasket measure defined in
[
64
]
. For this, we prove that
\widetilde{\mu}
satisfies the same box-counting characterization as the annulus measure stated in Proposition
2.9
and the CLE
6
gasket measure stated in Proposition
2.11
.

Proposition 9.5
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that for all balls or boxes
B\subset[0,1]^{2}
,

\widetilde{\mu}(\pi^{-1}(B))=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{cY_{k}(B)}{2^{2k}A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},1)}\qquad\text{in probability}.

Recall that we have a similar characterization for the annulus measures
\mu^{A}
from Proposition
2.9
for domains
B
that have rectifiable boundary. However, since
\pi(\mathcal{X})
does not have rectifiable boundary, it is not immediately clear that the same holds for the measure
\widetilde{\mu}^{\ell}
, which is supported on
\pi(\mathcal{X})
. The proof of this proposition follows from two key ingredients. We first show that we can approximate
\pi(\mathcal{X})
by rectifiable domains in an appropriate way. We then finish the proof by using the fact that the measure does not give mass to the set of
2
-arm points (Lemma
2.10
).

Proof of Proposition
9.5
.

For the ease of notation, we let
\ell\in\mathbf{N}
be arbitrary and implicitly restrict to the event
\{\diam(\mathcal{X})\geq 2^{-\ell+1}\}
throughout the proof. Since the probability of this event goes to
1
as
\ell\to\infty
, the desired convergence in probability then follows.

Define
Y_{k}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(B):=\sum_{A\in\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}Y^{A}_{k}(B)
. It follows from Proposition
2.9
that for all balls or boxes
B
,

(9.1)

\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(B)=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{cY^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}_{k}(B)}{2^{2k}A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},1)}\qquad\text{in $L^{2}$}.

Note that the formula above can be defined for any set that is a finite union of disjoint balls or boxes. Recall also that for each
B
we have
\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(\partial B)=0
a.s. by
[
39
, Corollary 4.16]
. We would like to conclude that (
9.1
) remains true when
B
is replaced by
B\cap\pi(\mathcal{X})
. Since the boundary of
\mathcal{X}
is not rectifiable, we argue as follows.

Fix
m>\ell
. Let
B_{m}^{\mathcal{X}}
be the union of the set of boxes
B^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{B}_{m}
such that
B^{\prime}
has non-empty intersection with
\pi(\mathcal{X})
. Then it follows from (
9.1
) that

\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(B^{\mathcal{X}}_{m}\cap B)=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{cY^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}_{k}(B^{\mathcal{X}}_{m}\cap B)}{2^{2k}A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},1)}\qquad\text{in $L^{2}$}.

Let
B_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{k}
be such that
B_{1}\subset B
and
2B_{1}
intersects
\pi(\mathcal{X})
. Then for
k>m
,
B_{1}\subset B_{m}^{\mathcal{X}}
and
2B_{1}
has an open arm that goes distance at least
2^{-\ell}
. So
Y_{k}(B)\leq Y_{k}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(B_{m}^{\mathcal{X}}\cap B)
. Now
suppose that a box
B_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{k}
inside
B_{m}^{\mathcal{X}}\cap B
is counted in
Y^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}_{k}(B^{\mathcal{X}}_{m})
but
2B_{1}
does not intersect
\pi(\mathcal{X})
. Let
B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{m}
be the box containing
B_{1}
and having non-empty intersection with
\pi(\mathcal{X})
. Then
B^{\prime}
has at least two open arms that go to distance
2^{-\ell}
and are separated by two closed arms. Thus, for all
\ell
and all
k>m

\displaystyle Y_{k}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(B_{m}^{\mathcal{X}}\cap B)

\displaystyle\geq Y_{k}(B)

\displaystyle\geq Y_{k}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(B_{m}^{\mathcal{X}}\cap B)-Y_{k}^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\!\left(\bigcup\{B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{m}:\text{$B^{\prime}$ has four arms that go distance $2^{-\ell}$}\}\right).

For each
\varepsilon>0
, by Lemma
2.10
we can choose
m
sufficiently large so that

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\mathbf{E}\!\left[\left\lvert\frac{cY_{k}(B)}{2^{2k}A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},1)}-\frac{cY^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}_{k}(B_{m}^{\mathcal{X}}\cap B)}{2^{2k}A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},1)}\right\rvert\mathbf{1}_{\{\diam(\mathcal{X})\geq 2^{-\ell}\}}\right]\leq\varepsilon

Similarly, again by Lemma
2.10
, we have

\begin{split}&\mathbf{E}[\lvert\widetilde{\mu}^{\ell}(\pi^{-1}(B))-\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}(B^{\mathcal{X}}_{m}\cap B)\rvert\mathbf{1}_{\{\diam(\mathcal{X})\geq 2^{-\ell}\}}]\\
&\leq\mathbf{E}\!\left[\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\!\left(\bigcup\{B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{m}:\text{$B^{\prime}$ has four arms that go distance $2^{-\ell}$}\}\right)\right]\\
&\leq\varepsilon.\end{split}

This concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1
and Corollary
2.12
.

Let
\widetilde{\mu}=\mu^{\mathcal{H}_{\ell}}\circ\pi
be the measure defined above, and let
\mu
be the conformally covariant gasket measure on
\mathcal{X}
.
Let
(B_{i})_{i\in\mathbf{N}}
be a countable basis of boxes in
[0,1]^{2}
. By Lemma
2.10
and
[
66
, Proposition 2.6]
, both measures give
0
mass to the set of double points of
\mathcal{X}
, hence they are characterized by their values on
(\widetilde{\mu}(\pi^{-1}(B_{i}))_{i\in\mathbf{N}}
. By Proposition
9.5
and Proposition
2.11
, both
\widetilde{\mu}(\pi^{-1}(B_{i}))
and
\mu(\pi^{-1}(B_{i}))
are given by the following limits in probability

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}(\pi^{-1}(B_{i}))

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{cY_{k}(B_{i})}{2^{2k}A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},1)},

\displaystyle\mu(\pi^{-1}(B_{i}))

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\widetilde{c}Y_{k}(B_{i})}{2^{(91/48)k}}.

It follows that
\frac{2^{2k}A^{\CLE}_{1}(2^{-k},1)}{2^{(91/48)k}}\to c
for some constant
c>0
(since both measures are non-trivial). In particular,
