c\widetilde{\mu}(\pi^{-1}(B_{i}))=\mu(\pi^{-1}(B_{i}))
almost surely for each
i
, hence
c\widetilde{\mu}=\mu
. It is then straightforward to check that the GHP convergence of
(\mathcal{X}_{n},d_{n},\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
and the GHf convergence of
(\mathcal{X}_{n},d^{{\mathrm{path}}}_{n},d_{n},\pi_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
imply the GHPf convergence of
(\mathcal{X}_{n},d_{n},\mu_{n},\pi_{n})_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
.
∎

10.
Distance, resistance, and displacement exponents

In this section, we are going to prove Corollaries
1.2
–
1.4
.

Proof of Corollary
1.2
.

We first show show that
\mathbf{E}[S_{n}\mid\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n}]\lesssim{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Let
R\in\mathbf{N}
be large,
\nu>0
be small, and consider percolation on the enlarged box
B^{+}=[-1,2]\times[0,1]
. Note that both the chemical distance as well as the effective resistance between
\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}
and
\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n}
is the same on
[0,1)^{2}
as on
B^{+}
, since adding extra sites to the left and right does not influence the distances. Let
A_{K}
be the event that there exists a left-to-right crossing of
\Lambda_{n}
that does not come within distance
R^{-2K+3}
of the top and bottom of
[0,1]^{2}
. If
\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n}
but
A_{K}
does not hold, then there must be a closed path that comes within distance
R^{-2K+3}
of the top and bottom side of
[0,1]^{2}
, and an open path from left to right that fits above or below this closed path. Thus, there exists a box of size
R^{-2K+3}
that shares one side with the top (resp. bottom) side of
[0,1]^{2}
, and that has two open arms and one closed arm coming out of it that go distance at least
\frac{1}{2}
in the half-plane. The number of such boxes is at most
\lesssim R^{2K}
, and the probability of such an arm event occurring for one box is at most
\lesssim R^{-(4-\nu)K}
for every
\nu>0

by Lemma
2.2
, since the half-plane
3
-arm exponent is
2
. On the other hand, if there exists a left-to-right crossing
\gamma
of
\Lambda_{n}
that does not come within distance
R^{-2K+3}
of the top and bottom, then
\gamma
is a path between two points
x,y
in
B^{+}
such that
\gamma
does not come within distance
R^{-2K+3}
of
\partial B^{+}
. Furthermore,
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{-1}S_{n}
is bounded from above by
d_{n}(x,y)
. Let
\alpha>1
and let
C_{1}=C_{1}(\alpha,\nu)
from Proposition
7.1
. Then there exists
C_{3}
such that
R^{K-1}\geq C_{1}(R^{K-C_{3}}+\dist(\gamma,\partial B^{+})^{-\frac{1}{4}-\nu})
for all
K
sufficiently large, since
R^{\frac{1}{4}\cdot 2K}<R^{K-1}
. Thus, by Proposition
7.1
, we have
\mathbf{P}({\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{-1}S_{n}>R^{K-1},A_{K})\lesssim R^{-\alpha K}
. Here we use that
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}=\mathbf{q}_{n}(\mathfrak{p})
for some sufficiently small
\mathfrak{p}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\,\mathbf{E}[{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{-1}S_{n}\mid\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\sum_{K\geq 1}R^{K}\mathbf{P}({\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{-1}S_{n}>R^{K-1}\mid\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\sum_{K\geq 1}R^{K}\!\left(\mathbf{P}(A^{c}_{K}\mid\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n})+\mathbf{P}({\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{-1}S_{n}>R^{K-1},A_{K}\mid\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\sum_{K\geq 1}C(R,\alpha,\nu)R^{K}(R^{2K}R^{-(4-\nu)K}+R^{-\alpha K})\leq C(R,\alpha,\nu)

for
\alpha
large enough, for some constant
C(R)
whose value may change from line to line. Here we also use that
\mathbf{P}(\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n})
is bounded from below uniformly in
n
.

We now show that
\operatorname{quant}_{q}[S_{n}\mid\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n}]\gtrsim{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}
. Let
A_{M,n}
be the event that there is a set of at most
M
points
u_{1,n},\ldots,u_{M,n}
that separate
\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}
from the line
\{\frac{1}{4}\}\times[0,1]
and a set of at most
M
points
v_{1,n},\ldots,v_{M,n}
that separate
\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n}
from the line
\{\frac{3}{4}\}\times[0,1]
. Define
A_{M}
,
u_{1},\ldots,u_{M}
and
v_{1},\ldots,v_{M}
similarly for
\Lambda
. By Lemma
2.7
, we can find a coupling such that almost surely on the event
A_{M}\cap\{\partial_{L}[0,1]^{2}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}[0,1]^{2}\}
the events
A_{M,n}\cap\{\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n}\}
hold for
n
large enough, with the
u_{i,n},v_{i,n}
converging to
u_{i},v_{i}
, respectively, for all
i
. Note that by the BK inequality, there exists
p_{0}<1
such that
\mathbf{P}(A_{M,n}^{c})\leq p_{0}^{M}
for all
n
. Furthermore, by the parallel law, we have
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{-1}S_{n}\geq\frac{1}{M}\min_{i,j}d_{n}(u_{i,n},v_{j,n})
. Under the coupling above, the latter converges almost surely to
\frac{1}{M}\min_{i,j}d(u_{i},v_{j})
, which is a.s. positive since the Euclidean distance between the
u_{i}
and
v_{j}
is at least
\frac{1}{2}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{P}({\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{-1}S_{n}\leq x\mid\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\!\left[\mathbf{P}\!\left(\frac{1}{M}\min_{i,j}d_{n}(u_{i,n},v_{j,n})\leq x,\,A_{M,n}\mid\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n}\right)+\mathbf{P}(A_{M,n}^{c}\mid\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n})\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\mathbf{P}\!\left(\frac{1}{M}\min_{i,j}d(u_{i},v_{j})\leq x,\,A_{M}\mid\partial_{L}\Lambda\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda\right)+Cp_{0}^{M}.

for some
C>0
. The last line can be made smaller than
q
upon choosing
M
sufficiently large depending on
q
and
x
sufficiently small depending on
q
and
M
. We conclude that
\operatorname{quant}_{q}[S_{n}\mid\partial_{L}\Lambda_{n}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda_{n}]\geq x{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}
.

It remains to show that
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}=n^{\beta+o(1)}
. Let
R\in\mathbf{N}
and let
Q\subset[0,1]^{2}

be a parallelogram that is small enough such that
RQ\subset[0,1]^{2}
. Recall that
X_{Q,n}
is defined analogously to
X_{n}
, but on the parallelogram
Q
and conditional on the event
\mathcal{E}(\Lambda_{Q,n})
. Then

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{-1}\med(X_{Q,n})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathfrak{q}}_{Rn}^{-1}\med(X_{Q,Rn})=\med(X_{Q}).

If we let
\Gamma
denote a
\CLE_{6}
in
Q
and
d_{Q}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
the metric
d_{Q}
with respect to
\Gamma
, then by
[
66
, Theorem 1.2]
and
[
67
, Theorem 1.5]
, we have that
d_{RQ}(R\cdot,R\cdot;R\Gamma)=R^{\beta}d_{Q}(\cdot,\cdot;\Gamma)
. Thus,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathfrak{q}}_{Rn}^{-1}\operatorname{median}(X_{Q,Rn})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}}{{\mathfrak{q}}_{Rn}}{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{-1}\operatorname{median}(X_{RQ,n})

\displaystyle=\!\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}}{{\mathfrak{q}}_{Rn}}\right)\operatorname{median}(X_{RQ})

\displaystyle=\!\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}}{{\mathfrak{q}}_{Rn}}\right)R^{\beta}\operatorname{median}(X_{Q}).

This shows that for each
\varepsilon>0
and
R>1
, there exists
n_{0}=n_{0}(\varepsilon,R)
such that

(10.1)

R^{\beta-\varepsilon}{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}\leq{\mathfrak{q}}_{Rn}\leq R^{\beta+\varepsilon}{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}

for all
n\geq n_{0}
. Note that
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}
is not obviously increasing, so we need to do a bit more work in order to conclude that
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}=n^{\beta+o(1)}
as
n\to\infty
. Recall that
\kappa=\kappa(n)
was defined such that
R^{\kappa-1}\leq n\leq R^{\kappa}
. We show that
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}\asymp{\mathfrak{q}}_{R^{\kappa-1}}\asymp{\mathfrak{q}}_{R^{\kappa}}
, which will imply the desired result.

Write
Q=[0,q]^{2}
and let
Q^{\prime}=[0,\frac{q}{2R}]\times[0,2Rq]
. Then for every
n
, we can embed
\Lambda_{Q^{\prime},n}
and
\Lambda_{Q,R^{\kappa}}
into
\Lambda_{n}
in such a way that
\Lambda_{Q^{\prime},n}
is taller than
\Lambda_{Q,R^{\kappa}}
but less wide. Furthermore, let
S^{1}_{Q,n}=\inf\{d_{n}(x_{n},y_{n})\colon x\in\partial_{L}\Lambda_{Q,n},\,y\in\partial_{R}\Lambda_{Q,n}\}
, and define
S^{1}_{Q}
analogously. Since
\mathbf{P}[\partial_{L}\Lambda_{Q,n}\leftrightarrow\partial_{R}\Lambda_{Q,n}]=\frac{1}{2}
, the
1/4
-quantiles of
S^{1}_{Q,n}
and
S^{1}_{Q^{\prime},n}
are finite.

Then we have the stochastic domination
S^{1}_{Q^{\prime},n}\leq S^{1}_{Q,R^{\kappa}}
for all
n
.
By Theorem
1.1
and Lemma
2.7
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{-1}\operatorname{quant}_{1/4}(S^{1}_{Q,n})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{quant}_{1/4}(S^{1}_{Q}),

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{-1}\operatorname{quant}_{1/4}(S^{1}_{Q^{\prime},n})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{quant}_{1/4}(S^{1}_{Q^{\prime}}).

Thus,

\displaystyle\operatorname{quant}_{1/4}(S^{1}_{Q})=

\displaystyle\,\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathfrak{q}}_{R^{\kappa}}^{-1}\operatorname{quant}_{1/4}(S^{1}_{Q,R^{\kappa}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}}{{\mathfrak{q}}_{R^{\kappa}}}{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{-1}\operatorname{quant}_{1/4}(S^{1}_{Q,R^{\kappa}})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\,\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}}{{\mathfrak{q}}_{R^{\kappa}}}{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}^{-1}\operatorname{quant}_{1/4}(S^{1}_{Q^{\prime},n})=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\!\left(\frac{{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}}{{\mathfrak{q}}_{R^{\kappa}}}\right)\operatorname{quant}_{1/4}(S^{1}_{Q^{\prime}}).

So there exists a constant
C=C(R)
such that for all
n
,
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}\leq C{\mathfrak{q}}_{R^{\kappa}}
. Similarly, in Lemma
5.7
, it was shown that there exists a constant
c=c(R)
such that for all
n
,
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}\geq c{\mathfrak{q}}_{R^{\kappa-1}}
. Let
\kappa_{0}
be such that
R^{\kappa_{0}-1}<n_{0}\leq R^{\kappa_{0}}
It follows from (
10.1
) and the above that

{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}\geq c{\mathfrak{q}}_{R^{\kappa-1}}\geq c{\mathfrak{q}}_{n_{0}R^{\kappa-1-\kappa_{0}}}\geq cR^{(\kappa-1-\kappa_{0})(\beta-\varepsilon)}\geq n^{\beta-\varepsilon}

for
n
sufficiently large, where the value of the constant
c
may change from line to line. Similarly,

{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}\leq C{\mathfrak{q}}_{R^{\kappa}}\leq C{\mathfrak{q}}_{n_{0}R^{\kappa-k_{0}}}\leq CR^{\kappa-\kappa_{0}(\beta+\varepsilon)}\leq n^{\beta+\varepsilon}

for
n
sufficiently large, where the value of the constant
C
may change from line to line. We conclude that
{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}=n^{\beta+o(1)}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

To transfer the result to the IIC, we use the following result proved in
[
51
,
39
]
. We denote by
\mathcal{A}_{1}(r,R)
the event that there exists an open crossing of the annulus
B(0,R)\setminus B(0,r)
. Recall from
[
60
]
that
A_{1}(r,R)=\mathbf{P}[\mathcal{A}_{1}(r,R)]=(r/R)^{5/48+o(1)}
.

Lemma 10.1

(
[
39
, Proposition 5.2]
)
.

There exists
c>0
such that the following holds. For each
10<r<R/100
, total variation between the restriction to
B(0,r)
of
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{IIC}}
and
\mathbf{P}[\cdot\mid\mathcal{A}_{1}(1,R)]
is at most
(r/R)^{c}
.

Proof of Corollary
1.3
.

We first show the upper bound. Let
\varepsilon>0
, and let
\alpha=\alpha(\varepsilon)>1
be large. By applying Lemma
10.1
with
r=n
and
R=n^{\alpha}
, and Proposition
7.1
on the box
B(0,2n)
with exponent
\alpha^{2}
, we have

(10.2)

\begin{split}\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{IIC}}[S_{n}>n^{\varepsilon}{\mathfrak{q}}_{2n}]&\leq n^{-c(\alpha-1)}+\frac{\mathbf{P}[S_{n}>n^{\varepsilon}{\mathfrak{q}}_{2n},\,\mathcal{A}_{1}(1,n^{\alpha})]}{\mathbf{P}[\mathcal{A}_{1}(1,n^{\alpha})]}\\
&\lesssim n^{-c(\alpha-1)}+n^{-\alpha^{2}\varepsilon}n^{(5/48+\varepsilon)\alpha}\end{split}

for some absolute
c>0
. Since
{\mathfrak{q}}_{2n}\leq n^{\beta+o(1)}
by Corollary
1.2
, this shows that
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{IIC}}
-almost surely we have

S_{n}\leq n^{\beta+o(1)}\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

We turn to the lower bound. Let
\alpha>1
be arbitrary and let
M=M(\varepsilon,\alpha)\in\mathbf{N}
. For
k\in\mathbf{N}
let
A_{M,k}
be the event that there are at most
M
disjoint crossings of the annulus
A(0;2^{k-1},2^{k})
. By RSW and the BK inequality we have
\mathbf{P}[(A_{M,k})^{c}]\leq p_{1}^{M}
for some
p_{1}\in(0,1)
. On the event
A_{M,k-2}\cap A_{M,k}
, let
u_{1},\ldots,u_{M}
(resp.
v_{1},\ldots,v_{M}
) be a collection of points in
A(0;2^{k-3},2^{k-2})
(resp.
A(0;2^{k-1},2^{k})
) that separate its inner from its outer boundary in the percolation. Let

Z_{k}=\min_{i,j}D_{A(0;2^{k-3},2^{k})}(u_{i},v_{j})\,\mathbf{1}_{A_{M,k-2}\cap A_{M,k}}

where
D_{A}
denotes the unnormalized geodesic or resistance metric on percolation on
\mathbf{T}\cap A
. We then have
S_{2^{k}}\geq M^{-2}Z_{k}
by the parallel law on the event
\mathcal{A}_{1}(1,2^{k})
.

By Theorem
1.1
and Lemma
2.7
, the laws of
{\mathfrak{q}}_{2^{k}}^{-1}Z_{k}
converge to their CLE
6
analogue, and since the CLE
6
metric is a.s. positive, there exists for each
p_{2}=p_{2}(\varepsilon,\alpha)>0
some
c_{1}=c_{1}(\varepsilon,\alpha,p_{2})>0
such that
\limsup_{k\to\infty}\mathbf{P}[Z_{k}\leq c_{1}{\mathfrak{q}}_{2^{k}}\mid A_{M,k-2}\cap A_{M,k}]\leq p_{2}
. Therefore

\mathbf{P}[Z_{k}\leq c_{1}{\mathfrak{q}}_{2^{k}}]\leq p_{1}^{M}+p_{2}=:p_{0},

and by independence

\mathbf{P}[S_{2^{k+\varepsilon k}}\leq M^{-2}c_{1}{\mathfrak{q}}_{2^{k}},\,\mathcal{A}_{1}(1,2^{k+\varepsilon k})]\leq p_{0}^{\varepsilon k}\quad\text{for $k$ sufficiently large.}

The same argument as in (
10.2
) then implies that for all
\alpha>1
,

\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{IIC}}[S_{2^{k+\varepsilon k}}\leq M^{-2}c_{1}{\mathfrak{q}}_{2^{k}}]\leq 2^{-c(1-\varepsilon)\alpha k}+2^{(5/48+\varepsilon)\alpha k}p_{0}^{\varepsilon k}\quad\text{for $k$ sufficiently large.}

We can choose
p_{0}=p_{0}(\varepsilon,\alpha)
sufficiently small by choosing
M(\varepsilon,\alpha)
sufficiently large and
p_{2}(\varepsilon,\alpha)
sufficiently small, thus obtaining for all
\alpha>1
,

\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{IIC}}[S_{2^{k+\varepsilon k}}\leq M^{-2}c_{1}{\mathfrak{q}}_{2^{k}}]\leq 2^{-c\alpha k}\quad\text{for $k$ sufficiently large.}

Let
k
be the largest integer such that
2^{k+\varepsilon k}\leq n
. Then
S_{n}\geq S_{2^{k+\varepsilon k}}
, and so for all
\varepsilon^{\prime}
small enough and
n
large enough depending on
\varepsilon^{\prime}
, we have

\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{IIC}}[S_{n}\leq n^{-\varepsilon^{\prime}}{\mathfrak{q}}_{n}]\leq\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{IIC}}[S_{2^{k+\varepsilon k}}\leq M^{-2}c_{1}{\mathfrak{q}}_{2^{k}}]\lesssim n^{-c\alpha},

as required.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.4
.

Let
\mu
be the vertex counting measure of the percolation cluster containing the origin (resp. the IIC). We first show that
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{IIC}}
-a.s. we have
\mu(B(0,n))=n^{91/48+o(1)}
as
n\to\infty
.

The lower bound for
\mu(B(0,n))
is similar to the lower bound for the metric in the proof of Corollary
1.3
above. Let
\varepsilon>0
, let
\alpha>1
be arbitrary and let
M=M(\varepsilon,\alpha)\in\mathbf{N}
. Let
A_{M,k}
be the event that there are
m\leq M
disjoint open clusters
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}^{k}_{m}
crossing the annulus
A(0;2^{k-1},2^{k})
. Let

Z_{k}=\min_{i}|\mathcal{C}^{k}_{i}|\,\mathbf{1}_{A_{M,k}}.

Then on the event
\mathcal{A}_{1}(1,2^{k})
, we have
\mu(B(0,2^{k}))\geq Z_{k}
. By convergence of the (renormalized) percolation measure to the
\CLE_{6}
gasket measure,
2^{-2k}A(1,2^{k})^{-1}Z_{k}
converges to the analogous quantity for
\CLE_{6}
. Thus, for any
p_{0}>0
there is
c_{1}>0
such that

\mathbf{P}[Z_{k}\leq c_{1}2^{2k}A(1,2^{k})]\leq p_{0}

for
k
sufficiently large. By independence, we have

\mathbf{P}[\mu(B(0,2^{k+\varepsilon k}))\leq c_{1}2^{2k}A(1,2^{k}),\,\mathcal{A}_{1}(1,2^{k+\varepsilon k})]\leq p_{0}^{\varepsilon k}\quad\text{for $k$ sufficiently large.}

Now in exactly the same way as in the proof of Corollary
1.3
, using the fact that
A(1,n)=n^{-5/48+o(1)}
, we obtain that for all
\nu>0
and
\alpha>1
,

\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{IIC}}[\mu(B(0,n))\leq n^{91/48-\nu}]=O(n^{-\alpha}).

For the upper bound, note that on the event
\mathcal{A}_{1}(1,n)
, every site in the cluster containing the origin has an arm going Euclidean distance at least
n/2
. Thus, Lemma
A.4
immediately implies that

\mathbf{P}[\mu(B(0,n))\geq n^{91/48+\nu},\,\mathcal{A}_{1}(1,n)]=O(n^{-\alpha})

as
n\to\infty
for any
\alpha>1
. Again, it follows that

\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{IIC}}[\mu(B(0,n))\geq n^{91/48+\nu}]=O(n^{-\alpha})

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, which concludes the proof that
\mu(B(0,n))=n^{91/48+o(1)}
holds
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{IIC}}
-a.s.

The proof of Corollary
1.4
then follows from
[
56
, Propositions 3.1 and 3.2]
, applied with
v(n)=n^{91/48}
,
r(n)=n^{\beta}
,
\mathcal{I}(n)=n^{1/(91/48+\beta)}
,
\lambda=\lambda(n)=n^{o(1)}
,
m=m(n)=n^{o(1)}
, and
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(n)=n^{-o(1)}
.
∎

Appendix A
Arm calculations

We prove some arm bounds that are needed in Sections
6
and
10
.

A.1.
A four-arm bound

We prove the following lemma, which is used in Section
6.1
. Let
B^{(k)}
be a box of side length
\frac{1}{n}R^{\kappa-k}\asymp R^{-k}
, and consider the percolation restricted to the sites in
B^{(k)}
.

Lemma A.1
.

For all
\zeta>0
, there exists
R=R(\zeta)
large enough and a constant
C=C(R,\zeta)
such that for all
n,k
,
x\in B^{(k)}
, and
a>0
such that
R^{\kappa-k}\geq an\geq R
, we have

(A.1)

\mathbf{P}\!\left(\textrm{an arm from $B(x,a)$ to each of the $4$ sides of $B^{(k)}$}\right)\leq C\cdot\!\left(\frac{a}{R^{-k}}\right)^{\frac{5}{4}-\zeta}.

The event in (
A.1
) denotes the event that there exist four disjoint arms
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{4}
inside
B^{(k)}\setminus B(x,a)
such that
\eta_{i}
connects
B(x,a)
to the
i
th side of the box
B^{(k)}
, where the sides of
B^{(k)}
are numbered clockwise starting from the top. Note that we do not require that
B(x,a)\subset B^{(k)}
. The bound given on the right-hand side of (
A.1
) is just the
4
-arm probability from radius
a
to
R^{-k}
, so the result is immediate when
B(x,a)
is away from the boundary of
B^{(k)}
. The proof explains that the same (and in fact a stronger) bound is valid when
B(x,a)
is near the boundary of
B^{(k)}
.

Proof of Lemma
A.1
.

Recall that we are working in a coordinate system determined by the triangular lattice. In this proof, when we consider distances of points from various lines parallel to the coordinate axes, we refer to the 1-Minkowski distance with respect to this coordinate system and not their Euclidean distance.

Let
d_{1}>0
be the distance from
x
to the closest side of
B^{(k)}
and let
x_{1}
be the lattice-parallel projection of
x
onto this side. Similarly, let
d_{2}
be the distance from
x
to the second closest side and let
x_{2}
be the vertex of
B^{(k)}
connecting the two sides of
B^{(k)}
closest to
x
. Note that if
B(x,a)
has an arm to each side of
B^{(k)}
, then
B(x,a)
has 4 arms to
B(x,d_{1})
,
B(x_{1},2d_{1})\cap B^{(k)}
has 3 arms in the half-plane to
B(x_{1},d_{2})\cap B^{(k)}
, and
B(x_{2},2d_{2})\cap B^{(k)}
has 2 arms in the wedge domain of angle
\frac{\pi}{3}
or
\frac{2\pi}{3}
to
B(x_{2},R^{-k})\cap B^{(k)}
. We can now conclude the proof by using arm estimates like in Lemma
5.10
. That is, given
\zeta>0
, there exists
R=R(\zeta)
large enough so that all the necessary arm exponent estimates apply. Thus, we find that for all
n,k,x,a
such that
R^{\kappa-k}\geq an\geq R
we have

\begin{split}\mathbf{P}(\textrm{an arm from $B(x,a)$ to each of the $4$ sides of $B^{(k)}$})&\leq\!\left(\frac{a}{d_{1}}\right)^{\frac{5}{4}-\zeta}\!\left(\frac{2d_{1}}{d_{2}}\right)^{2-\zeta}\!\left(\frac{2d_{2}}{n^{-1}R^{\kappa-k}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}-\zeta}\\
&\lesssim\!\left(\frac{a}{R^{-k}}\right)^{\frac{5}{4}-\zeta}\cdot\!\left(\frac{d_{1}}{d_{2}}\right)^{3/4}\cdot\!\left(\frac{d_{2}}{R^{-k}}\right)^{1/4}\\
&\leq\!\left(\frac{a}{R^{-k}}\right)^{\frac{5}{4}-\zeta},\end{split}

as required.
∎

A.2.
Six semi-arm concentration

We prove the following result, which is used in Section
6.2.1
. Recall the definitions of the events
H_{j}
and
H
from Section
6.2.1
.

Lemma A.2
.

The following holds for all
R
large enough. For any
\alpha>0

and
\nu
as in the definition of events
H_{j}
, there exists
C=C(\alpha,R,\nu)
such that for all
k,n
and
k\leq j\leq\kappa
, we have that

\mathbf{P}(H_{j}^{c})\leq C\cdot R^{-4\alpha j}.

In particular, we have
\mathbf{P}\!\left(H^{c}\right)\leq C^{\prime}\cdot R^{-4\alpha k}
, for a constant
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(\alpha,R,\nu)
.

In order to prove this lemma, we want to bound the probability that
m
different boxes of level
j
have six semiarms of length
i-j-4
. To achieve this, we want to decorrelate the six semiarm events for different boxes. To do so, we use the following lemma, whose proof we omit since it is analogous to
[
49
, Lemma A.2]
. The lemma states that around each box, we can find a sequence of annuli that are all pairwise disjoint and such that the overall ratio between the inner and outer boundary distances is upper bounded by a quantity involving the distance between these boxes. Then we upper bound the six semiarm events for a set of
m
boxes with the probability that each annulus in the sequence has 6 arms crossing it. If the distance between the boxes is large, this probability will be small by Lemma
2.2
.

Lemma A.3
.

Fix
m\in\mathbf{N}
, and let
i,j\in\mathbf{N}
with
i\leq j-10
. Let
B(0,R^{-i}/2)
be the box centered at the origin with side length
R^{-i}
, and tile it with boxes of side length
n^{-1}R^{\kappa-j}
. Let
K
be the set of centers of such boxes that are fully contained inside
B(0,R^{-i}/2)
. Suppose that
z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}\in K
are distinct. Then there exist
n_{u}\in\{1,\ldots,m\}

for each
1\leq u\leq m
and
s_{u,l},r_{u,l}\in\mathbf{R}_{\geq 0}
such that
0<s_{u,l}<r_{u,l}\leq n^{-1}R^{\kappa-i}
for
1\leq l\leq n_{u}-1
and
0=s_{u,n_{u}}<\frac{1}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-j}\leq r_{u,n_{u}}
so that all the annuli
A(z_{u};s_{u,l},r_{u,l})
are pairwise disjoint and

\prod_{u=1}^{m}\frac{\prod_{l=1}^{n_{u}-1}s_{u,l}}{\prod_{l=1}^{n_{u}}r_{u,l}}\leq C(R,m)\prod_{u=1}^{m}\frac{1}{R^{-i}\wedge\min_{1\leq v<u}|z_{u}-z_{v}|},

where
C(R,m)
is a constant depending only on
R,m
. We use the convention that
\prod_{l=1}^{n_{u}-1}s_{u,l}=1
if
n_{u}=1
, and the minimum of an empty set is infinite.

Proof of Lemma
A.2
.

Fix
k\leq j\leq\kappa
. Using the union bound, we can bound the probability
\mathbf{P}(H_{j}^{c})
from above by

(A.2)

\begin{split}\mathbf{P}\!\left(H_{j}^{c}\right)&\leq P\!\left(N_{j}^{h}\geq R^{j\nu}\right)+\sum_{i=k-2}^{j-10}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B^{(i)}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{P}\!\left(N_{j,B^{(i)}}\geq R^{j\nu}\right)\\
&\leq P\!\left(N_{j}^{h}\geq R^{j\nu}\right)+\sum_{i=k-2}^{j-10}R^{2i-2k+6}\cdot\mathbf{P}(N_{j,B^{(i)}}\geq R^{j\nu}),\end{split}

where the sum in the first line is over
R^{2i-2k+6}
boxes
B^{(i)}
that have side length
R^{-i}
and are centered at centers of boxes in
\mathcal{B}^{R,k}_{i+3,n}
. In the second line, we are regarding
B^{(i)}
as an abstract box of side length
R^{-i}
rather than a subbox of
B^{(k)}
. This quantity provides an upper bound on
\mathbf{P}(N_{j,B^{(i)}}\geq R^{i\nu})
for all boxes
B^{(i)}
in the above sum, even if these boxes are not fully contained inside
B^{(k)}
– sites of
B^{(i)}\setminus B^{(k)}
can only make
N_{j,B^{(i)}}
larger.

We start by examining the behavior of
N_{j,B^{(i)}}
. Fix any
k-2\leq i\leq j-10
. Fix a box
B^{(i)}=B(0,R^{-i}/2)
of side length
R^{-i}
as described above. For brevity of notation, in the remaining section of the proof we will write
N_{j}
instead of
N_{j,B^{(i)}}
. We will find a bound on the
m
’th moment of
N_{j}
for all
m\in\mathbf{N}
, and then use
m
large enough to obtain the desired upper bound on (
A.2
). To do so, we will need to analyze the probability that we have the
6
-semiarm event for any
m
fixed level
j
boxes. Given any
m
such boxes in
B^{(i)}
, Lemma
A.3
gives us a way to find a collection of pairwise disjoint annuli centered around these boxes. Suppose
z_{1},\ldots z_{m}
are centers of these boxes, and denote the boxes by
B_{u}=B\!\left(z_{u},\frac{1}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-j}\right)
for
u=1,\ldots,m
. Moreover, let
\tilde{l}_{u}
denote the level of the gap in the semiarms of box
B_{u}
. Let
s_{u,l},r_{u,l}
be as in Lemma
A.3
. Then if there are six semiarms in the
u
’th box, each of the annuli

A(z_{u};s_{u,l},r_{u,l})\cap\!\left(A(z_{u};\frac{1}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-j+3},\frac{1}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-j+\tilde{l}_{u}})\cup A(z_{u};\frac{1}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-j+\tilde{l}_{u}+3},\frac{1}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-i-4})\right)

for
1\leq u\leq m
and
1\leq l\leq n_{u}

has six alternating arms. To account for the fact that our semiarms have gaps, we have intersected our annuli
A(z_{u};s_{u,l},r_{u,l})
above by the set on which we know we have an alternating
6
arm event.

Furthermore, all of these annuli are pairwise disjoint. So we can upper bound the event that we have
6
semiarms of length
j-i-4
around each of the
m
boxes by the
independent
events that tell us that there are 6 arms of alternating colors in each of the annuli above.

Recall from Lemma
2.2
that for any
\zeta>0
we can choose
R=R(\zeta)
large enough so that for any
20
20

20

This bound ensures that the inner graph radius of the annulus
A(z_{u};s^{\prime},r^{\prime})
is at least
R
.

n^{-1}\cdot R\leq s^{\prime}\leq r^{\prime}
the 6-arm probability for any annulus
A(z_{u};s^{\prime},r^{\prime})
is bounded from above by
(\frac{s^{\prime}}{r^{\prime}})^{35/12-\zeta}
. Note that the smallest annulus radius relevant for us is
n^{-1}R^{\kappa-j+3}/2\geq n^{-1}R
for all
j\leq\kappa
, thus this lemma applies. Finally, note that the inverse of the product of the aspect ratios of the new annuli centered at
z_{u}
can be bounded from above by

2R^{4}\cdot R^{3}\cdot\frac{\frac{1}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-j+3}}{r_{u,n_{u}}}\prod_{l=1}^{n_{u}-1}\frac{s_{u,l}}{r_{u,l}}.

To see this, note that the
\frac{1}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-j+3}
term is precisely the smallest radius of our annuli, the
R^{3}
term comes from the gap in our arms that has aspect ratio
R^{3}
and the
2R^{4}
term stems from the fact that our arms have length at most
\frac{1}{2}n^{-1}R^{\kappa-i-4}
, while
r_{u,1}
is chosen to be smaller than
n^{-1}R^{\kappa-i}
.

Combining the above observations, we have

\begin{split}&\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{u}\textrm{ has 6 semiarms of length }j-i-4\textrm{ for each }1\leq u\leq m\right)\\
&\leq C(m,R)\sum_{0\leq\tilde{l}_{1}<j-i}\cdots\sum_{0\leq\tilde{l}_{m}<j-i}\!\left(\prod_{u=1}^{m}R^{11}\frac{R^{-j}}{r_{u,n_{u}}}\prod_{l=1}^{n_{u}-1}\frac{s_{u,l}}{r_{u,l}}\right)^{35/12-\zeta},\end{split}

where
C(m,R)
denotes a constant depending only on
m,R
and can increase from line to line,
35/12
is the 6-arm exponent and
\zeta>0
is a constant that will be chosen later. Now by Lemma
A.3
, we have

\begin{split}&\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{u}\textrm{ has 6 semiarms of length }j-i-4\textrm{ for each }1\leq u\leq m\right)\\
&\leq C(m,R)\sum_{0\leq\tilde{l}_{1}<j-i}\cdots\sum_{0\leq\tilde{l}_{m}<j-i}\!\left(\prod_{u=1}^{m}\frac{R^{11}\cdot R^{-j}}{R^{-i}\wedge\min_{v<u}|z_{v}-z_{u}|}\right)^{35/12-\zeta}\\
&\leq C(m,R)(j-i)^{m}\!\left(\prod_{u=1}^{m}\frac{R^{11}\cdot R^{-j}}{R^{-i}\wedge\min_{1\leq v<u}|z_{v}-z_{u}|}\right)^{35/12-\zeta}\\
&\leq C(m,R)(j-i)^{m}\cdot R^{-jm(35/12-\zeta)}\!\left(\prod_{u=1}^{m}\frac{1}{R^{-i}\wedge\min_{1\leq v<u}|z_{v}-z_{u}|}\right)^{35/12-\zeta}.\end{split}

Let
\beta=35/12-\zeta
. We can use the above to bound
\mathbf{E}[N_{j}^{m}]
as follows:

\begin{split}\mathbf{E}[N_{j}^{m}]&=\mathbf{E}\!\left[\!\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq B^{(i)}\\
B\textrm{ has level }j\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{\!\left\{B\textrm{ has 6 semiarms of length }j-i-4\right\}}\right)^{m}\right]\\
&=\sum_{1\leq\tilde{m}\leq m}c_{\tilde{m}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B_{1},\ldots,B_{\tilde{m}}\\
\textrm{ distinct}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{P}\!\left(B_{1},\ldots,B_{{\tilde{m}}}\textrm{ all have 6 semiarms of length }j-i-4\right)\\
&\leq\sum_{1\leq\tilde{m}\leq m}c_{\tilde{m}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B_{1},\ldots,B_{\tilde{m}}\\
\textrm{ distinct}\end{subarray}}C(\tilde{m},R)(j-i)^{\tilde{m}}R^{-j\tilde{m}\beta}\!\left(\prod_{u=1}^{\tilde{m}}\frac{1}{R^{-i}\wedge\min_{1\leq v<u}|z_{v}-z_{u}|}\right)^{\beta}.\end{split}

The constants
c_{\tilde{m}}
in the above come from the multinomial expansion. Noting that
c_{\tilde{m}}
and
C(\tilde{m},R)
are bounded by some constant depending only on
m,R
and splitting the sum over
\tilde{m}
boxes into
\tilde{m}
separate sums, we can further bound
\mathbf{E}[N_{j}^{m}]
as

\begin{split}\mathbf{E}[N_{j}^{m}]&\leq C(m,R)\sum_{1\leq\tilde{m}\leq m}(j-i)^{\tilde{m}}R^{-j\tilde{m}\beta}\frac{1}{\tilde{m}!}\sum_{B_{1}}\bigg[\!\left(\frac{1}{R^{-i}}\right)^{\beta}\\
&\cdot\sum_{B_{2}}\!\left(\frac{1}{|z_{1}-z_{2}|}\right)^{\beta}\cdots\sum_{B_{\tilde{m}}}\!\left(\frac{1}{\min_{v<\tilde{m}}\{|z_{\tilde{m}}-z_{v}|\}}\right)^{\beta}\bigg]\\
&\leq C(m,R)\sum_{1\leq\tilde{m}\leq m}(j-i)^{\tilde{m}}R^{-j\tilde{m}\beta}\frac{1}{\tilde{m}!}\sum_{B_{1}}\bigg[R^{i\beta}\sum_{a_{2}=i}^{j}\sum_{B_{2}:|z_{1}-z_{2}|\leq R^{-a_{2}}}R^{a_{2}\beta}\\
&\cdot\cdots\sum_{a_{\tilde{m}}=i}^{j}\sum_{B_{\tilde{m}}:\min_{v<\tilde{m}}|z_{\tilde{m}}-z_{v}|\leq R^{-a_{\tilde{m}}}}R^{a_{\tilde{m}}\beta}\bigg].\end{split}

Now note that the number of boxes
B_{u}
such that
\min_{v<u}|z_{u}-z_{v}|\leq R^{-a_{u}}
is at most
uR^{2(j-a_{u})+2}
, since such a level
j
box must intersect at least one of the
u
boxes centered around
z_{0},\ldots z_{u-1}
of radius
R^{-a_{u}}
. This implies that

\begin{split}\mathbf{E}[N_{j}^{m}]&\leq C(m,R)\sum_{1\leq\tilde{m}\leq m}(j-i)^{\tilde{m}}R^{-j\tilde{m}\beta}\frac{1}{\tilde{m}!}\sum_{B_{1}}R^{i\beta}\sum_{a_{2}=i}^{j}2R^{2(j-a_{2})}R^{a_{2}\beta}\cdots\sum_{a_{\tilde{m}}=i}^{j}\tilde{m}R^{2(j-a_{\tilde{m}})}R^{a_{\tilde{m}}\beta}\\
&\leq C(m,R)\sum_{1\leq\tilde{m}\leq m}(j-i)^{\tilde{m}}R^{-j\tilde{m}\beta}R^{2(j-i)+i\beta}((j-i)R^{2j+j(\beta-2)})^{\tilde{m}-1}\\
&\leq C(m,R)(j-i)^{2m}R^{-(\beta-2)(j-i)}\leq C(m,R)R^{-(11/12-2\zeta)(j-i)},\end{split}

In the above, the constant
C(m,R)
increases from line to line, but only depends on
m
and
R
throughout.

We can analogously obtain that for all
m\geq 1
there exists a constant
C(m,R)
such that

\mathbf{E}\!\left[(N_{j}^{h})^{m}\right]\leq C(m,R)\cdot R^{-(1-2\zeta)(j-k)}.

Indeed, we can redo the above calculation with
k
in place of
i
and
\beta=2-\zeta
in place of
\beta=35/12-\zeta
. This corresponds to the
3
-arm exponent in the half-plane (see Lemma
2.2
). To complete the calculation, one just needs to note that now the number of boxes
B_{u}
that are suitably close to the boundary and such that
\min_{v<u}|z_{u}-z_{v}|\leq R^{-a_{u}}
is at most
4R^{9}\cdot uR^{j-a_{u}+1}
.

Finally, going back to equation (
A.2
) and applying Markov’s inequality, we get that for any
m\in\mathbf{N}

\begin{split}\mathbf{P}(H_{j}^{c})&\leq\frac{\mathbf{E}\!\left[\!\left(N_{j}^{h}\right)^{m}\right]}{R^{mj\nu}}+\sum_{i=k-2}^{j-10}R^{2i-2k+6}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{E}\!\left[N_{j,B^{(i)}}^{m}\right]}{R^{mj\nu}}\\
&\leq C(m,R,\nu)\cdot\bigg[\frac{R^{-(1-2\nu)(j-k)}}{R^{mj\nu}}+\sum_{i=k-2}^{j-10}\frac{R^{2i-2k}}{R^{mj\nu}}R^{-(11/12-2\zeta)(j-i)}\bigg]\\
&\leq C(m,R,\nu)\cdot jR^{(2-m\nu)j},\end{split}

where we have chosen
\zeta=1/6
for example to make our bounds valid. Setting
m=m(\alpha,\nu)=\lceil\frac{4+4\alpha}{\nu}\rceil+1
in the above yields

\mathbf{P}(H_{j}^{c})\leq C(\alpha,\nu,R)\cdot R^{-4\alpha j}.

Summing this formula over all
j\geq k
, we find that
\mathbf{P}(H^{c})\leq C(\alpha,R,\nu)\cdot R^{-4\alpha k}
for a possibly larger choice of the constant
C(\alpha,R,\nu)
, as wanted.
∎

A.3.
One-arm concentration

We prove the following lemma which we need for the volume upper bound in the proof of Corollary
1.4
. The lemma is a one-arm analogue of Lemma
A.2
for the case
j=\kappa
.

Lemma A.4
.

Consider critical percolation on
\Lambda_{n}
. Let
N_{n}
be the number of open sites
z\in\Lambda_{n}
such that
z
has an open arm that goes Euclidean distance at least
1/2
. Then for any
\alpha>0
and
\nu>0
, there exists
C=C(\alpha,\nu)
such that for all
n
, we have

(A.3)

\mathbf{P}\left(N_{n}\geq n^{\frac{91}{48}+\nu}\right)\leq Cn^{-\alpha}.

The proof of Lemma
A.4
is an easier version of Lemma
A.2
.

Proof of Lemma
A.4
.

Like before, we need to show that
n^{-91/48-\nu}N_{n}
has bounded moments. Let
m\in\mathbf{N}
and let
z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}\in\Lambda_{n}
be distinct such that each
z_{u}
has an open arm that goes Euclidean distance at least
1/2
. We apply Lemma
A.3
with
i=0
and
j=\kappa
. Let
s_{u,l},r_{u,l}
be as in Lemma
A.3
. Note that the
r_{u,l}
can be chosen such that
s_{u,l}<r_{u,l}\leq 1/4
. Then each annulus
A(z_{u};s_{u,l},r_{u,l})
has an open arm crossing it and the annuli are all disjoint. Therefore, by independence and because the 1-arm exponent is
5/48
(recall
[
60
]
), for each
\zeta>0
, there exists
C(\zeta,m)
such that for all
n
,

\begin{split}{}&\mathbf{P}\!\left(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m}\text{ each have an open arm that goes Euclidean distance at least $\frac{1}{2}$}\right)\\
\leq{}&C(\zeta,m)\left(\prod_{u=1}^{m}\frac{n^{-1}}{r_{u,n_{u}}}\cdot\frac{\prod^{n_{u}-1}_{l=1}s_{u,l}}{\prod_{l=1}^{n_{u}-1}r_{u,l}}\right)^{\frac{5}{48}-\zeta}\\
\leq{}&C(\zeta,m)\left(\prod_{u=1}^{m}\frac{n^{-1}}{\min_{1\leq v<u}|z_{u}-z_{v}|}\right)^{\frac{5}{48}-\zeta},\end{split}

where the value of
C(\zeta,m)
may change from line to line. The term
\frac{n^{-1}}{r_{u,n_{u}}}
corresponds to the one-arm bound for the inner annulus of radii
s_{u,n_{0}}=0
and
r_{u,n_{u}}
. The final inequality is due to Lemma
A.3
. It follows that for all
n
,

\begin{split}&\mathbf{E}[N_{n}^{m}]\\
\leq{}&\sum_{1\leq\widetilde{m}\leq m}c_{\widetilde{m}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z_{1},\ldots,z_{\widetilde{m}}\\
\text{distinct}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{P}\!\left(z_{1},\ldots,z_{\widetilde{m}}\text{ each have an open arm that goes Euclidean distance at least $\frac{1}{2}$}\right)\\
\leq{}&\sum_{1\leq\widetilde{m}\leq m}c_{\widetilde{m}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z_{1},\ldots,z_{\widetilde{m}}\\
\text{distinct}\end{subarray}}C(\zeta,\widetilde{m})\left(\prod_{u=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{n^{-1}}{\min_{1\leq v<u}|z_{u}-z_{v}|}\right)^{\frac{5}{48}-\zeta}\\
\leq{}&\sum_{1\leq\widetilde{m}\leq m}C(\zeta,\widetilde{m})n^{(2-\frac{5}{48}+\zeta)\widetilde{m}}\\
\leq{}&C(\zeta,m)n^{(\frac{91}{48}+\zeta)m},\end{split}

where
c_{\widetilde{m}}
is as in the proof of Lemma
A.2
and the values of the constants
C(\zeta,m),C(\zeta,\widetilde{m})
may change from line to line. The third inequality is a standard summation inequality and follows as in the proof of Lemma
A.2
.

Now by Markov’s inequality,

\begin{split}\mathbf{P}\!\left(N_{n}\geq n^{\frac{91}{48}+\nu}\right)\leq{}&C(\zeta,m)n^{-(\frac{91}{48}+\nu)m+(\frac{91}{48}+\zeta)m}=C(\zeta,m)n^{(\zeta-\nu)m}.\end{split}

The desired result follows from choosing
\zeta=\nu/2
and
m=\lceil 2\alpha/\nu\rceil
.
∎
